Transport-Reaction Models


Abstract




\KOMAoptions

twoside=false

At first, I would like to express my gratitude to my advisors Dr. Christina Lienstromberg and Prof. Dr. Juan J. L. Velázquez. They always took time to provide guidance and feedback, which led to many fruitful discussions.

I would also like to thank my parents and my sister. The intense work on my thesis during the last months would not have been possible without their continuous support.

Chapter 1
Introduction

Transport-reaction equations arise in the mathematical modeling of the movement of populations. In contrast to parabolic equations, hyperbolic models realistically account for a finite propagation speed of organisms. Therefore, scientists increasingly focus on hyperbolic and kinetic models
[
7
, p. 36]
.

A particularly interesting question is whether mathematical models are able to describe the variety of patterns observed in nature: rippling waves in myxobacteria swarms, milling schools of fish, swarms of insects or pedestrian traffic jams, to name only a few of them
[
7
]
. Diffusive-generated patterns have originally been studied by Turing in his pioneer work
[
38
]
and his idea has been applied many times, for instance in the context of morphogenesis
[
13
]
. Patterns generated by transport on the other hand are less well understood.

The aim of this master’s thesis is to study the linear transport-reaction system

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})=B\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})

(1)

on the
d
-dimensional hypercube
[0,1]^{d}
with periodic boundary conditions, i.e. on the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}.
Concerning notation and interpretation, the parameter
N
is the number of population subgroups and
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are the transport directions of each component. The matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
describes linear interactions of the subgroups.

Typically, (
1
) appears as a linearization of the non-linear partial differential equation

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})=F(u(t,\mathbf{x})),

(2)

where
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{N})
and
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a sufficiently smooth function. There is a large amount of research which deals with the question of stability of a given equilibrium point of (
2
); see for instance
[
18
,
29
,
31
]
. Many times, however, only the one dimensional case
d=1
is studied.

The transport-reaction equation (
1
) can also be interpreted as a velocity discretization of

\partial_{t}u(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}u(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})=-\sigma(\mathbf{v})+\int_{V}\kappa(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})u(t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})\,d\mathbf{v}^{\prime},

(3)

where the velocity
\mathbf{v}
can take values in a set
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The function
\sigma\colon V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
describes collisions causing absorption
[
4
]
and corresponds to the diagonal entries of
B
. The so called scattering kernel
\kappa\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
governs the transition of particles with incoming velocity
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
to particles with outgoing velocity
\mathbf{v}

[
4
]
. It corresponds to the off-diagonal entries of
B.
The linear Boltzmann-type equation (
3
) is, for example, connected to velocity-jump processes and neutron transport, see
[
19
,
7
,
4
]
.

Throughout the whole thesis, we try to be as general as possible in the sense that we consider the transport-reaction equation (
1
) on the spaces
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for all
1\leq p<\infty
and all dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
simultaneously. Whenever we need to restrict ourselves to
p=2
or
d=1,
we try to at least indicate how future research can approach the other cases.

The thesis is structured as follows:
Chapter

2
and
Chapter

3
deal with well-posedness of (
1
) and basic properties such as positivity and mass conservation. We use a semigroup approach and show that

-A_{p}+B_{p}=-\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}+B\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}\end{pmatrix}

with an appropriately chosen domain generates a semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
which we call the transport-reaction semigroup
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}.
We continue our study with a detailed spectral analysis of both the generator
-A_{p}+B_{p}
and the transport-reaction semigroup
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
Chapter

5
deals with the incorporation of different side lengths
L>0
of the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and Neumann boundary conditions for symmetric models in dimension
d=1
. In
Chapter

6
, we apply our results to, for example, a Goldstein-Kac-type equation
[
22
]
and a sophisticated model from
[
18
]
. Finally,
Chapter

7
is devoted to pattern formation and transport-driven instabilities.

We tried our best to write every chapter as self-contained as possible and to motivate all sections of the thesis with a detailed introduction at the beginning of each chapter.

One main contribution of the thesis is the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{2}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})}}\quad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

and a description of
\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})
in terms of roots of polynomials of order
N
for all dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
. This result allows a very detailed study of the long-term behavior of solution to (
1
). To the best of our knowledge, all current results deal with the one-dimensional case
d=1.
The proofs are based on Fourier transform and so called matrix multiplication operators from
[
21
]
.

The other main contribution is the chapter on pattern formation and transport-driven instabilities. We introduce the notion of hyperbolic instabilities, which allows us to prove a dichotomy: every transport-driven instability in
d=1
and with arbitrary number of components
N
is either a Turing pattern or a hyperbolic instability. Intuitively, hyperbolic instabilities correspond to increasingly oscillating and chaotic behavior. We do not hold expertise in biological modeling but we believe that our finding reflects the nature of movement of populations: either there is a regular pattern or one observes bustle. As a byproduct of our analysis, we find an algebraic condition in terms of the transport speeds
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
and
B
which ensures the existence of Turing patterns. The mathematical details of this chapter are based on perturbation theory from
[
23
]
.

1
Function Spaces and Notation

In order to rigorously define standard
L^{p}
spaces for
1\leq p<\infty
on a

d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we follow the approach from
[
11
, p. 238f.]
and consider the quotient map

q\colon[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{d},

which identifies opposite sides of the hypercube. This mapping induces the
measurable space
(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,q_{\sharp}\mathcal{B}([0,1]^{d}),\,q_{\sharp}\mathcal{L}^{d})
where
\mathcal{B}([0,1]^{d})
is the Borel

\sigma
-algebra on the hypercube and
\mathcal{L}^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional
Lebesgue measure. Moreover, the canonical norm on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N})
is given by

\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\coloneqq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|u_{j}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4)

for
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{N})\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
To give a better visualization, it should be mentioned that functions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
may be considered as periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or as functions on
[0,1)^{d}.

The more appropriate point of view will follow by the context when it matters

[
11
, p. 238f.]
. Generally, we are interested in functions

u\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N},
where the value of
u
at
(t,\mathbf{x})
is for instance the concentration of population subgroups at time
t
. The reason we are
working with complex-valued functions is that we approach the transport-reaction equation (
1
) from a
semigroup and spectral theory perspective. This may be counterintuitive at first
glance, but ultimately the only difference is that also complex initial functions are
allowed and that no inconsistencies in the spectral analysis occur. Given

real
initial values
u_{0}\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N},
every solution of the
equations we study will also be real valued.

It should also be mentioned that we almost always mean strongly continuous (semi-)group, when we use the term (semi-)group. A relatively detailed introduction to semigroup theory is given in Appendix
B
to Appendix
D
.

Last but not least, the thesis contains a list of symbols, which can be found at the very end.

Chapter 2
The Transport Semigroup

The first questions regarding the transport-reaction model
(
1
) are to ask for existence and uniqueness of
solutions. This chapter is devoted to answer these questions in the non-reacting case
B=0
by using a semigroup approach. In the process, we study the domain of the generator of the
transport semigroup and identify
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N})
as a core. The linear reactions in (
1
) are incorporated in the next chapter.

Throughout the whole chapter, we assume that the dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the number
of components
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the transport directions

\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
1\leq p<\infty
are
arbitrarily chosen fixed parameters, if not stated otherwise. We work on the
Banach space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
, which is defined in
(
4
).

We start by introducing the transport semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
explicitly via

(T(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(\mathbf{x}-t\mathbf{v_{1}})\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}(\mathbf{x}-t\mathbf{v_{N}})\end{pmatrix}

(5)

for
t\geq 0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
directions
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{N})\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Strictly speaking, we should include the indices
d
and
p

into the notation as the semigroup depends on the Banach space, but we will
often drop these two indices to improve readability.

Lemma 2.1
.

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
defines a contraction semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Proof.

The lemma with domain
\mathbb{R}^{d}
instead of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is a standard example for a
contraction semigroup and strong continuity follows by the density of

\mathbb{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N});
see for example
[
11
, Proposition 8.5, p. 238]
. On a torus, the proof works similarly. A density argument
and Fubini can be used to show

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|u_{j}(\mathbf{x})|^{p}\,d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|u_{j}(\mathbf{x}-t\mathbf{v_{j}})|^{p}\,d\mathbf{x}

for all
j=1,\cdots,N
and all
t\geq 0.
Moreover, the semigroup
property is a consequence of

((a\text{ mod }1)+b)\text{ mod }1=(a+b)\text{ mod }1\qquad\text{for all }a,\,b\in\mathbb{R}.

∎

In the following, we denote the generator of the transport semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
by
(-A,\,D(-A)).

Similarly to the semigroup, the generator depends on
d
and
p
. This should be
kept in mind for later chapters on the spectral analysis of the generator. In
this chapter, however, the dependence does not play a significant role, which is
why we drop the index. By definition, it holds

D(-A)=\left\{u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)}{t}\text{
exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\text{ for all }j=1,\cdots,N\right\}.

(6)

For continuously differentiable functions
u
, the limits in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
are simply given by

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)}{t}=-\mathbf{v}_{j}\cdot\nabla u_{j}

for all
j=1,\cdots,N.
We start with a functional analytic
approach to extend this result to weakly differentiable functions
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Studying convergence of difference quotients with
respect to the
L^{p}
-norm is very common and can for example be found in

[
9
, Part 2; 5.8.2, p. 293f.]
. Nevertheless, we decided to include this
approach because in the literature, the arguments are typically only given for the
standard unit vectors as directions. In our case, we have arbitrary directions

\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
This functional analytic
perspective is somewhat technical in the case
p=1
since
L^{1}
is not
reflexive. Therefore, these proofs also motivate a pure semigroup approach: on

\mathbb{T}^{d}
, abstract semigroup theory can be applied to obtain a shorter and easier
proof for the
L^{p}
-convergence of the difference quotients.

Consider a function
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and define the difference quotients

D_{k}^{h}u(\cdot)\coloneqq\frac{u(\cdot+h\mathbf{e_{k}})-u(\cdot)}{h}

(7)

for
h\in\mathbb{R}
and
k=1,\cdots,d.
In this definition,

\mathbf{e_{k}}
is the
k
-th standard unit vector of
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The next lemma
deals with the limit behavior as
h\rightarrow 0
(note that
h
is not assumed to have
a particular sign).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and let
k=1,\cdots,d.

(i)

Let
D_{k}^{h}u\rightharpoonup v
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Then the

k
-th weak derivative exists in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\partial_{k}u=v.

(ii)

Conversely, if
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it follows that
D_{k}^{h}u\rightarrow\partial_{k}u
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
p>1
and
D_{k}^{h}u\rightharpoonup\partial_{k}u
for
p=1
.

All convergences are with respect to
h\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

The details are given in Appendix
A
.
∎

Although the directions
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are
arbitrary in the definition of the generator of the transport semigroup
(
6
), a corresponding result can be shown in the exact same manner. That is, if we define the directional difference quotient

D_{\mathbf{v}}^{h}u(\cdot)\coloneqq\frac{u(\cdot+h\mathbf{v})-u(\cdot)}{h}.

(8)

for
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\,\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
h\in\mathbb{R},
the following lemma
holds true.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty,\,u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then
D_{\mathbf{v}}^{h}u\rightarrow\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla u
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
p>1

and
D_{\mathbf{v}}^{h}u\rightharpoonup\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla u
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
p=1
as

h\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

Use the inequality

\|D_{\mathbf{v}}^{h}u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq|\mathbf{v}|\,\||\nabla u|\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}

for
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and argue as in the proof of

Lemma

2.2
. Also the case
p=1
works
analogously.
∎

We define the operator
-\mathcal{A}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

-\mathcal{A}u=-\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}.

(9)

Here, we did not specify the domain intentionally. It will vary and we will
specify it later on for different scenarios. With the functional analytic arguments
from above, we obtain the following result concerning the domain of the
generator of
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}.

Lemma 2.4
.

For
1<p<\infty,
the generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
is an extension of

(-\mathcal{A},\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. In the case
p=1,
the generator is an
extension of
(-\mathcal{A},\,C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Proof.

The statement for
p=1
follows immediately from the chain rule and dominated
convergence. For
p>1,
we can apply
Lemma

2.3
componentwise to obtain

\frac{u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)}{t}=-D_{\mathbf{v_{j}}}^{-t}u_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow-\mathbf{v_{j}}\cdot\nabla u_{j}

in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
j=1,\cdots,N.

∎

Remarkably enough, the previous functional analytic arguments are insufficient
to show strong convergence in the case
p=1
. An alternative and shorter proof
that uses semigroup theory works for all cases
1\leq p<\infty.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. Then
(-A,\,D(-A))
extends
(-\mathcal{A},\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Remark 2.6
.

This implies in particular, that strong convergence in

Lemma

2.3
also holds for
p=1.

Proof.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Moreover, take an
approximation
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that

u^{(n)}\rightarrow u
in
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
By the convergence of the derivatives, it
follows that
-Au^{(n)}=-\mathcal{A}u^{(n)}\rightarrow-\mathcal{A}u
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Since generators of
C_{0}
-semigroups are closed by Hille-Yosida, see

Theorem

B.6
, we obtain
u\in D(-A)
and
-Au=-\mathcal{A}u.

∎

At this point, it is unclear, whether the generator of the transport semigroup
is uniquely determined by
(-\mathcal{A},\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).
However, the
semigroup is extremely well behaved in the sense that it is easy to identify

C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
as a core, i.e.
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is dense
in
D(-A)
w.r.t. the graph norm
\|\cdot\|_{-A}
.

Theorem 2.7
.

C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is a core for
(-A,\,D(-A)).
In particular,

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the only strongly continuous semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N})

with a generator that extends
(-\mathcal{A},\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Proof.

Clearly, we have
T(t)(C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N}).
This implies that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is a core for
(-A,\,D(-A))
by

[
8
, II. Proposition 1,7, p. 53]
.

The addendum is well known but we recall its short and nice proof. Let

(\widetilde{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a semigroup with generator
(-\widetilde{A},\,D(-\widetilde{A}))\supseteq(-\mathcal{A},\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. The
fact that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is a core and the closedness of

-\widetilde{A}
due to Hille-Yosida, see
Theorem

B.6
, imply

(-\widetilde{A},\,D(-\widetilde{A}))\supseteq(-A,\,D(-A))
. Hille-Yosida
also yields existence of some
\lambda\in\rho(-A)\cap\rho(-\widetilde{A})
and
hence, both the operators

\lambda+\widetilde{A}\colon D(-\widetilde{A})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

and

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\lambda+\widetilde{A}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{D(-A)}}=\lambda+A\colon D(-A)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

are bijective. We obtain
-A=-\widetilde{A}
, so the semigroups

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(\widetilde{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
coincide by
[
30
, Chapter 1,
Theorem 2.6, p. 6]
.

∎

Remark 2.8
.

By defining (
5
) for all
t\in\mathbb{R},
one can
directly see that
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
can be extended to a
C_{0}
-group

(T(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and that

D(-A)=\left\{u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)}{t}\text{ exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\text{ for all }j=1,\cdots,N\right\}.

(10)

The difference is subtle but notice that we have
t\rightarrow 0
in the above formula
instead of
t\searrow 0
as in (
6
). This equality
on the domain follows by an explicit computation because

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)}{t}\text{ exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\text{ for all }j=1,\cdots,N

if and only if

\lim_{t\nearrow 0}\frac{u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)}{t}\text{ exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\text{ for all }j=1,\cdots,N.

In particular, the operator
A
with
D(A)\coloneqq D(-A)
generates the

C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(-t))_{t\geq 0}
given by

(T(-t)u)(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(\mathbf{x}+t\mathbf{v_{1}})\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}(\mathbf{x}+t\mathbf{v_{N}})\end{pmatrix}

for
t\geq 0,
cf.
[
8
, II. 3.a, p. 79]
.

We now compute the adjoint semigroup
(T_{p}(t)^{*})_{t\geq 0}
of

(T_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})^{*}
and the adjoint
-A_{p}^{*}
of the generator

-A_{p}
, which will be pretty useful for the spectral analysis performed in

Chapter

4
. We would like to point out that the index
p

does play a crucial role here, in contrast to all other results in the chapter.

Recall the classical result
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})^{*}=L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
where
1<p<\infty
and
q
is the dual exponent of
p,
i.e.
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1,
see
[
6
, IV.8, Theorem 1, p. 286]
. Moreover,
the adjoint of the bounded operator
T_{p}(t)
is defined by

\langle T_{p}(t)u,\,v\rangle=\langle u,\,T_{p}(t)^{*}v\rangle\quad\text{
for all }u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ and all }v\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Again, the case
p=1
is somewhat a special case because
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is
not reflexive.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
(T_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup generated by

(-A_{p},\,D(-A_{p})).
Then
(T_{p}(t)^{*})_{t\geq 0}
is strongly continuous on

L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and it holds

\displaystyle(T_{p}(t)^{*})_{t\geq 0}

\displaystyle=(T_{q}(-t))_{t\geq 0},

\displaystyle(-A_{p}^{*},\,D(-A_{p}^{*}))

\displaystyle=(A_{q},\,D(A_{q})).

Proof.

The space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is reflexive by for
1<p<\infty,
see

[
6
, IV.8, Corollary 2, p. 288]
. Hence, the
adjoint semigroup is again strongly continuous by
[
8
, I 5.14
Proposition, p. 44]
and the sun dual semigroup coincides with the
adjoint semigroup, cf.
[
8
, II 2.6, p. 62]
. In
particular, the generator of the adjoint (strongly continuous) semigroup is the
adjoint of
-A_{p},
namely
-A_{p}^{*},
by
[
8
, II 2.6 proposition, p. 63]
.

It is left to show
-A_{p}^{*}=A_{q}
and to characterize the domain. Recall that
we have

\displaystyle D(-A_{p}^{*})=\left\{v\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\exists w\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ with }\langle-A_{p}u,v\rangle=\langle u,w\rangle\text{ for all }u\in D(-A_{p})\right\}

and

-A_{p}^{*}v=w

for all
v\in D(-A_{p}^{*}).
Using that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is a core for

(-A_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
by
Theorem

2.7
, it follows

D(-A_{p}^{*})=\left\{v\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\exists w\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ with }\langle-A_{p}u,v\rangle=\langle u,w\rangle\text{ for all }u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\right\}.

The next step is to show

(-A_{p}^{*},\,D(-A_{p}^{*}))\supseteq(A_{q},\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

(11)

To this end, let
u,v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
We use the notation

\mathbf{v_{j,k}}
for the
k
-th component of

\mathbf{v_{j}}
and compute

\displaystyle\langle-A_{p}u,v\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(-\mathbf{v_{j}}\cdot\nabla u_{j})\overline{v_{j}}\,d\mathbf{x}=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\partial_{k}u_{j}\overline{v_{j}}\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{j}\partial_{k}\overline{v_{j}}\,d\mathbf{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{j}\overline{\mathbf{v_{j}}\cdot\nabla v_{j}}\,d\mathbf{x}=\langle u,\,A_{q}v\rangle.

This shows (
11
), i.e.
(T_{p}^{*}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup with a
generator that extends
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

On the other hand,
(T_{q}(-t))_{t\geq 0}
is the unique semigroup on
L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with a generator that extends
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
by
Remark

2.8
and

Theorem

2.7
, which yields

(T_{p}(t)^{*})_{t\geq 0}=(T_{q}(-t))_{t\geq 0}.

In particular, the generators of both semigroups coincide.
∎

The dual characterization
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})^{*}=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
via
the dual exponent also holds for
p=1,
see
[
6
, IV.8, Theorem 5, p. 289]
. The difference to the cases
1<p<\infty
is that the
adjoint semigroup fails to be strongly continuous, hence we have not considered
the generators on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
yet. We restrict ourselves to the case

d=1
with
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
and make up this technical leeway with the
definitions

-A_{\infty}u\coloneqq-\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u_{N}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

(12)

and

D(-A_{\infty})\coloneqq\big\{u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,u\text{ is
weakly differentiable, }-A_{\infty}u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\big\}.

(13)

We obtain the following result concerning the dual of
(-A_{1},\,D(-A_{1})).

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
p=1
and let
d=1
. Assume that the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}

are non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
. Then it holds

\displaystyle D(-A_{\infty})

\displaystyle=W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),

\displaystyle(-A_{1}^{*},\,D(-A_{1}^{*}))

\displaystyle=(A_{\infty},W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Proof.

The characterization of the domain follows directly from

u_{j}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{v_{j}}(-A_{\infty}u)_{j}

for all
j=1,\cdots,N
, so we are left to compute the adjoint
-A_{1}^{*}.

Let
\widetilde{u}\in D(-A_{1}^{*}).
Then there exists
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with

\langle-A_{1}u,\,\widetilde{u}\rangle=\langle u,\,w\rangle

for all
u\in D(-A_{1}).
In particular, given
j=1,\cdots,N,
we can choose
\varphi\in\mathbb{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
and

u_{j}\coloneqq\frac{1}{v_{j}}\varphi,\qquad u_{i}\coloneqq 0\quad\text{ for }i\neq j

as the transport directions are non-vanishing. Testing with
u
implies

-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi^{\prime}\overline{\widetilde{u}_{j}}\,dx=\frac{1}{v_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi\overline{w_{j}}\,dx

(14)

for all test functions
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}).
We obtain that
\widetilde{u}
is
weakly differentiable with
\widetilde{u}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Now, given
\widetilde{u}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
we can compute

\displaystyle\langle-A_{1}u,\,\widetilde{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}v_{j}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{j}^{\prime}\overline{\widetilde{u}_{j}}\,dx=\sum_{j=1}^{N}v_{j}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{j}\overline{\widetilde{u}_{j}^{\prime}}\,dx=\langle u,\,A_{\infty}\widetilde{u}\rangle

This shows
-A_{1}^{*}\widetilde{u}=A_{\infty}\widetilde{u}
because
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is a core for
(-A_{1},\,D(-A_{1}))
by
Theorem

2.7
.
∎

In dimension
d=1,
we can even show
D(-A)=W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
if we assume
that the transport directions are non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{j}\neq 0
for
all
j=1,\cdots,N.
It should be noted that the assumption cannot be
dropped. By definition (
6
) of the domain of the
generator, there are no restrictions on
u_{j}
if
v_{j}=0
and the
component could be an arbitrary function in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}).

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let
d=1.
Assume that
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
are
non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0.
Then

(-A,\,D(-A))=(-\mathcal{A},\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Proof.

One direction follows from
Lemma

2.5
. Concerning the other
direction, let
u\in D(-A)
. By
Theorem

2.7
, we can choose a
sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
u^{(n)}\rightarrow u

and
\mathcal{A}u^{(n)}=Au^{(n)}\rightarrow Au
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Since we
assumed non-vanishing transport directions, it follows that

\|{u_{j}^{\prime}}^{(n)}-{u_{j}^{\prime}}^{(m)}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}=\frac{1}{|v_{j}|}\|(\mathcal{A}u^{(n)})_{j}-(\mathcal{A}u^{(m)})_{j}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as }n,\,m\rightarrow\infty

holds for all
j=1,\cdots,N
. Hence, the sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n}
is Cauchy in

W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
We obtain
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
Au=\mathcal{A}u
by
Lemma

2.5
.

∎

For higher dimensions, there are in some sense too many directions. On an
intuitive level, a function
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that the components
u_{j}

“behave nicely” along the directions
\mathbf{v_{j}}
has enough
regularity to be an element of
D(-A).
In general, this should be insufficient
for
u
to be weakly differentiable for dimensions
d\geq 2
and one would expect

W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subsetneq D(-A)
. However, we can only prove this under
the assumption that one component is transport periodic. Let us clarify what we
mean by that.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be non-vanishing, i.e.

\mathbf{v_{1},}\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\neq 0.
Moreover, let
j=1,\cdots,N
.

(i)

Component
j
is transport periodic if there exists
t^{*}>0
such that

t^{*}\mathbf{v_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In this case,

\tau_{j}\coloneqq\min\{t>0\,\colon\,t\mathbf{v_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}.

is called the period of the
j
-th component.

(ii)

The model is jointly periodic if there exists
t^{*}>0
such that

t^{*}\mathbf{v_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for all
j=1,\cdots,N
. In this
case,

\tau\coloneqq\min\{t>0\,\colon\,t\mathbf{v_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\text{
for all }j=1,\cdots,N\}.

is called the period of the model.

Remark 2.13
.

The definition of jointly periodic coincides with the transport semigroup

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
defined in (
5
) being
periodic in the canonical sense of
[
8
, IV. Definition 2.23, p. 266]
. We need a concept to distinguish between the different
components in order to give a relatively weak condition for
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})=D(-A)
to fail in higher dimensions.

Remark 2.14
.

In dimension
d=1,
every component is transport periodic with
\tau_{j}=1/|v_{j}|,
assuming that
v_{j}\neq 0.
This is not the case in
higher dimensions.

Let us characterize transport periodicity algebraically. To this end, we use the
notation
\mathbf{v_{j,k}}
for the
k
-th component of

\mathbf{v_{j}}.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be non-vanishing, i.e.

\mathbf{v_{1},}\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\neq 0.
Moreover, let
j=1,\cdots,N.
The following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

Component
j
is transport periodic.

(ii)

\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\in\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\mathbb{Q}

for all
k=1,\cdots,d
and one (and hence all)
k^{*}
with

\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\neq 0.

(iii)

\displaystyle\bigcap^{d}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}.

Proof.

The details are given in Appendix
A
.
∎

Joint periodicity can also be characterized in an algebraic way.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be non-vanishing, i.e.

\mathbf{v_{1},}\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\neq 0.
The following assertions are
equivalent:

(i)

The model is jointly periodic.

(ii)

\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\in\mathbf{v_{j^{*},k^{*}}}\mathbb{Q}

for all
j=1,\cdots,N,
all
k=1,\cdots,d
and one (and hence
all)
j^{*}
and
k^{*}
with
\mathbf{v_{j^{*},k^{*}}}\neq 0.

(iii)

\displaystyle\bigcap_{j=1}^{N}\displaystyle\bigcap^{d}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}.

Proof.

Notice that the model is jointly periodic if and only if there exists
t^{*}>0

such that

t^{*}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}^{Nd}.

The result follows from an application of

Lemma

2.15
to the concatenated
vector.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

For
d\geq 2,
the above results show that there are very few transport
directions such that one component is transport periodic or the model is jointly
periodic in the sense that these directions are a nullset in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

\mathbb{R}^{Nd}
respectively. However, these are the natural cases a human would
choose as an example when trying to show results for
d\geq 2.
Even for these
”simple” periodic cases, many properties which hold true for
d=1
fail in
higher dimensions.

The first negative result for higher dimensions is the next theorem.

Theorem 2.18
.

Let
d\geq 2
and let
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}
be non-vanishing,
i.e.
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\neq 0
. Assume that there is one
transport periodic component. Then

(-\mathcal{A},\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\subsetneq(-A,\,D(-A)).

Proof.

Let component
j
be transport periodic with period
\tau_{j}
.
We show existence of a function
u\in D(-A)
with
u\notin W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for all

1\leq p<\infty
. The idea is to take a non-differentiable function
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
u_{j}
is constant along direction

\mathbf{v_{j}}.

Let
\mathbf{c}=(\frac{1}{2},\cdots,\frac{1}{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be the center of the torus.
By assumption, the curve

\gamma\colon[0,\tau_{j}]\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{d}\quad\text{with}\quad\gamma(t)\coloneqq\mathbf{c}+t\mathbf{v_{j}}

fulfills
\gamma(0)=\gamma(\tau_{j}).
Let
K=\range(\gamma)
be the range of

\gamma
. Then there exists
\delta>0
with

U_{\delta}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\,\colon\,\dist(\mathbf{x},\,K)<\delta\}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}^{d}.

(15)

We define
u_{j}\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\text{$U_{\delta}$}}
and
u_{i}\equiv 0
for all

i=1,\cdots,N
with
i\neq j.
Notice that
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and that
u_{j}
is
constant along direction
\mathbf{v_{j}},
i.e.

u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)\equiv 0.

Hence,
u\in D(-A)
with
-Au=0
and it is left to show that
u_{j}\notin W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for all
1\leq p<\infty.
Assume that for all

k=1,\cdots,d
there exist
g_{k}\in L^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}g_{k}\varphi\,d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{j}\partial_{k}\varphi\,d\mathbf{x}=\int_{U_{\delta}}\partial_{k}\varphi\,d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\partial U_{\delta}}\varphi\mathbf{n_{k}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\mathbf{x})

holds for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with outer normal
\mathbf{n}
of

U_{\delta}.
Notice that the last step follows from Gauss’s theorem. Testing with

\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{\delta})
and
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{U_{\delta}}^{c})
respectively implies
g_{k}=0
almost
everywhere on
(\partial U_{\delta})^{c}
by the fundamental lemma of calculus of
variations. The boundary
\partial U_{\delta}
is a Lebesgue-nullset and hence,

g_{k}=0
holds almost everywhere in
\mathbb{T}^{d}
for all
k=1,\cdots,d.

Consequently,
\nabla u_{j}=0
and
u_{j}
is constant by the connectedness of the
torus. This contradicts the definition of
u_{j}
.
∎

Figure 1:
An
illustration of the idea behind the function constructed in
(
15
) for
d=2
in the exemplary case

\mathbf{v_{j}}=(\tfrac{1}{4},\tfrac{1}{8}).
The whole area is the torus

\mathbb{T}^{2}
and the green area is the support of
u_{j}
for

\delta=\tfrac{1}{4\sqrt{5}}
.

After achieving a better understanding of the domain of the generator, it would
be reasonable to turn our attention to the question of well-posedness for two
abstract Cauchy problems. Let
T>0
and
I=[0,T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{t\geq 0}
be an interval. The linear hyperbolic Cauchy problem on
I
reads

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+Au(t)&=&F(t)&\qquad 0<t<T,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}

(16)

for
u_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
an interval and
F\in C(I,\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. The semilinear hyperbolic Cauchy problem on
I
is given by

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+Au(t)&=&F(t,u(t))&\qquad 0<t<T,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}

(17)

for
u_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
F\in C(I\times L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

We decided to postpone the overview on well-posedness results for these
equations to
Chapter

3
because the transport
semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
with generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
is a special case of
the transport-reaction semigroup studied in the next chapter.

Chapter 3
The Transport-Reaction Semigroup

As in the previous chapter, we assume that the dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the number of
components
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the transport directions

\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
1\leq p<\infty
are
arbitrarily chosen fixed parameters, if not stated otherwise. We work on the
Banach space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
, which is defined in
(
4
).

Eventually, our goal is to study the stability and long-time behavior of
solutions to the transport-reaction model (
2
). Given an equilibrium state
u=c=\const\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
F(c)=0,
we follow the
standard approach by defining
u=c+w
and linearizing the system for the
perturbation
w.
For a solution of the form
u=c+w
, one has

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
\vdots\\
w_{N}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla w_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla w_{N}\end{pmatrix}=F(u)=DF(c)w+\mathcal{O}(|w|^{2})

where
DF(c)\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is the Jacobian of
F
evaluated at
c.
The
(relabeled) linearized system then reads

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=DF(c)u.

For this reason, we will study the equation

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

(18)

for an a priori arbitrary matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}.
It should be noted
that
B
is a
real
matrix and that it induces a bounded linear operator
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
by pointwise matrix multiplication. We will use the letter
B
for
the matrix
and
the operator. From a semigroup point of view, there are
two different perspectives on (
18
). One possibility is to consider the abstract Cauchy problem

\dot{u}(t)+Au(t)=Bu(t),

(19)

and to solve it via analyzing the generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
of the transport
semigroup. This approach can be carried out with the knowledge from

Chapter

2
and Appendix
B
.
The other possibility is to consider the abstract Cauchy problem

\dot{u}(t)+(A-B)u(t)=0

(20)

and to study the semigroup generated by
-A+B.
This section addresses the
second approach and we study the reaction and transport-reaction semigroups
generated by
B
and
-A+B
respectively. The main advantage is that standard
perturbation theory gives a detailed description of the perturbed semigroup,
which allows us to prove qualitative properties of the solutions to
(
18
).

An overview of the results from perturbation theory for semigroups we
apply, including references and adaptations to our simple case, can be found in
Appendix
C
.

As a first step, we take a closer look to the semigroup generated by
(B,\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and characterize positivity. The main idea for this characterization was
found on
[
5
]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty.
A function
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is called non-negative if

u_{j}(\mathbf{x})\geq 0\text{ for almost all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\text{ and all }j=1,\cdots,N.

In this case, we write
u\geq 0
and we define
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})_{+}\coloneqq\{u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,u\geq 0\}.

Remark 3.2
.

The set
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})_{+}
is a closed subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
since convergence in

\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
implies pointwise convergence almost everywhere along a
subsequence.

Definition 3.3
.

A semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is called positive if each operator

T(t)
is positive, i.e. if

u\geq 0\implies T(t)u\geq 0

for all
t\geq 0.
Similarly, a group
(T(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is called positive if

T(t)
is positive for all
t\in\mathbb{R}.

Remark 3.4
.

These defintions make
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
a Banach lattice and are just special cases of
already existing general theory, cf.
[
33
]
. Postive
semigroups arise naturally in biology and physics when
u
describes non-negative
quantities, like for example concentrations. Moreover, there are strong results concerning
specral theory and long-time asymptotics of positive semigroups, which is why we
are interested in a characterization of all matrices
B
such that the semigroup
generated by
-A+B
becomes positive. The fundamentals in a concrete context can
be found in
[
8
]
and the detailed theory is presented in

[
1
]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The operator
(B,\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
generates the uniformly continuous semigroup

(S(t))_{t\geq 0}=(e^{tB})_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
where
e^{tB}
is the operator
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
given by pointwise matrix multiplication with the matrix
e^{tB}.

The semigroup is positive if and only if the off-diagonal entries of
B
are
non-negative.

Proof.

Notice that
B
defines a bounded linear operator on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
due to the
estimate

\|Bu\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\|B\|_{\infty}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Hence,
(B,\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
generates the uniformly continuous semigroup

(S(t))_{t\geq 0}=(e^{tB})_{t\geq 0}
by
[
30
, Chapter 1, Theorem 1.2, p. 2]
.

Let us show that this semigroup is positive if the off-diagonal entries of
B

are non-negative. To this end, we firstly assume that the off-diagonal entries
are positive, i.e.

b\coloneqq\min_{i\neq j}B_{ij}>0.

(21)

Let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})_{+}
and let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
such that
u(\mathbf{x})\geq 0
. By definition, we have

(S(t)u)(\mathbf{x})=e^{tB}u(\mathbf{x})=(I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}+tB)u(\mathbf{x})+R_{t}u(\mathbf{x})

for a matrix
R_{t}
with
\|R_{t}\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(t^{2})
as
t\rightarrow 0.
This implies
existence of
t_{1}=t_{1}(B)>0
and
C=C(B)>0
such that

\left(e^{tB}u(\mathbf{x})\right)_{j}\geq\big((I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}+tB)u(\mathbf{x})\big)_{j}-\|R_{t}\|_{\infty}\|u(\mathbf{x})\|_{\infty}\geq\big((I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}+tB)u(\mathbf{x})\big)_{j}-Ct^{2}\|u(\mathbf{x})\|_{\infty}

for all
j=1,\cdots,N
and
0\leq t\leq t_{1}.
By our assumption
(
21
), we also have existence of
t_{2}=t_{2}(B)>0

with

\big(I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}+tB\big)_{ij}\geq tb>0

for all
i,j=1,\cdots,N
and all
0\leq t\leq t_{2}.
Notice
that the identity matrix dominates the diagonal for small
t.

Both estimates together imply

\left(e^{tB}u(\mathbf{x})\right)_{j}\geq tb\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}(\mathbf{x})-Ct^{2}\|u(\mathbf{x})\|_{\infty}\geq t(b-Ct)\|u(\mathbf{x})\|_{\infty}\geq 0

for all
j=1,\cdots,N
and
t\leq t^{*}=t^{*}(B)\coloneqq\min\{t_{1},\,t_{2},\,b/C\}.
This implies that the operators
S(t)
are positive for

0\leq t\leq t^{*}.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},
choose
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
r\in[0,t^{*})
such that
t=mt^{*}+r.

Then

S(t)=S(r)S(t^{*})^{m}

is a positive operator, since compositions preserve positivity.

Secondly, let the off-diagonal entries of
B
be non-negative. In this case, we
take a sequence
(B^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of matrices with positive off-diagonal entries
such that
B^{(n)}\rightarrow B
in
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}.
Let
u\geq 0,\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
with
u(\mathbf{x})\geq 0
and
t\geq 0.
Our first step
implies

(S(t)u)(\mathbf{x})=e^{tB^{(n)}}u(\mathbf{x})+(e^{tB}-e^{tB^{(n)}})u(\mathbf{x})\geq-\|e^{tB}-e^{tB^{(n)}}\|_{\infty}\|u(\mathbf{x})\|_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0

as
n\rightarrow\infty,
i.e.
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
defines a positive semigroup.

Conversely, if there exists one negative off-diagonal entry

B_{ij}<0
, we define the non-negative function
u\equiv e_{j}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for the standard unit vector
e_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}.

Similar to before, we obtain the estimate

(S(t)u)_{i}(\mathbf{x})\equiv\left(e^{tB}e_{j}\right)_{i}\leq\big((I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}+tB)e_{j}\big)_{i}+Ct^{2}=t(B_{ij}+Ct)<0

for some constant
C=C(B)>0
and small
t.
The last inequality is a
consequence of the negativity of
B_{ij}
. In particular,

S(t)
is not a positive operator for small enough
t>0
and the semigroup

(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
cannot be positive.
∎

Given this knowledge, we can use perturbation theory to show that
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
generates a strongly continuous semigroup, which we call the
transport-reaction semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}.

Theorem 3.6
.

The operator
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}

on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Moreover, the following properties hold:

(i)

\|R(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\leq e^{\|B\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}t}

(ii)

R(t)u=T(t)u+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)BR(s)u\,ds

(iii)

R(t)u=T(t)u+\int_{0}^{t}R(t-s)BT(s)\,ds

(iv)

R(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}R_{k}(t)

with

R_{0}(t)=T(t),\quad R_{k+1}(t)u=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)BR_{k}(s)u\,ds

(v)

R(t)u=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(T(\tfrac{t}{n})e^{t/n\,B}\right)^{n}u

for all
t\geq 0
and
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Moreover, the graph norms of
-A
and

-A+B
on
D(-A)
are equivalent, the series in
(iv)
converges in

\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and for every
fixed
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
the convergence in
(v)
is uniform on compact subsets of

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

In all of the above statements,
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the transport semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

defined in (
5
) and with generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
.

Proof.

Let us recall that
(-A,\,D(-A))
generates the transport semigroup

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
from
Chapter

2
and that
(B,\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
generates the uniformly continuous semigroup
(e^{tB})_{t\geq 0},
see

Lemma

3.5
.

The fact that
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup and properties

(i)-(iii)
directly follow from

Theorem

C.1
. Property
(iv)
is a
consequence of
Theorem

C.2
. Also the
addenda, except for the uniform convergence of
(v)
, are consequences of these
two theorems.

Furthermore, property
(v)
and its corresponding addendum can be shown by
applying
Theorem

C.3
. Notice that

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a contraction semigroup, so we have the stability bound

\displaystyle\|(T(\tfrac{t}{n})e^{t/n\,B})^{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\|T(\tfrac{t}{n})\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\|e^{t/n\,B}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle\leq(e^{t/n\,\|B\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}})^{n}=e^{t\|B\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}}.

Also, we already know that
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup from
the previous steps. Hence, we can find some
\lambda>\|B\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}

such that
(\lambda I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-(-A+B))D(-A)=L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
by Hille-Yosida, see

Theorem

B.6
. Another consequence of Hille-Yosida is that the closure of

(-A+B,\,D(-A))
is again
(-A+B,\,D(-A)).
Therefore, in view of

Theorem

C.3
, the formula on the right-hand
side of
(v)
is indeed a formula for the transport-reaction semigroup.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N})
is a core for
(-A+B,\,D(-A)).
In particular,

(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the only strongly continuous semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with a
generator that extends
(-A+B,\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Proof.

We know from
Theorem

2.7
that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is
a core for
(-A,\,D(-A)).
Therefore,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is also a
core for
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
because the graph norms of both operators are
equivalent by
Theorem

3.6
. The uniqueness property is a general result for cores, cf.

Theorem

2.7
.
∎

For completeness, we give a brief intermezzo on the well-posedness of two abstract
Cauchy problems. Let
T>0
. The linear hyperbolic Cauchy problem on the interval
I=[0,T)
reads

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+(A-B)u(t)&=&F(t)&\qquad 0<t<T,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}

(22)

for
u_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
F\colon(0,T)\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. The semilinear hyperbolic
Cauchy problem on
I
is given by

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+(A-B)u(t)&=&F(t,u(t))&\qquad 0<t<T,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}

(23)

for
u_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
F\colon(0,T)\times L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

The standard solution concepts for equations of type
(
22
) and
(
23
) are classical and mild
solutions, as well as maximal existence times
T_{\text{max}}(u_{0})
. A detailed
overview of all concepts we use, including precise definitions and references,
can be found in Appendix
B
and we
strongly recommend reading Appendix
B
for further details
on the topic.

Without any a priori bounds of the solution in concrete examples, we can only
apply the standard existence and uniqueness results from
Appendix
B
.

Theorem 3.8

(cf.
Theorem

B.14
)
.

The initial value problem (
22
) has a
unique classical solution
u\in C(I,\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\cap C^{1}((0,T),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
on
I

for every
u_{0}\in D(-A),
if
F
satisfies one of the following two properties:

(i)

F\in C^{1}([0,T],\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})),

(ii)

F\in C((0,T),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\cap L^{1}((0,T),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\quad\text{ and }\quad F(s)\in D(-A)\text{ for all }s\in(0,T)\text{ with }AF(\cdot)\in L^{1}((0,T),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Remark 3.9
.

The homogeneous equation with
F\equiv 0
has the global classical solution

u(\cdot)=R(\cdot)u_{0}\in C^{1}([0,\infty),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
for
u_{0}\in D(-A),
see

Theorem

B.10
.

In the semilinear case (
23
), the
existence theorem for mild solutions reads as follows.

Theorem 3.10

(cf.
Theorem

B.17
)
.

Let
F\colon[0,\infty)\times L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
be continuous in
t
and locally
Lipschitz continuous in
u
, uniformly in
t
on bounded intervals. Then for
every
u_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
there is a time
T_{\text{max}}=T_{\text{max}}(\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})})>0,
possibly
T_{\text{max}}=T,
such that the
initial value problem (
23
) has a
unique mild solution
u
on
[0,T_{\text{max}}).
Moreover, if
T_{\text{max}}<T,
then

\lim_{t\nearrow T_{\text{max}}}\|u(t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\infty.

Remark 3.11
.

If for each
t^{\prime}>0,
there exists a constant
C(t^{\prime})>0
such that

\|F(t,u)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C(1+\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})})

holds for all
t\in[0,t^{\prime}]
and all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, then
(
23
) has a mild solution on

[0,\infty),
i.e. one can choose
T=\infty
and it holds
T_{\text{max}}=\infty,

see
B.18
.

Under stronger regularity assumptions on the right-hand side and the initial
state, one obtains existence of strong solutions.

Theorem 3.12

(cf.
Theorem

B.19
)
.

Let
F\in C([0,\infty)\times L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,D(-A))
be uniformly (in t) locally
Lipschitz continuous in
u
. Then for every
u_{0}\in D(-A),
the initial value
problem (
23
) possesses a unique
maximal strong solution

u\in C([0,T_{\text{max}}),\,D(-A))\cap C^{1}((0,T_{\text{max}}),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Moreover, the solution depends continuously on the initial data in the sense of
the next theorem.

Corollary 3.13

(cf.
Corollary

B.20
)
.

Let
F\in C([0,\infty)\times L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
be globally Lipschitz continuous
in
u.
Then the mild solution depends Lipschitz continuously on
u_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
i.e. for all
T>0,
the mapping
u_{0}\mapstochar\rightarrow u
is Lipschitz continuous
from
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
into
C([0,T],\,L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.

After this summary of well-posedness results for the general transport reaction
equation (
2
), we again focus on the linear case
(
18
).

Similar to the transport semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0},
we can extend the
semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
to a strongly continuous group
(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
show an analogous result to (
10
). However, we
have to be a little bit more careful as in
Remark

2.8

because the semigroup is less explicit.

Lemma 3.14
.

The semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
can be (uniquely) extended to the
C_{0}
-group

(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
where
(R(-t))_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup
(R_{-}(t))_{t\geq 0}

generated by
(A-B,\,D(A))
with
D(A)=D(-A).
Moreover, we have

D(-A)=\left\{u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{R(t)u-u}{t}\textup{ exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\right\}.

(24)

Proof.

Both
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
and
(A-B,\,D(A))
generate the
C_{0}
-semigroups

(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(R_{-}(t))_{t\geq 0}
respectively by

Theorem

3.6
, since the change of signs simply
changes the direction of movement and the sign of
B,
i.e. the model is still
of the type covered in
Theorem

3.6
.

Now,
[
8
, II 3.11, p. 79]
implies that
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
generates a strongly continuous group
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

with

D(-A)=\left\{u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\widetilde{R}(t)u-u}{t}\text{ exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\right\}.

(25)

Notice that the generator theorems in
[
8
]
are insufficient to
show that
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is in fact the
C_{0}
-group we would
expect. We only have that
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup with a
generator that
extends

(-A+B,\,D(-A))
and that

(\widetilde{R}(-t))_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup whose generator
extends

(A-B,\,D(A)).
But this has the consequence that the generators of these
semigroups extend
(-A+B,\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
(A-B,\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
respectively. However,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

is a core of
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
and
(A-B,\,D(A))
by

Corollary

3.7
, so we obtain

(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}=(R(t))_{t\geq 0}\quad\text{ and }\quad(\widetilde{R}(-t))_{t\geq 0}=(R_{-}(t))_{t\geq 0}.

That is,
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}=(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is an extension of

(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
. Given this equality,
(
24
) follows from (
25
), which
completes the proof.
∎

Alternatively, one can prove the above result in a natural way by explicitly
defining the extension
(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and showing the group property. In this
case, one can use a comparable argument with cores to show
(
24
). The logic is interesting in itself
and we also present this approach.

A second proof of
Lemma

3.14
.

Let
(R_{-}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup generated by
(A-B,\,D(A)).
We
define

R(t)=\begin{cases}R(t)&t\geq 0,\\
R_{-}(-t)&t\leq 0.\end{cases}

This defines a strongly continuous group by
R(-t)=R(t)^{-1}.
The latter
follows from

\frac{d}{dt}R(-t)R(t)u=(A-B)R(-t)R(t)u+R(-t)(-A+B)R(t)u=0

for all
u\in D(-A)
and density of
D(-A)
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Let
(\widetilde{A},\,D(\widetilde{A}))
be the generator of the
C_{0}
-group
(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
in the
sense of
[
8
, II 3.11, p. 79]
, i.e.

D(\widetilde{A})=\left\{u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{R(t)u-u}{t}\text{ exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\right\}

and

\widetilde{A}u=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{R(t)u-u}{t}

for
u\in D(\widetilde{A}).
It is left to show that
(\widetilde{A},\,D(\widetilde{A}))=(-A+B,\,D(-A))
holds. The direction “
\subseteq
”
follows directly from the definition of a generator, see
Definition

B.3
.
For the other direction, notice that
(\widetilde{A},\,D(\widetilde{A}))

generates a strongly continuous semigroup
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
by

[
8
, II 3.11, p. 79]
with
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subseteq D(\widetilde{A})
because
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
lies in the
domains of the generators of
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(R_{-}(t))_{t\geq 0}
by

Lemma

2.5
. Hence,
(\widetilde{A},\,D(\widetilde{A}))
is
closed by
Theorem

B.6
and extends
(-A+B,\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).
The set
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is a core of
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
,
see
Corollary

3.7
, and as a consequence, we have

(\widetilde{A},\,D(\widetilde{A}))\supseteq(-A+B,\,D(-A))
by the
closedness of
\widetilde{A}.

∎

The next chapter deals with the spectral properties of the generators

(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
and the generated semigroups
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on

L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, mostly in the case
p=2.

We also present methods which enable some extensions of the results to
1\leq p<\infty.
At this point, it is unclear whether the spectra are independent of
p
and a key
strategy will be to start by showing equality of the spectra for all
p\geq 2.

In a second step, one can extend the results to
1\leq p\leq 2
by using a
duality argument. To this end, it is crucial to study the adjoint transport-reaction semigroup
(R_{p}(t)^{*})_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})^{*}.
Recall the classical result

L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})^{*}=L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, where
1<p<\infty
and
q
is the dual
exponent of
p,
i.e.
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1,
see

[
6
, IV.8, Theorem 1, p. 286]
. Moreover, the
adjoint of the bounded operator
R_{p}(t)
is defined by

\langle R_{p}(t)u,\,v\rangle=\langle u,\,R_{p}(t)^{*}v\rangle\quad\text{
for all }u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ and all }v\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Similar to
Proposition

2.9
and
Proposition

2.10
, we
split our analysis into the cases
1<p<\infty
and
p=1.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

generated by
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p})).
Then
(R_{p}(t)^{*})_{t\geq 0}
is a
strongly continuous semigroup on
L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with generator
(A_{q}+B_{q}^{T},\,D(A_{q}))
. Moreover, it holds

((-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*},\,D((-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*}))=(A_{q}+B_{q}^{T},\,D(A_{q}))^{.}

Proof.

The space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is reflexive, see
[
6
, IV.8, Corollary 2, p. 288]
. Hence, the adjoint semigroup is again strongly
continuous by
[
8
, I 5.14 Proposition, p. 44]
and
the sun dual semigroup coincides with the adjoint semigroup, cf.

[
8
, II 2.6, p. 62]
. In particular, the generator of
the adjoint (strongly continuous) semigroup is the adjoint
(-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*}
by

[
8
, II 2.6 proposition, p. 63]
.

It is left to show
(-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*}=A_{q}+B_{q}^{T}
. First of all, it should be
noted that

\displaystyle\langle B_{p}u,\,v\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(Bu(\mathbf{x}))_{j}\overline{v_{j}(\mathbf{x})}\,d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\langle Bu(\mathbf{x}),\,v(\mathbf{x})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\,d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle u(\mathbf{x}),\,B^{T}v(\mathbf{x})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{j}(\mathbf{x})\overline{(B^{T}v(\mathbf{x}))_{j}}\,d\mathbf{x}=\langle u,\,B_{q}^{T}v\rangle

holds for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
v\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
i.e.

B_{p}^{*}=B_{q}^{T}.

Secondly, recall that we have already shown
(-A_{p}^{*},\,D(-A_{p}^{*}))=(A_{q},\,D(A_{q}))
in
Proposition

2.9
. Finally, the boundedness of
B_{p}

implies

((-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*},\,D((-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*}))=(-A_{p}^{*}+B_{p}^{*},\,D(-A_{p}^{*}))=(A_{q}+B_{q}^{T},\,D(A_{q})).

∎

The dual characterization
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})^{*}=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
via
the dual exponent also holds for
p=1,
see
[
6
, IV.8, Theorem 5, p. 289]
. The difference to the cases
1<p<\infty
is that the
adjoint semigroup fails to be strongly continuous. As in

Chapter

2
, we restrict ourselves to the case
d=1
with

v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
and make up this technical leeway with the definitions

(-A_{\infty}+B_{\infty})u\coloneqq-\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u_{N}^{{}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}+Bu

(26)

and

D(-A_{\infty}+B_{\infty})\coloneqq D(-A_{\infty})=\big\{u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,u\text{ is weakly differentiable, }-A_{\infty}u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\big\}.

(27)

We obtain the following result concerning the dual of
(-A_{1}+B_{1},\,D(-A_{1})).

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
p=1
and let
d=1
. Assume that the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}

are non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
. Then it holds

\displaystyle D(-A_{\infty}+B_{\infty})

\displaystyle=W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),

\displaystyle((-A_{1}+B_{1})^{*},\,D((-A_{1}+B_{1})^{*}))

\displaystyle=(A_{\infty}+B_{\infty}^{T},\,W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).

Proof.

The characterization of the domain was proven in

Proposition

2.10
. Moreover, we have
B_{1}^{*}=B_{\infty}^{T},
which
can be obtained just like in
Proposition

3.15
.

Proposition

2.10
also yields

((-A_{1}+B_{1})^{*},\,D((-A_{1}+B_{1})^{*}))=(-A_{1}^{*}+B_{1}^{*},\,D(-A_{1}^{*}))=(A_{\infty}+B_{\infty}^{T},\,W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))

because
B_{1}
is bounded.
∎

We will now focus our attention on two important qualitative properties of
transport-reaction semigroups: positivity and mass conservation. It turns out
that both properties can be fully characterized by conditions on the matrix
B
.
These conditions are the same as for the ODE model

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{y}(t)&=&By(t)&\qquad t>0,\\
y(0)&=&y_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}

(28)

with given initial state
y_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{N},
which is very reasonable because
transport should not have any impact on the positivity of the concentrations or
the total mass of the system.

Theorem 3.17
.

The semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A+B,\,D(A))
is
positive if and only if the off-diagonal entries of
B
are non-negative.

Proof.

Let us begin by showing that
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is positive if the off-diagonal
entries of
B
are non-negative. The strategy is to apply
(v)
from

Theorem

3.6
. Recall that
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})_{+}
is a closed
subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, see
Remark

3.2
. Let

u\geq 0
and let
t\geq 0.
The result simply follows from the fact that the
transport semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}=(e^{tB})_{t\geq 0}

are positive by
Lemma

3.5
. Hence,

\left(T(\tfrac{t}{n})e^{t/n\,B}\right)^{n}u\geq 0

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
implies
R(t)u\geq 0
.

Conversely, assume that the semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a positive
semigroup. This time, the idea is to conclude that
B=(-A+B)+A
generates a
positive semigroup. If this is the case, the statement will follow from

Lemma

3.5
. Some caution is required,
since the perturbation of the generator
-A+B
of the positive semigroup

(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is unbounded. Nevertheless, we can apply

Theorem

C.3
. More precisely, we
know that
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
and
(A,\,D(A))
generate the positive

C_{0}
-semigroups
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(T(-t))_{t\geq 0}
respectively. The
latter is a consequence of the fact that
(-A,\,D(-A))
generates a positive

C_{0}
-group, cf.
Remark

2.8
. We have the stability
bound

\displaystyle\|(R(\tfrac{t}{n})T(-\tfrac{t}{n}))^{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\leq\|R(\tfrac{t}{n})\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}^{n}\leq e^{\|B\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}t},

since
(T(-t))_{t\geq 0}
is a
C_{0}
-semigroup of contractions and by an
application of statement
(i)
from
Theorem

3.6
.
Next, we notice that
B\in\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
implies that

(\lambda I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-B)L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})=L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

holds for
\lambda>\|B\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}
and therefore, a standard density
argument implies density of
(\lambda I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-B)D(-A)
by the density of

D(-A)
and the continuity of
B
. We can finally apply

Theorem

C.3
and conclude that
(B,\,D(-A))

has a closure which generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(\widetilde{S}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
This is unsurprising as the closure of
(B,\,D(-A))
is the operator
B
with
full domain. As a consequence,
(\widetilde{S}(t))_{t\geq 0}=(S(t))_{t\geq 0}

by
[
8
, II. 1.4, p. 51]
. More importantly, we obtain
the formula

S(t)u=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(R(\tfrac{t}{n})T(-\tfrac{t}{n})\right)^{n}u

for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
t\geq 0.
It now follows similarly to the first
part of the proof that
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a positive semigroup, i.e.
B

generates a positive semigroup.
∎

Corollary 3.18
.

The group
(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A+B,\,D(A))
is
positive if and only if
B
is a diagonal matrix.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.14
, the group
(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is
positive if and only if the two semigroups
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
and

(R_{-}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, which are generated by
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
and
(A-B,\,D(A))
respectively, are positive semigroups.
Theorem

3.17

implies that this is the case if and only if the diagonal entries of
B
and

-B
are non-negative, i.e. iff
B
is a diagonal matrix.
∎

Definition 3.19
.

We stay that
y\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
is associated to conservation if the quantity

\langle y,\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\in\mathbb{C}

is independent of
t\geq 0
for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
The model is called mass
conserving if
(1,\cdots,1)^{T}
is associated to conservation.

Remark 3.20
.

Mass conservation essentially means

\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{j}(t,\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{0}(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}

for all
t>0
, the solution
u
of

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

and “any” initial value
u_{0}.
This definition via the PDE has the
disadvantage that one needs to specify an appropriate set of admissible initial
functions, whereas the semigroup is defined on the whole space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

We now characterize all vectors
y\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
associated to conservation.
Interestingly enough, the proof relies on the structure of the semigroup and
does not use
\tfrac{d}{dt}R(t)u=(-A+B)R(t)u
for
u\in D(-A).

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=e^{tB}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}

holds for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
t\geq 0.

Proof.

The two key ingredients to this observation are

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(T(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,dx

(29)

and

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(e^{tB}u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=e^{tB}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}

(30)

for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
t\geq 0.
They follow from the independence of
integrals of periodic functions w.r.t. translations and the definition of the
operator
e^{tB}
as a pointwise matrix multiplication. The equations
(
29
) and (
30
) imply

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\big((T(\tfrac{t}{n})e^{t/n\,B})^{n}u\big)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\big(e^{t/n\,B}(T(\tfrac{t}{n})e^{t/n\,B})^{n-1}u\big)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}e^{t/n\,B}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\big((T(\tfrac{t}{n})e^{t/n\,B})^{n-1}u\big)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=e^{tB}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}.

for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
t\geq 0
by an application of property
(v)
from

Theorem

3.6
and a simple induction.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

If
B
is a stable matrix, i.e.
\sigma(B)\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{-}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,\real(\lambda)<0\}
, the above lemma shows that the averages of the
solution converge to
0
.

Theorem 3.23
.

A vector
y\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
is associated to conservation if and only if
B^{T}y=0.

Proof.

``\impliedby"
Let
y\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
with
B^{T}y=0
and let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
We can
use
Lemma

3.21
to compute

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\langle y,\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\langle y,\,e^{tB}\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}=\langle y,\,Be^{tB}\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}

\displaystyle=\langle B^{T}y,\,e^{tB}\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}=0

for
t>0.
The continuity of
t\mapstochar\rightarrow R(t)u
on
[0,\infty)
yields

\langle y,\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}=\langle y,\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}

(31)

for all
t\geq 0.

``\implies"
Let
y\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
be associated to conservation, i.e.

\langle y,\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}=\langle y,\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}

holds for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
t\geq 0.
Differentiating with respect to
t

implies

\langle B^{T}y,\,e^{tB}\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}=0

for all
t>0
because of the computation done in
``\impliedby"
and the fact
that the right-hand side is independent of
t
. Letting
t\searrow 0
results in

\langle B^{T}y,\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}=0.

Finally, taking
u\equiv\overline{z}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
yields
\langle B^{T}y,\,z\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}=0
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Chapter 4
Spectral Analysis of the Transport-Reaction Semigroup

As always, we assume that the dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the number of components

N\in\mathbb{N}
, the transport directions
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
1\leq p<\infty
are arbitrarily chosen fixed parameters, if not
stated otherwise. We work on the Banach space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with norm

\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
, which was defined in (
4
).

Our long-term goal is to study the stability of solutions of the
transport-reaction model (
2
). Let us recall from the
introduction of
Chapter

3
that the linearized
equation reads as

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

for an a priori arbitrary matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}.
In

Chapter

3
, we considered the abstract Cauchy problem

\dot{u}(t)+(A-B)u(t)=0

(32)

on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, where
(-A,\,D(-A))
is the generator of the transport semigroup
introduced in
Chapter

2
and
B
is the bounded linear
operator on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
induced by the matrix
B
. Notice that we
slightly abuse notation and again use the letter
B
for the matrix
and
the
operator. Distinction between both of them will always follow within the context
of usage.

Semigroup and spectral theory offer a great tool to study stability properties
both qualitatively and quantitatively with precise convergence rates. The most
important definitions and results we will apply can be found in Appendix

D
, including additional references. Roughly
speaking, if
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup generated by
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
,
certain spectral properties of the operators
R(t)
for
t\geq 0
imply
stability. It is then natural to try to find a connection between the spectrum
of the generator
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
and the spectrum of
R(t)
for
t\geq 0.

This connection does indeed exist via the spectral mapping theorem

``\quad\sigma(R(t))=e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}\quad".

(33)

Standard results in the literature write
\sigma(R(t))\backslash\{0\}
in
(
33
). But we have seen that

(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
extends to a strongly continuous group, which implies
0\in\rho(R(t))
for all
t\in\mathbb{R},
see
Lemma

3.14
.
However, the equation is put in quotation marks as it does not always hold. In
our case, the PDE is hyperbolic and the semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
does not
have powerful properties like analyticity. It is in general not even eventually
norm continuous and one cannot apply
Corollary

D.12
. In fact, (
33
)
fails for the transport semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
and the best one can hope
for is a weaker version of (
33
), the so
called weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}

(34)

The objective of the whole chapter is to show
(
34
) for the transport-reaction
semigroups
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Unfortunately, we often need to
restrict ourselves to the cases
p=2
or
d=1.

This chapter is structured as follows: we start by performing a complete
spectral analysis of the transport semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
for
d=1
. These
computations mainly serve as a motivating example and show that
(
33
) cannot be expected to hold for the
transport-reaction semigroups. Beyond that, the computations indicate that a
weak spectral mapping theorem should hold.

We continue by characterizing the spectrum of the generator
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
for arbitrary dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Afterwards, we study the spectra of the
generators
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
for dimension
d=1
and arbitrary
1\leq p<\infty
. It may seem counter intuitive to start this way, since these results
are insufficient to perform a stability analysis for the model without knowing
that (
34
) holds. However, a great understanding of the spectrum of the generator for all
1\leq p<\infty
is
required to extend a weak spectral mapping theorem from
p=2
to different
p
.
In particular, independence of
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
of
p
plays a crucial
role. When studying the spectrum for
p\neq 2
, we assume
d=1
because in this case,
the generator has compact resolvent if the transport directions are
non-vanishing. This implies that the spectrum consists only of eigenvalues. For
higher dimensions, we show that
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
does not have
compact resolvent if one component
j
is transport periodic, see

Definition

2.12
. Rigorously proving stability results then
turns into a difficult task for
p\neq 2
and
d\geq 2
. A computation of the
eigenvalues of
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
could yield an incomplete picture without
further work on the problem
\sigma(-A+B)=\sigma_{p}(-A+B)
.

We finally show (
34
) for
p=2

and all dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
. For
d=1,
we can partially extend the result to
other
p
by using the main ideas from
[
25
]
. More precisely,
we show that the weak spectral mapping theorem all
1\leq p<\infty

is basically equivalent to the weak spectral mapping theorem for
p=1
, cf.
corollary

4.27
for the precise statement. In particular, (
34
) extends to all
1\leq p<\infty
if the group
(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is positive but unfortunately, the model
is quite unspectacular if
B
is a diagonal matrix, cf.
Corollary

3.18
.

We conjecture that (
34
) holds for
all dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
and all
1\leq p<\infty
but proving this seems to be
a difficult task.

2
Spectral Analysis of the Transport Semigroup

The next example is a complete spectral analysis in the non-reacting case in dimension

d=1
and with non-vanishing transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
. It shows
that one cannot expect (
33
) to hold,
even in the most simplest scenario. Essentially, the example we formulate is a
detailed and generalized version of
[
8
, IV. Example 2.6 (iv), p. 254]
and
[
34
, Example 4.12, p. 100]
. The
main ideas are already presented there.

Example 4.1

(
N=1
)
.

Let us consider
d=1,\,N=1
and
B=0
with non-vanishing transport direction

v\neq 0.
In this case, we have

-Au=-vu^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad(T(t)u)(x)=u(x-tv)

with
D(-A)=W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T})
by
Corollary

2.11
.
Clearly, the semigroup is periodic with period
\tau=1/|v|.
By the spectral
inclusion
Theorem

D.11
, we obtain

e^{\tau\sigma(-A)}\subseteq\sigma(T(\tau))=\sigma(I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})})=\{1\}

and thus
\sigma(-A)\subseteq 2\pi iv\mathbb{Z}.
In fact, there holds equality:
for
\lambda\in 2\pi iv\mathbb{Z}
we define the function

u(x)=e^{-\tfrac{\lambda}{v}x}.

Then

-Au=-vu^{\prime}=\lambda u

and
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T})
as it fulfills the periodic boundary conditions. That is, we have shown

\sigma(-A)=\sigma_{p}(-A)=2\pi iv\mathbb{Z}

explicitly.

In the following, we compute the spectrum of
T(t)
for
t\geq 0.
To this end,
let us introduce some notation. For a rational number
p/q\in\mathbb{Q}
, we choose

p\in\mathbb{Z},\,q\in\mathbb{N}
and define the
q
-th roots of unit
\Gamma_{q}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,z^{q}=1\}.
The goal is to show

\sigma(T(t))=\begin{cases}\mathbb{S}^{1}\qquad tv\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q},\\
\Gamma_{q}\qquad tv=\frac{p}{q}\text{ with }p\text{ and }q\text{
coprime}\end{cases}.

Recall that
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
defines a group of isometries and hence

\displaystyle r(T(t))=\max\{|\lambda|\,\colon\,\lambda\in\sigma(T(t))\}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|T(t)^{n}\|^{\frac{1}{n}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|T(nt)\|^{\frac{1}{n}}=1,

(35)

where
r(T(t))
is the spectral radius of
T(t),
see

Definition

D.3
, and the second equality follows from
[
35
, Theorem 1.16, p. 7]
. Equation (
35
) holds for
all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and we particularly obtain

r(T(t)^{-1})=r(T(-t))=1.

Now, note that boundedness and invertibility of
T(t)
implies

\sigma(T(-t))=\frac{1}{\sigma(T(t))}.

Therefore, we also get
