1=r(T(-t))=\max\{|\lambda|\,\colon\,\lambda\in\sigma(T(-t))\}=\max\left\{\tfrac{1}{|\lambda|}\,\colon\,\lambda\in\sigma(T(t))\right\},

(36)

so (
35
) and (
36
) imply
\sigma(T(t))\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{1}
for all
t\geq 0.

At this point, we again use the spectral inclusion
Theorem

D.11
to deduce

e^{t\sigma(-A)}\subseteq\sigma(T(t))

(37)

for all
t\geq 0.
Recall
\sigma(-A)=2\pi iv\mathbb{Z}
. In the case
tv\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q},
it is well known and relatively easy to see that

e^{t2\pi iv\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{1}

is a dense inclusion. By the closedness of spectra, it follows

\mathbb{S}^{1}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A)}}\subseteq\overline{\sigma(T(t))}=\sigma(T(t))\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{1}.

In the second case, let
tv=\tfrac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q}
for coprime
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and

q\in\mathbb{N}.
We can use the spectral mapping theorem
[
35
, Theorem 5.3, p. 84]
for bounded linear operators and the holomorphic map

z\mapstochar\rightarrow z^{q}
to obtain

\sigma(T(t))^{q}=\sigma\big(T(\tfrac{p}{qv})\big)^{q}=\sigma\big(T(\tfrac{p}{qv})^{q}\big)=\sigma\big(T(\tfrac{1}{v})^{p}\big)=\sigma(T(\sign(v)\tau)^{p})=\sigma(I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})})=\{1\},

i.e.
\sigma(T(t))\subseteq\Gamma_{q}.
On the other hand,

\Gamma_{q}=e^{2\pi i\tfrac{p}{q}\mathbb{Z}}=e^{t2\pi iv\mathbb{Z}}=e^{t\sigma(-A)}\subseteq\sigma(T(t))

by (
37
) and the fact that
p
and
q
are
coprime (
e^{2\pi ip/q}
is a primitive
q
-th root if and only if
p
and
q

are coprime, which is a classical result from Algebra).

Remark 4.2
.

The result on the spectrum of
T(t)
for
t\geq 0
is an interesting result in
itself. It gives precise information on the solvability of a class of difference
equations on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}),
i.e. solvability in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
of the equation

\lambda u(x)-u(x-tv)=\big((\lambda-T(t))u\big)(x)=v(x)\quad\text{ for
almost all }x\in\mathbb{T},

given
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and
v\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}).
Note that also the question of
uniqueness of the solution can be answered precisely due to
\sigma_{p}(T(t))=e^{t\sigma_{p}(-A)}=e^{t\sigma(-A)}
by
Theorem

D.10
.

Example 4.3

(arbitrary
N\in\mathbb{N}
)
.

The result from
Example

4.1
can be naturally
extended to the case
d=1,\,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
B=0
with non-vanishing transport
directions
v_{j}\neq 0
for
j=1,\cdots,N.
That is,
N
species move
along a circle without reacting with each other. We have

-Au=-\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u_{N}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\quad(T(t)u)(x)=\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(x-tv_{1})\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}(x-tv_{N})\end{pmatrix}

with
D(-A)=W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

Corollary

2.11
. Both the generator and the
semigroup act on the
N
different components independently and therefore, we get

\sigma(-A)=\sigma_{p}(-A)=2\pi i\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\end{subarray}}^{N}v_{j}\mathbb{Z}

and

\sigma(T(t))=\begin{cases}\mathbb{S}^{1}&\exists j=1,\cdots,N\text{ with }tv_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q}\\
\cup_{j=1}^{N}\Gamma_{q_{j}}&tv_{j}=\tfrac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\text{ with }\,p_{j}\text{ and }q_{j}\text{ coprime}\end{cases}.

(38)

If we additionally assume that the semigroup is periodic, the first condition
in (
38
) reduces to a condition on
tv_{1}
because

tv_{j}
is irrational any
j=1,\cdots,N
if and only if
tv_{1}
is irrational, see
Lemma

2.16
.

Let us emphasize that the above computation verify the weak spectral mapping
theorem

\sigma(T(t))=\begin{cases}\begin{cases}\mathbb{S}^{1}&\exists j=1,\cdots,N\colon tv_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q}\\
\cup_{j=1}^{N}\Gamma_{q_{j}}&tv_{j}=\tfrac{p_{j}}{q_{j}}\text{ with }\,p_{j}\text{ and }q_{j}\text{
coprime}\end{cases}\end{cases}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}\overline{e^{t2\pi iv_{j}\mathbb{Z}}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A)}}

and show that the spectral mapping theorem
(
33
) does not hold. The equality in
(
33
) fails for all
t\geq 0
such that
there exists
j=1,\cdots,N
with
tv_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q}.
In
addition, we see that the spectrum of the transport semigroup and its generator
are independent of
1\leq p<\infty.

3
Spectral Analysis of the Generator

As mentioned in the introduction of the chapter, we start with a
characterization of
\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})
for all dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
. The key
observation, which will also be crucial for the proof of the weak
spectral mapping theorem, is that Fourier transformation turns linear differential
operators acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
matrix multiplication
operators
on
l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, the space of the Fourier coefficients. Then, we
can use existing theory of matrix multiplication operators from

[
21
]
and obtain a complete characterization of

\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})
in terms of roots of polynomials of degree
N
.

Consider the Fourier transformation
\mathcal{F}\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with

(\mathcal{F}u)(k)=\hat{u}(k)\qquad\text{ for }k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

(39)

where
\hat{u}(k)=(\hat{u}_{1}(k),\cdots,\hat{u}_{N}(k))^{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
are the unique Fourier coefficients

\hat{u}(k)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(x)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}\,d\mathbf{x}

with

u(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}.

(40)

It is well known that the Fourier transformation defines an isometric
isomorphism and that the Fourier series (
40
) of
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
converges to
u
in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
norm, see

[
11
, Theorem 8.20, p. 248]
. In particular, equation

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|u(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\hat{u}(k)|^{2}

(41)

is called Parseval’s identity.

For our application, it is of great importance that Fourier series of

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
functions are very well-behaved: for any
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
m\in\mathbb{N},
there exists a constant

C=C(d,m,u)>0
such that

|\hat{u}(k)|\leq C(1+|k|)^{-m}

(42)

holds, see
[
15
, Theorem 3.3.3, p. 196]
. Moreover, for

u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
the Fourier series (
40
)
converges to
u
in the
C^{\infty}
norm by
[
32
, Inversion theorem for

C^{\infty}
5.4, p. 72]
, i.e. all derivatives of the Fourier series of
u
converge uniformly to the derivatives of
u
.

At a first glance, the connection of our problem to the abstract setting in

[
21
]
is not evident. We recall the setting from the
paper to make it clear and comprehensible.

Let
(X,\,\Sigma,\,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space, let
1\leq p<\infty
and define
E\coloneqq L^{p}(X,\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Let
q\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
be a measurable function.

Definition 4.4

(
[
21
, Definition 1]
)
.

An operator
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\,D(\mathcal{A}_{q}))
defined on
L^{p}(X,\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

by
\mathcal{A}_{q}\colon f\mapstochar\rightarrow qf,
i.e.

\mathcal{A}_{q}f(x)=q(x)f(x)

(43)

for
x\in X
and for all
f\in D(\mathcal{A}_{q})=\left\{f\in L^{p}(X,\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,qf\in L^{p}(X,\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\right\}
is called matrix
multiplication operator.

Proposition 4.5

(
[
21
, Proposition 1]
)
.

If
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\,D(\mathcal{A}_{q}))
is a matrix multiplication operator
on
L^{p}(X,\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with non-void resolvent set
\rho(\mathcal{A}_{q})
, its
spectrum is given by

\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{q})=\bigcap_{q^{\prime}\in[q]}\overline{\bigcup_{x\in X}\sigma(q^{\prime}(x))}.

(44)

Here,
[q]
is the equivalence class of all measurable functions
\mu
-a.e.
equal to
q
.

In our application, we take
(X,\Sigma,\,\mu)=(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}),\,\#)
, where
\#
is the counting measure and
p=2
. Then
L^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{C}^{N})=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,q\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
and
(
43
) reduces to

\mathcal{A}_{q}f(k)=q(k)f(k)

(45)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
f\in D(\mathcal{A}_{q})=\left\{f\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,qf\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\right\}.
Secondly,
(
44
) comes down to

\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{q})=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sigma(q(k))}

(46)

because functions on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
coinciding
\#
-a.e. are equal. Notice that

q(k)
is a matrix and its spectrum is simply given by the roots of

\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow\det(\lambda I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}-q(k))
.

The following lemma formalizes the above mentioned idea and can for
instance be applied to every reasonable linear differential operator on

L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
in order to compute its spectrum.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\,D(\mathcal{A}))
be a densely defined and closed operator
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with non-void resolvent set
\rho(\mathcal{A})
. Assume that there exists a function
q\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
such that
\mathcal{F}(D(\mathcal{A}))=D(\mathcal{A}_{q})
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathcal{A}_{q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{-1}}

commutes for its induced matrix multiplication operator
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\,D(\mathcal{A}_{q})).
Then it holds

\sigma(\mathcal{A})=\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{q})=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sigma(q(k))}.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.
By assumption,

\lambda I_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\mathcal{A}\colon D(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

is invertible if and only if

\mathcal{F}\big(\lambda I_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\mathcal{A}\big)\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\lambda I_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\mathcal{A}_{q}\colon D(\mathcal{A}_{q})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

is invertible. The second equality follows from
(
46
).
∎

Before applying
Proposition

4.6
to the generator
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
of the transport-reaction semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
, we need to
characterize the domain
D(-A_{2})
and the application of
-A_{2}+B_{2}
in terms of Fourier coefficients. Let us start with the latter.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
be the generator of the transport-reaction
semigroup
(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Then

\big((-A_{2}+B_{2})u\big)(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}

holds for all
u\in D(-A_{2})
, where
M(k)\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
are the matrices defined by

M(k)\coloneqq-2\pi i\begin{pmatrix}k\cdot\mathbf{v_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&k\cdot\mathbf{v_{N}}\end{pmatrix}+B\eqqcolon-2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)+B.

(47)

Proof.

The first step is to consider
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Using
Lemma

2.5
, a calculation shows

((-A_{2}+B_{2})u)(\mathbf{x})=-\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}(\mathbf{x})\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}(\mathbf{x})\end{pmatrix}+Bu(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}.

(48)

Every technicality and convergence of the Fourier series works out nicely because the Fourier series of
u
converges to
u
in the
C^{\infty}
norm, see (
42
) and its subsequent comment.

Now, take an arbitrary function
u\in D(-A_{2}).
By
Corollary

3.7
, there exists a sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
u^{(n)}
converges to
u
in the graph norm of
-A_{2}+B_{2}.
Let
v\coloneqq(-A_{2}+B_{2})u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with

v(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{v}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}.

Parseval’s identity (
41
) and (
48
) imply that the convergence of
u^{(n)}
to
u
in the graph norm is equivalent to

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\hat{u^{(n)}}(k)-\hat{u}(k)|^{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0

and

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|M(k)\hat{u^{(n)}}(k)-\hat{v}(k)|^{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0

as
n\rightarrow\infty.
In particular, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{u^{(n)}}(k^{*})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\hat{u}(k^{*})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad M(k^{*})\hat{u^{(n)}}(k^{*})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\hat{v}(k^{*})

as
n\rightarrow\infty
for every fixed
k^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},
which implies
\hat{v}(k^{*})=M(k^{*})\hat{u}(k^{*}).
Since
k^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
was chosen arbitrarily, we arrive at

((-A_{2}+B_{2})u)(\mathbf{x})=v(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{v}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}.

∎

With the knowledge from
Proposition

4.7
, it is easy to characterize the domain
D(-A_{2}+B_{2})=D(-A_{2})
in terms of Fourier coefficients.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
be the generator of the transport-reaction
semigroup
(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Then it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(D(-A_{2}))

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\hat{u}\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|M(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}<\infty\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\hat{u}\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\mathbf{V}(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}<\infty\bigg\},

where
M(k)
and
\mathbf{V}(k)\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
are the matrices given by

M(k)=-2\pi i\begin{pmatrix}k\cdot\mathbf{v_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&k\cdot\mathbf{v_{N}}\end{pmatrix}+B=-2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)+B.

(49)

Proof.

If
u\in D(-A_{2}),
the function
(-A_{2}+B_{2})u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is given by

\big((-A_{2}+B_{2})u\big)(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}

(50)

due to
Proposition

4.7
. Therefore, Parseval’s identity (
41
) yields

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|M(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}<\infty.

Conversely, let
\hat{u}\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|M(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}<\infty.

(51)

We need to show that the function
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
given by

u(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}

lies in the domain
D(-A_{2})
of the generator
-A_{2}+B_{2}.
To this end, consider the sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined by

u^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\sum_{|k|\leq n}\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}.

Notice that
u^{(n)}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
converges to
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
by Parseval’s identity (
41
). Moreover,

\big((-A_{2}+B_{2})u^{(n)}\big)(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{|k|\leq n}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
due to (
51
) and Parseval’s identity (
41
). This yields
u\in D(-A_{2})
by the closedness of
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2})),
which is a consequence of the Hille-Yosida
Theorem

B.6
.

The second equality in the proposition is an immediate consequence of the triangle inequality in
l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

∎

Remark 4.9
.

Complementary to
Theorem

2.18
,
Proposition

4.8
shows that the domain
D(-A_{2})
is in general a larger subspace than
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for dimensions
d\geq 2,
even if the transport directions
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are non-vanishing.

Finally, we can rigorously characterize the spectrum of the generator of the transport-reaction semigroup
(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
be the generator of the transport-reaction
semigroup
(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Then it holds

\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sigma(M(k))},

where
M(k)\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
is the matrix

M(k)=-2\pi i\begin{pmatrix}k\cdot\mathbf{v_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&k\cdot\mathbf{v_{N}}\end{pmatrix}+B=-2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)+B.

(52)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{M},\,D(\mathcal{A}_{M}))
be the matrix multiplication
operator on
l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
induced by
M=(k\mapstochar\rightarrow M(k))
(in the sense of
Definition

4.4
). Notice that
Proposition

4.8
shows
\mathcal{F}(D(-A_{2}))=D(\mathcal{A}_{M})
. Furthermore, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(-A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathcal{A}_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-A_{2}+B_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{-1}}

commutes by
Proposition

4.7
. The resolvent set
\rho(-A_{2}+B_{2})
is non-empty by the Hille-Yosida
Theorem

B.6
and all properties together imply that the assumptions of
Proposition

4.6
are fulfilled. An application of
Proposition

4.6
finishes the proof.
∎

A simple observation in form of the next lemma shows that the spectrum
\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})
is symmetric to the real line.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and let
M(k)\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
be the matrix

M(k)=-2\pi i\begin{pmatrix}k\cdot\mathbf{v_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&k\cdot\mathbf{v_{N}}\end{pmatrix}+B=-2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)+B.

Then

\sigma(M(-k))=\overline{\sigma(M(k))}

holds for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},
where the overline denotes complex conjugation.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of

M(-k)=\overline{M(k)}.

∎

Carrying over the characterization of
\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})
from
p=2
to
1\leq p<\infty
in all dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
seems to be a very difficult task. In the following, we mostly restrict ourselves to the one dimensional case
d=1
and assume that the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
are non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0.
All proofs rely on the fact that
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
has compact resolvent in these simple cases. Our assumptions are necessary for this approach, since
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
generally fails to have compact resolvent if one transport direction vanishes or if the model is considered in higher dimensions.

Let us recall that we have
D(-A_{p})=W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
under the assumptions
d=1
and non-vanishing transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
by
Corollary

2.11
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty.
Let
d=1
and let the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
be non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
. Then the generator
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
of
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
has
compact resolvent, i.e.
R(\lambda,\,-A_{p}+B_{p})
is compact for one (and hence all)

\lambda\in\rho(-A_{p}+B_{p}).

In particular, the following properties hold true:

(i)

\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})=\sigma_{p}(-A_{p}+B_{p})
contains at
most countably many eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}.

(ii)

If
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
is infinite, then
|\lambda_{j}|\rightarrow\infty
as

j\rightarrow\infty.

(iii)

For all
\lambda\in\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p}),
the operator
\lambda_{j}I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-(-A_{p}+B_{p})
has closed range and
\emph{dim}\ker(\lambda_{j}I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-(-A_{p}+B_{p}))=\emph{codim}\range(\lambda_{j}I_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-(-A_{p}+B_{p}))<\infty.

Proof.

We start by showing that the generator has compact resolvent. By

[
8
, II. 4.25, p. 117]
, it is equivalent to show that
the canonical embedding

(W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,\|\cdot\|_{-A+B})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})})

is compact. As the graph norms of
(-A+B,\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
(-A,\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
are
equivalent by
Theorem

3.6
, it is sufficient to
show compactness of the embedding

(W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,\|\cdot\|_{-A})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}).

Notice that the proof of
Corollary

2.11
also implies equivalence of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{-A}
and
\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
on

W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
The claim now follows from the classical
Rellich-Kondrachov embedding

W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\subset L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),

see
[
3
, Theorem 9.16, p. 285]
. Properties
(i)
to
(iii)
are a consequence of
[
35
, Theorem 2.16, p. 27]
because the spectrum
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
is non-empty. The easiest way to see is is to take an eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
of
B
with eigenvector
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\backslash\{0\}.
Then,
u\equiv z\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
satisfies
(-A_{p}+B_{p})u\equiv Bz=\lambda z=\lambda u.

∎

Remark 4.13
.

Below, it will become important that the theorem also holds true for the generator considered on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with
D(-A_{\infty})=W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
from
Proposition

3.16
. This follows from

W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
due to

W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{N})\subseteq W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\subset C(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, where
p>1
can be chosen arbitrarily.

The assumption of non-vanishing transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
cannot be dropped. By definition (
6
) of the domain of the
generator, there are no restrictions on
u_{j}
if
v_{j}=0
and the
component could be an arbitrary function in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}).
Consequently, the domain of the operator would no longer be compactly embedded in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Concerning higher dimensions
d\geq 2
, we observed in
Theorem

2.18
that the domain
of the generator of the semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is less regular if one component
j
is transport periodic in the sense of
Definition

2.12
. This suggests that the generator also fails to have compact resolvent. The next lemma shows this negative result.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty.
Let
d\geq 2
and let the transport directions

\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}
be non-vanishing, i.e.
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\neq 0
. Assume that one component

j
is transport periodic. Then the generator
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
of

(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
does not have compact resolvent.

Proof.

We show that the embedding

(D(-A),\,\|\cdot\|_{-A})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}).

is not compact. This is equivalent to our statement by
[
8
, II.
4.25, p. 117]
and the equivalence of the graph norms
\|\cdot\|_{-A+B}
and
\|\cdot\|_{-A}
, which was shown in
Theorem

3.6
.

To this end, we construct a bounded sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(D(-A),\,\|\cdot\|_{-A})
, which fails to have a convergent subsequence in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
The
idea is very similar to the proof of

Theorem

2.18
. Let
\mathbf{c}=(\frac{1}{2},\cdots,\frac{1}{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be the center of the torus.
By assumption, the curve

\gamma\colon[0,\tau_{j}]\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{d}\quad\text{with}\quad\gamma(t)\coloneqq\mathbf{c}+t\mathbf{v_{j}}

fulfills
\gamma(0)=\gamma(\tau_{j}).
Let
K=\range(\gamma)
be the range of

\gamma
and consider the sets
U^{(n)}
given by

U^{(n)}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\,\colon\,\dist(\mathbf{x},\,K)<\tfrac{1}{n}\}.

We define

u_{j}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\begin{cases}n^{(d-1)/p}&\mathbf{x}\in U^{(n)},\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and
u_{i}^{(n)}\equiv 0
for all

i=1,\cdots,N
with
i\neq j.
Notice that
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with

\|u^{(n)}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{p}=n^{d-1}\tau_{j}|\mathbf{v_{j}}|\frac{\pi^{(d-1)/2}}{\Gamma(\tfrac{d+1}{2})}n^{1-d}=\tau_{j}|\mathbf{v_{j}}|\frac{\pi^{(d-1)/2}}{\Gamma(\tfrac{d+1}{2})}

(53)

for sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The exact formula is a consequence of the volume of the
(d-1)
-dimensional Euclidean ball with radius
1/n
. In addition,
u_{j}^{(n)}
is constant along direction
\mathbf{v_{j}},
i.e.

u_{j}^{(n)}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}^{(n)}(\cdot)\equiv 0.

This implies
u^{(n)}\in D(-A)
with
-Au^{(n)}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and boundedness of the sequence in
(D(-A),\,\|\cdot\|_{-A}).

On the other hand,
u^{(n)}\rightarrow 0
pointwise almost everywhere because of
d\geq 2
. If a
subsequence of
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converged in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, a subsubsequence would converge to
0
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. This contradicts
(
53
).
∎

Remark 4.15
.

We refer to
Theorem

2.18
and
Figure

1
for an illustration of the idea.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty.
Let
d=1
and let the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
be non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0.
Let
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
be the generator of the transport-reaction semigroup
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Then it holds

\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})=\sigma_{p}(-A_{p}+B_{p})=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))},

where
M(k)\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
is the matrix

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&v_{N}\end{pmatrix}+B\eqqcolon-2\pi ikV+B.

In particular, the spectrum is independent of
p
.

Proof.

Let us recall

(-A_{p}+B_{p})u=-\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u_{N}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}+Bu

from
Corollary

2.11
. We have

{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A_{p}+B_{p}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}}

(54)

for all
1\leq p,\,p^{\prime}\leq\infty
by
Corollary

2.11
. It should be
noted that we also defined the generator for
p=\infty
with
D(-A_{\infty}+B_{\infty})=W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
in
(
26
) and (
27
), cf.
Proposition

3.16
. The proof includes this case because we apply an argument with the adjoints of the
generators in Step 3. In fact, our proof shows that the theorem is also valid for

p=\infty
but we do not need this result in the sequel.

Step 1:
\sigma(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})\subseteq\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
for all
1\leq p\leq p^{\prime}\leq\infty.

Let
1\leq p\leq p^{\prime}\leq\infty
and let
\lambda\in\sigma(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}}).

We know
\sigma(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})=\sigma_{p}(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})
from

Theorem

4.12
and

Remark

4.13
. Hence,
\lambda
is
an eigenvalue and there exists
u\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\backslash\{0\}
with

(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})u=\lambda u
. The circle
\mathbb{T}
has finite mass, so we have
W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subseteq W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and consequently

(-A_{p}+B_{p})u=(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})u=\lambda u

by (
54
). In words,
\lambda
is also an eigenvalue of
-A_{p}+B_{p}.

Step 2:
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})=\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})
for all
2\leq p\leq\infty.

Let
2\leq p\leq\infty.
One direction follows from Step 1. It is left to
show
\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})\subseteq\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p}).

Theorem

4.10
implies

\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))}

where

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&v_{N}\end{pmatrix}+B=-2\pi ikV+B.

The spectrum
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
is closed and therefore, we only need
to prove
\sigma(M(k))\subseteq\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}.
Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
\lambda\in\sigma(M(k)).
Then there
exists
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\backslash\{0\}
with
M(k)z=\lambda z
and we define

u\coloneqq ze^{2\pi ikx}.

Clearly,
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\,u\neq 0
and (
54
), together with
Proposition

4.7
, implies

(-A_{p}+B_{p})u=(-A_{2}+B_{2})u=M(k)ze^{2\pi ikx}=\lambda ze^{2\pi ikx}=\lambda u.

We obtain
\lambda\in\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
and Step 2 follows.

Step 3:
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})=\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})
for all
1\leq p\leq 2.

Let
1\leq p\leq 2.
The whole idea is to apply the classical result that
taking the adjoint does not change the spectrum; see for example
[
35
, Theorem
1.24, p. 12]
. Let
q
be the dual exponent of
p
. We
have seen
(-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*}=A_{q}+B_{q}^{T}
in

Proposition

3.15
and

Proposition

3.16
for the cases
1<p\leq 2
and
p=1
respectively. For
1\leq p\leq 2,
the dual
exponent fulfills
2\leq q\leq\infty
and an application of Step 2 yields

\displaystyle\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})=\sigma((-A_{p}+B_{p})^{*})=\sigma(A_{q}+B_{q}^{T})=\sigma(A_{2}+B_{2}^{T})=\sigma((A_{2}+B_{2}^{T})^{*})=\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2}).

Notice that the adjoint generators are still of the class covered throughout the whole chapter, since the different sign for
A
just changes the direction of movement and
B^{T}
is still a real
N\times N
matrix.
∎

We continue our study of the spectrum of the generator in
Chapter

7
, where we analyze the asymptotics of the eigenvalues of
M(k)
as
|k|\rightarrow\infty.
The results from
Chapter

7
give deep insights into the qualitative behavior of solutions to (
18
).

4
Weak Spectral Mapping Theorems

The idea to use existing theory of matrix multiplication operators is
sufficient to prove a weak spectral mapping theorem for
p=2
in all dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Our strategy is to not only apply
Proposition

4.6
for the generator

(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
of the semigroup, but also for each semigroup operator

R_{2}(t)
itself.

Afterwards, we use the methods from
[
25
]
to demonstrate mathematical tools one can use to extend the result to different
p.
Given that the spectrum
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
is independent of
1\leq p<\infty
, the ideas from
[
25
]
show that the weak spectral mapping theorem for all
p
is essentially equivalent to the weak spectral mapping theorem for
p=1.

In particular, assuming that the
C_{0}
-group
(R_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is positive, abstract theory from
[
2
]
can be used to show a weak spectral mapping theorem for all
1\leq p<\infty
. Unfortunately, the group is positive if and only if
B
is a diagonal matrix, see
Corollary

3.18
.

A weak spectral mapping theorem for
1\leq p<\infty,\,d\in\mathbb{N}
and all matrices
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
remains an open problem.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
. Then

(R_{2}(t)u)(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{tM(k)}\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}

(55)

holds for all
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Here,
M(k)
is the matrix

M(k)=-2\pi i\begin{pmatrix}k\cdot\mathbf{v_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&k\cdot\mathbf{v_{N}}\end{pmatrix}+B=-2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)+B.

(56)

Proof.

Let us recall that a function
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is given by its Fourier series

u(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}},

where
(\hat{u}(k))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
are the unique Fourier coefficients of
u
. Moreover, we have already proven

\big((-A_{2}+B_{2})u\big)(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}

for all
u\in D(-A_{2})
in
Proposition

4.7
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(D(-A_{2}))=\bigg\{\hat{u}\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|M(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}<\infty\bigg\}

(57)

in
Proposition

4.8
. The statement of the proposition is very intuitive but a rigorous justification is a priori unclear and a little technical.

We start by estimating the matrix norm of
e^{tM(k)}.
According to definition,

M(k)=-2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)+B
. Notice that
\mathbf{V}(k)
is a diagonal matrix
with real entries, thus
e^{-t2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)}
defines an isometry for all

t\geq 0.
The Lee-Trotter product formula from

Theorem

C.3
yields

\|e^{tM(k)}\|_{\infty}\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|e^{-t/n\,2\pi i\mathbf{V}(k)}e^{t/n\,B}\|_{\infty}^{n}\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|e^{t/n\,B}\|_{\infty}^{n}\leq e^{t\|B\|_{\infty}}.

(58)

We obtain that the matrix norms of
e^{tM(k)}
are uniformly bounded in
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Consequently,

\bigg\{\hat{u}\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|e^{tM(k)}\hat{u}(k)|^{2}<\infty\bigg\}=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

(59)

and the series in (
55
) is well defined for all
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
We define the linear operators
\widetilde{R}(t)
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
via

(\widetilde{R}(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{tM(k)}\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}

for all
t\geq 0.
Then, (
58
) gives the estimate

\|\widetilde{R}(t)u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq e^{t\|B\|_{\infty}}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

(60)

and the family
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
fulfills the semigroup property. The next step is to show

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\left(\frac{\widetilde{R}(t)u-u}{t}\right)(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot\mathbf{x}}=((-A_{2}+B_{2})u)(\mathbf{x})

(61)

for all
u\in D(-A_{2})
and with convergence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Let us firstly note that the series is well defined by (
57
). Secondly, if (
61
) holds true, (
60
) and density of
D(-A_{2})
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
imply that
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is indeed a strongly continuous semigroup.

We now start with the proof of (
61
). By Parseval’s identity, (
61
) is equivalent to

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\bigg|\bigg(\frac{e^{tM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}}{t}-M(k)\bigg)\hat{u}(k)\bigg|^{2}=0.

The result comes down the question, whether we can interchange the two limits
because

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\sum_{|k|\leq n}\bigg|\bigg(\frac{e^{tM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}}{t}-M(k)\bigg)\hat{u}(k)\bigg|^{2}=0

holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The fundamental theorem of calculus implies

e^{tM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}=t\int_{0}^{1}M(k)e^{stM(k)}\,ds=t\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}e^{stM(k)}\,ds\bigg)M(k)

(62)

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.
The last equality follows from the Riemann definition of the
integral, boundedness of
M(k)
and
M(k)e^{rM(k)}=e^{rM(k)}M(k)
for all
r\geq 0.
As a result, (
62
) and (
58
) show that there is a finite constant
C=C(\|B\|_{\infty},\,1)>0
with

\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg(\frac{e^{tM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}}{t}

\displaystyle-M(k)\bigg)\hat{u}(k)\bigg|^{2}=\bigg|\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}e^{stM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}\,ds\bigg)M(k)\hat{u}(k)\bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\|e^{stM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}\|_{\infty}\,ds\bigg)^{2}|M(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}\leq C|M(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}

for all
t\leq 1
. Finally, this estimate yields

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\searrow 0}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\bigg|\bigg(\frac{e^{tM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}}{t}-M(k)\bigg)\hat{u}(k)\bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{t\searrow 0}\sum_{|k|>n}\bigg|\bigg(\frac{e^{tM(k)}-I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}}{t}-M(k)\bigg)\hat{u}(k)\bigg|^{2}\leq C\sum_{|k|>n}|M(k)\hat{u}(k)|^{2}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}.
For
u\in D(-A_{2}),
the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
implies the
desired result by (
57
).

In summary, we have proven that
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
defines a strongly continuous semigroup with a generator that extends
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
. In particular, the generator of
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
extends
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
Corollary

3.7
implies
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}=(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}.

∎

Remark 4.18
.

Equation (
55
) is an explicit formula of the solution to the abstract
Cauchy problem (
32
) (in a
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

setting).

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
. Then the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{2}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})}}\quad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

holds.

Proof.

Let
t\geq 0.

Proposition

4.17
allows us to apply the same methods we used for
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2}))
to
R_{2}(t)
. Let us check the assumptions of
Proposition

4.6
for
(R_{2}(t),\,L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and the matrix multiplication operator
(\mathcal{A}_{e^{tM}},\,D(\mathcal{A}_{e^{tM}}))
on
l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
induced by
e^{tM}\coloneqq(k\mapstochar\rightarrow e^{tM(k)}).

Firstly, we have seen
D(\mathcal{A}_{e^{tM}})=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
in
(
59
) and
\mathcal{F}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
obviously holds true. Secondly, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(R_{2}(t))=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathcal{A}_{e^{tM}})=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{e^{tM}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{-1}}

commutes by
Proposition

4.17
and, last but not least, the resolvent set
\rho(R_{2}(t))
is non-empty because
R_{2}(t)
is bounded. An
application of
Proposition

4.6
implies

\sigma(R_{2}(t))=\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{e^{tM}})=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sigma(e^{tM(k)})}.

The operators
M(k)
are just matrices, so we obtain

\sigma(e^{tM(k)})=e^{t\sigma(M(k))}

by
[
35
, Theorem 5.3, p. 84]
. Now, we apply
Theorem

4.10
and conclude

\displaystyle\sigma(R_{2}(t))

\displaystyle=\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{e^{tM}})=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(e^{tM(k)})}=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{t\sigma(M(k))}}\subseteq\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{t\overline{\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(l))}}}

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{t\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))}}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})}}.

The other direction is the easy one, which follows from the spectral inclusion theorem

\sigma(R_{2}(t))\supseteq e^{t\sigma(-A_{2}+B_{2})},

see
Theorem

D.11
, and the closedness of

\sigma(R_{2}(t))
.

For
t\leq 0,
the result can be shown with
Lemma

3.14
and
an application of the weak spectral mapping theorem to the semigroup

(R_{2}(-t))_{t\geq 0}.

∎

We now follow
[
25
]
and present methods to extend the weak spectral mapping
theorem to other
1\leq p<\infty
. We present the ideas in detail and adapt the
proofs to our system of PDEs, if necessary. For the rest of the chapter, we will assume

Assumption 1
.

(i)

The spectrum
\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})
of the generator of the transport-reaction semigroup
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is independent of
1\leq p<\infty.

(ii)

The spectrum
\sigma(A_{p}+B_{p}^{T})
of
(A_{p}+B_{p}^{T},\,D(A_{p}))
is independent of
1\leq p<\infty.

Remarks 4.20
.

(i)

Under
1
(i),
Proposition

3.15
almost implies
1
(ii) because of

\sigma(A_{p}+B_{p}^{T})=\sigma(-A_{q}+B_{q})

for all
1<p<\infty
with dual exponent
q
. Here, we used that the spectrum is unchanged when taking the adjoint.

(ii)

The operator
(A_{p}+B_{p}^{T},\,D(A_{p}))
is still of the class covered in this thesis. The change of sign for
A_{p}
just changes the direction of movement and
B_{p}^{T}
is a real
N\times N
matrix.

This assumption is of course only fulfilled in the case
d=1
with non-vanishing transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0,
see
Theorem

4.16
. Nevertheless, we decided to formulate all results under
1
instead of restricting ourselves to the case
d=1
. The reason for this is to emphasize the independence of the arguments of the spacial dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

Let us start by formulating some technicalities.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
(R_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the transport-reaction group on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
. The following properties hold true for all
1\leq p<p^{\prime}<\infty
and all
t\in\mathbb{R}:

(i)

D(-A_{p}^{\prime})\subseteq D(-A_{p}),

(ii)

{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A_{p}+B_{p}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{D(-A_{p}^{\prime})}}=-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}}
,

(iii)

{\left.\kern-1.2ptR_{p}(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}}=R_{p^{\prime}}(t).

Under
1
(i), it also holds

(iv)

{\left.\kern-1.2ptR(\lambda,\,-A_{p}+B_{p})\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}}=R(\lambda,\,-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})
for all
\lambda\in\rho(-A+B).

Proof.

We start by showing
(i)
and
(ii)
at once. On
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
the generators coincide by
Lemma

2.5
and the definition of
B
as a pointwise matrix multiplication. Now, let
u\in D(-A_{p}^{\prime})
. By
Corollary

3.7
, there exists a sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with
u^{(n)}\rightarrow u
in the graph norm of
(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}},\,D(-A_{p^{\prime}})).
We also assumed
p<p^{\prime},
so
u^{(n)}
also converges to
u
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
((-A_{p}+B_{p})u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
This yields

(-A_{p}+B_{p})u^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})u

in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and the closedness of
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p})),
see
Theorem

B.6
, implies
u\in D(-A_{p})
with
(-A_{p}+B_{p})u=(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})u.

Property
(iii)
follows from the Dyson-Phillips series in
Theorem

3.6
and the fact that the transport groups
(T_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
(T_{p^{\prime}}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

coincide on
L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Concerning property
(iv),
notice that
1
(i) justifies the notation
\rho(-A+B)
used above. Let
\lambda\in\rho(-A+B)
. For
v\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subseteq L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}),
there exist unique
u_{p}\coloneqq R(\lambda,\,-A_{p}+B_{p})v\in D(-A_{p})
and
u_{p^{\prime}}\coloneqq R(\lambda,\,-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})v\in D(-A_{p^{\prime}})
such that

\big(\lambda-(-A_{p}+B_{p})\big)u_{p}=v=\big(\lambda-(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})\big)u_{p^{\prime}}

holds. Property
(ii)
yields

\big(\lambda-(-A_{p}+B_{p})\big)u_{p^{\prime}}=\big(\lambda-(-A_{p^{\prime}}+B_{p^{\prime}})\big)u_{p^{\prime}}=v,

which implies
u_{p^{\prime}}=u_{p}
by the injectivity of
\lambda-(-A_{p}+B_{p})
.
∎

Before we show equivalence of the weak spectral mapping theorem for all
1\leq p<\infty
to two weak spectral mapping theorems for
p=1
(under
1
), we state the two key theorems used in the proof.

For
1\leq p\leq 2,
the idea is to use an interpolation argument based on the Riesz-Thorin
theorem. Adapted to our application, the Riesz-Thorin theorem on a
\sigma
-finite measure space
(X,\Sigma,\mu)
reads as follows.

Theorem 4.22

(
[
26
, Theorem 3.16, p. 35]
)
.

Suppose that
S\colon L^{1}(X,\,\mathbb{C})+L^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow L^{1}(X,\,\mathbb{C})+L^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{C})
is a linear operator such that

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2ptS\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{L^{1}(X,\mathbb{C})}}

\displaystyle\colon L^{1}(X,\,\mathbb{C})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{1}(X,\,\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2ptS\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{L^{2}(X,\mathbb{C})}}

\displaystyle\colon L^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{C})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{C})

are bounded with norms
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
respectively. Then for
0<\theta<1,

S
defines a bounded linear operator

S\colon L^{p}(X,\,\mathbb{C})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{p}(X,\mathbb{C})

where

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1-\theta}{1}+\frac{\theta}{2}=1-\frac{\theta}{2}

(63)

and
\|S\|_{L^{p}(X,\mathbb{C})}\leq C_{1}^{1-\theta}C_{2}^{\theta}.

Remark 4.23
.

For
1\leq p\leq 2,
it holds
L^{p}(X,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subseteq L^{1}(X,\mathbb{C}^{N})+L^{2}(X,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, so it makes sense to consider
S
as an operator on
L^{p}(X,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

The Riesz-Thorin theorem is going to be applied in the setting of the next result.

Corollary 4.24

(
[
16
, Corollary 1.2, p. 208]
)
.

Let
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a semigroup on a Banach space
(E,\,\|\cdot\|_{E})
with generator

(\mathcal{A},\,D(\mathcal{A})).
Let
t>0.
Then the following assertions are
equivalent:

(i)

e^{\lambda t}\in\rho(R(t))

(ii)

\lambda+\tfrac{2\pi i}{t}\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\rho(\mathcal{A})
and the sequence
of operators

S^{(n)}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\sum_{k=-m}^{m}R\big(\lambda+\tfrac{2\pi i}{t}k,\,\mathcal{A}\big)

is bounded in
\mathcal{L}(E).

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
(R_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the transport-reaction group on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
. Under
1
(i), the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{p}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

for all
1\leq p\leq 2
is equivalent to the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{1}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{1}+B_{1})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

for
p=1.

Proof.

Let us assume that the weak spectral mapping theorem holds for
p=1
and let
1<p<2.
For
p=2,
the weak spectral mapping theorem holds by
Theorem

4.19
. Due to the spectral inclusion
Theorem

D.11
and the closedness of spectra, we only have to prove the inclusion

\sigma(R_{p}(t))\subseteq\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}.
Let
t>0.
The goal is to show

\Big(\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\Big)^{c}\subseteq\rho(R_{p}(t)).

To this end, let
\mu\in\big(\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\big)^{c}.
The semigroup operator
R_{p}(t)
is invertible with inverse
R_{p}(-t)
, so we can assume
\mu\neq 0
without loss of generality. In this case, there exists
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mu=e^{\lambda t}
and if we can show property
(ii)
from
Corollary

4.24
, then we are done.

Regarding the first condition of
(ii)
in
Corollary

4.24
, notice that
\mu=e^{\lambda t}\in\rho(R_{2}(t))
by
Theorem

4.19
and the assumed independence of
p
for the
spectrum of the generator.
Corollary

4.24
implies

\lambda+\tfrac{2\pi i}{t}\mathbb{Z}\subset\rho(-A_{2}+B_{2})=\rho(-A+B)=\rho(-A_{p}+B_{p}).

It is left to show that the sequence of operators
(S^{(n)}_{p})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined by

S^{(n)}_{p}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\sum_{k=-m}^{m}R\big(\lambda+\tfrac{2\pi i}{t}k,\,-A_{p}+B_{p}\big)

is bounded in

\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})).
From
\mu\in\rho(R_{1}(t))=\rho(R_{2}(t))
and

Corollary

4.24
, we already know that
(S^{(n)}_{1})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(S^{(n)}_{2})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are bounded sequences in
\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))

and
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
respectively. The idea is now to apply the
Riesz-Thorin
Theorem

4.22
. Notice that there exists
\theta\in(0,1)
such that (
63
) holds. Originally,
Theorem

4.22
is formulated for complex valued operators but it is easy to get around this restriction by identifying
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\{1,\cdots,N\},\,\mathbb{C})
through the canonical isomorphism

\Phi\colon L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\{1,\cdots,N\},\,\mathbb{C})

with

u\longmapsto\big[(\mathbf{x},j)\mapstochar\rightarrow u_{j}(\mathbf{x})\big].

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and define the operator

\displaystyle S^{(n)}\colon L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N})+L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})+L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\longmapsto S^{(n)}_{1}u_{1}+S^{(n)}_{2}u_{2}

for
u=u_{1}+u_{2}
with
u_{1}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
u_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Let us show that this map is well defined. To this end, assume

u=u_{1}+u_{2}=v_{1}+v_{2}

for
u_{1},\,v_{1}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
u_{2},\,v_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
We
obtain

u_{1}-v_{1}=v_{2}-u_{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Hence,
Lemma

4.21

(iv)
and the definitions
of
S^{(n)}_{1}
and
S^{(n)}_{2}
imply

\displaystyle S^{(n)}_{1}u_{1}+S^{(n)}_{2}u_{2}

\displaystyle=S^{(n)}_{1}v_{1}+S^{(n)}_{2}v_{2}+S^{(n)}_{1}(u_{1}-v_{1})+S^{(n)}_{2}(u_{2}-v_{2})

\displaystyle=S^{(n)}_{1}v_{1}+S^{(n)}_{2}v_{2}+S^{(n)}_{2}(u_{1}-v_{1})-S^{(n)}_{2}(v_{2}-u_{2})

\displaystyle=S^{(n)}_{1}v_{1}+S^{(n)}_{2}v_{2}.

Theorem

4.22
implies that
S^{(n)}(p)\coloneqq{\left.\kern-1.2ptS^{(n)}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}}
fulfills

\|S^{(n)}(p)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\leq\|S^{(n)}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N}))}^{1-\theta}\|S^{(n)}_{2}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}^{\theta}\eqqcolon C_{1,n}^{1-\theta}C_{2,n}^{\theta}.

(64)

Furthermore, we have

{\left.\kern-1.2ptS^{(n)}(p)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}}=S^{(n)}_{2}={\left.\kern-1.2ptS^{(n)}_{p}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}}

by
Lemma

4.21

(iv)
. Density of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and continuity of and
S^{(n)}(p)
and
S^{(n)}_{p}

respectively imply
S^{(n)}(p)=S^{(n)}_{p}
, which finally results in

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|S^{(n)}_{p}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}C_{1,n}^{1-\theta}\,\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}C_{2,n}^{\theta}<\infty

by (
64
) and the boundedness of
(S^{(n)}_{1})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(S^{(n)}_{2})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))

and
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
respectively. This shows the weak spectral mapping theorem for
1<p<2
and all
t\geq 0
.

For
t<0,
the statement immediately follows from
Lemma

3.14
.
∎

Theorem 4.26
.

Let
(R_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the transport-reaction group on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
. Under
1
, the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{p}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

for all
1\leq p<\infty
is equivalent to the two weak spectral mapping theorems

\displaystyle\sigma(R_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{1}+B_{1})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\sigma(\widetilde{R}_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{t\sigma(A_{1}+B_{1}^{T})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(A+B^{T})}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

for
p=1,
where
(\widetilde{R}_{1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(A_{1}+B_{1}^{T},\,D(A_{1})).

Proof.

For
1\leq p\leq 2,
the result simply follows from
Proposition

4.25
. Let
2<p<\infty
and let
t\geq 0.
The
adjoint semigroup of
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup

(\widetilde{R}_{q}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by the adjoint of
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
, namely
(A_{q}+B_{q}^{T},\,D(A_{q})),
see
Proposition

3.15
. Here,
1<q<2
is the
dual exponent of
p
and the adjoint semigroup and generator are still of the class covered by
Proposition

4.25
, since the different sign for
A
just changes the direction of movement and
B^{T}
is still a real
N\times N
matrix. Hence, we can use
1
(ii) to apply
Proposition

4.25
to the adjoint semigroup. We obtain

\sigma(R_{p}(t))=\sigma(\widetilde{R}_{q}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(A_{q}+B_{q}^{T})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}.

As in the proof of
Proposition

4.25
, the statement for
t<0
follows from
Lemma

3.14
.
∎

Unfortunately, proving
1
for arbitrary dimensions
d\in\mathbb{N}
and showing weak spectral mapping theorems for
p=1
are difficult tasks. Nevertheless, we can prove some partial results.

Corollary 4.27
.

Let
d=1
and let the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
be non-vanishing. Let
(R_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the transport-reaction group on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
. Then the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{p}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

for all
1\leq p<\infty
is equivalent to to the two weak spectral mapping theorems

\displaystyle\sigma(R_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{1}+B_{1})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\sigma(\widetilde{R}_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{t\sigma(A_{1}+B_{1}^{T})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(A+B^{T})}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

for
p=1,
where
(\widetilde{R}_{1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(A_{1}+B_{1}^{T},\,D(A_{1})).

Proof.

Given
d=1
and
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0,

1
is fulfilled by
Theorem

4.16
and we can apply
Theorem

4.26
.
∎

In particular, the weak spectral mapping theorem holds for all
1\leq p<\infty
in the case that
(R_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
defines a positive group on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with non-vanishing transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
. This happens if and only if
B
is a diagonal matrix, see
Corollary

3.18
, and it is a consequence of the following abstract result by Arendt an Greiner. Again,
(X,\Sigma,\mu)
is assumed to be a
\sigma
-finite measure space.

Theorem 4.28

(
[
2
, Corollary 1.4, p. 300]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\,D(\mathcal{A}))
be the generator of a strongly
continuous group
(R(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of positive operators on
L^{1}(X,\mu).
Then
the weak spectral mapping theorem holds, i.e.

\sigma(R(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(\mathcal{A})}}\quad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}.

Corollary 4.29
.

Let
(R_{1}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the transport-reaction group on
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{1}+B_{1},\,D(-A_{1}))
. Assume that
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is a diagonal matrix. Then the two weak spectral mapping theorems

\displaystyle\sigma(R_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{1}+B_{1})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\sigma(\widetilde{R}_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{t\sigma(A_{1}+B_{1}^{T})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(A+B^{T})}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

hold, where
(\widetilde{R}_{1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(A_{1}+B_{1}^{T},\,D(A_{1})).

Proof.

Given a diagonal matrix
B
, the groups
(R_{1}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
(\widetilde{R}_{1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
are positive due to
Corollary

3.18
. Now the result is a direct consequence of
Theorem

4.28
. Technically, we should pause for a moment before simply applying
Theorem

4.28
. The above theorem only holds for positive groups on
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C})
, but we consider positive groups on

L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
However, this subtlety causes no problem whatsoever because the canonical mapping

\Phi\colon L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\{1,\cdots,N\},\mathbb{C})

with

u\longmapsto\big[(\mathbf{x},j)\mapstochar\rightarrow u_{j}(\mathbf{x})\big]

defines an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 4.30
.

Let
d=1
and let the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
be non-vanishing. Let
(R_{p}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the transport-reaction group on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
and assume that
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is a diagonal matrix. Then the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{p}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{p}+B_{p})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B)}}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}

holds for all
1\leq p<\infty
.

Proof.

Combine
Corollary

4.27
and
Corollary

4.29
.
∎

At this point, we should critically reflect on
Corollary

4.30
. If
B
is a diagonal matrix
D
with diagonal entries
d_{1},\cdots,d_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
, the transport-reaction model (
32
) reads

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u^{\prime}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u^{\prime}_{N}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
d_{N}u_{N}\end{pmatrix}.

There is no interaction and one can directly compute the solution. A short
computation (use e.g.
Theorem

3.6
(v) for the
rigorous proof) shows

R(t)=e^{tD}T(t)\eqqcolon\begin{pmatrix}e^{d_{1}t}T_{1}(t)\\
\vdots\\
e^{d_{N}t}T_{N}(t)\end{pmatrix}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, where
(T(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is the transport group on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
defined in (
5
) and
Remark

2.8
.
Together with the computations from
Example

4.1
and

Example

4.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma(R_{p}(t))

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}e^{d_{j}t}\sigma(T_{j}(t))=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}e^{d_{j}t}\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{j})}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}\overline{e^{t(d_{j}+\sigma(-A_{j}))}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A_{j}+d_{j})}}

\displaystyle=\overline{e^{\cup_{j=1}^{N}t\sigma(-A_{j}+d_{j})}}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+D)}},

where we used the notation
-A_{j}\coloneqq-v_{j}\partial_{x}
for the
j
-th component of

-A.
This shows that it is actually possible to prove
Corollary

4.30
with explicit computations.

After considering
[
25
, Theorem 3.2, Theorem 3.3, p. 1049]
and

[
27
]
, a natural idea would be to split the reaction matrix

B
into its diagonal entries and its strictly lower and upper triangular
matrix, i.e. to write
B=D+(L+U).
If
(U_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup
generated by
-A_{p}+D_{p}
and
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup generated by

-A_{p}+B_{p},
compactness of
R_{p}(t)-U_{p}(t)
would imply that the the essential
spectra of the semigroup operators
U_{p}(t)
and
R_{p}(t)
coincide for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. This would allow us to prove a weak spectral mapping theorem in dimension
d=1
with non-vanishing transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
for all
1\leq p<\infty
, cf.
[
25
, Theorem 3.3, p. 1049]
. Unfortunately, the next example illustrates that this procedure does not work in the case of discrete values for the velocity.

Example 4.31
.

Consider
d=1,\,N=2
and the system

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}u=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}u.

Then,
D=I_{\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}}
and the equations can be solved explicitly. We
obtain

\displaystyle U(t)u

\displaystyle=e^{t}T(t)u=e^{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(\cdot-t)\\
u_{2}(\cdot-t)\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle R(t)u

\displaystyle=e^{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(\cdot-t)+u_{2}(\cdot-t)t\\
u_{2}(\cdot-t)\end{pmatrix}.

Consequently,

(R(t)-U(t))u=te^{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{2}(\cdot-t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}

and the difference of the semigroup operators is not compact for
t\neq 0
because the
unit ball in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
is not.

Chapter 5
Arbitrary Side Lengths and Neumann Boundary Conditions

So far, we considered the transport-reaction models on the domain
[0,1]^{d}
with
periodic boundary conditions, i.e. the domain
\mathbb{T}^{d}
defined in

Chapter

1
. An easy scaling argument in the spacial variable
can be used to see that the assumption of side length
1
of the torus can be
made without loss of generality in terms of mathematical treatment - models on

\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}\coloneqq L\mathbb{T}^{d}
for
L>0
can be studied completely analogously.
Nevertheless, the side length can affect stability and pattern formation
properties of the transport-reaction model and for this reason, it often makes
sense to incorporate this additional parameter in concrete examples.

Secondly, we describe the connection of “symmetric” models with appropriate
Neumann boundary conditions on
[0,L]
to models with periodic boundary
conditions on
[0,2L].
A precise mathematical explanation of what we mean by
that is given at the beginning of the second section of this chapter.

5
Arbitrary Side Lengths

Consider a side length
L>0
, the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}=L\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
recall that the transport-reaction equation for
N\in\mathbb{N}
particle groups reads

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=Bu,

(65)

where
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are the transport directions
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
describes linear reactions.

Chapter

3
dealt with the question of well-posedness
of (
65
) on
[0,1]^{d}
with periodic boundary
conditions, which corresponds to the choice of
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
as the
appropriate Banach space. Recall that the operator defined by

(-A_{p}+B_{p})u=-\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}+Bu

(66)

and with domain
D(-A_{p}+B_{p})=D(-A_{p})
studied in
Chapter

2

generates the transport-reaction semigroup
(R_{p}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
from

Theorem

3.6
.

For this section, it is more important to stress the dependence on
L
and on the
directions
\mathbf{v}\coloneqq(\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}),
rather
than to stress the dependence on
p
. Consequently, we use the notation

(-A+B)^{L,\mathbf{v}}
for the operator (
66
) on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
with domain

D(-A^{L,\mathbf{v}})=\left\{u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})\,\colon\,\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{u_{j}(\cdot-t\mathbf{v_{j}})-u_{j}(\cdot)}{t}\text{ exists in }L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})\text{ for all }j=1,\cdots,N\right\}.

and
(R^{L,\mathbf{v}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
for its generated semigroup. It should
be noted that this operator is indeed a generator. This follows similarly to

Chapter

2
and
Chapter

3
by
defining the transport semigroup on
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}
, see
(
5
), and using the perturbation argument from

Theorem

3.6
. If
L=1
, we drop the side length in the notation and
write
(-A+B)^{\mathbf{v}}
or
(R^{\mathbf{v}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. To
achieve rigor and conciseness, we also introduce the scaling operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\colon L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

(67)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\mathbf{x}\mapstochar\rightarrow u(L\mathbf{x}))

with inverse
\mathcal{S}^{-1}.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty,\,d\in\mathbb{N},\,N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
L>0
be the side length of the torus. Moreover, let
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be arbitrary transport directions. Then

R^{L,\mathbf{v}}(t)=\mathcal{S}^{-1}R^{\mathbf{v}/L}(t)\mathcal{S}

(68)

holds for all
t\geq 0
. In particular, we obtain the following properties:

(i)

\sigma(R^{L,\mathbf{v}}(t))=\sigma(R^{\mathbf{v}/L}(t))\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 0,

(ii)

D(-A^{L,\mathbf{v}})=\mathcal{S}^{-1}D(-A^{\mathbf{v}/L})=\mathcal{S}^{-1}D(-A^{\mathbf{v}}),

(iii)

(-A+B)^{L,\mathbf{v}}=\mathcal{S}^{-1}(-A+B)^{\mathbf{v}/L}\mathcal{S},

(iv)

\sigma((-A+B)^{L,\mathbf{v}})=\sigma((-A+B)^{\mathbf{v}/L}).

Proof.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})
and consider
\widetilde{u}\coloneqq\mathcal{S}u.
Then,
\widetilde{u}(t,\cdot)\coloneqq(R^{\mathbf{v}/L}(t)\widetilde{u})(\cdot)
is the solution of

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{u}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\widetilde{u}_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{L}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})=B\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{u}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\widetilde{u}_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})

on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with initial value
\widetilde{u}(0,\cdot)=\widetilde{u}(\cdot).

The rescaled function
u(t,\cdot)\coloneqq\mathcal{S}^{-1}\widetilde{u}(t,\cdot)
fulfills

u(0,\cdot)=\mathcal{S}^{-1}\widetilde{u}(\cdot)=u(\cdot)
and solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\mathbf{x})=\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{u}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\widetilde{u}_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\frac{\mathbf{x}}{L})+\frac{1}{L}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{N}\end{pmatrix}(t,\frac{\mathbf{x}}{L})

\displaystyle=B\widetilde{u}(t,\frac{\mathbf{x}}{L})=Bu(t,\mathbf{x}).

This shows
R^{L,\mathbf{v}}(t)=\mathcal{S}^{-1}R^{\mathbf{v}/L}(t)\mathcal{S}
on
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})
and density in

L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})
implies (
68
).

The second equality in
(ii)
is a direct consequence of the definition of the domain, see (
6
). All other properties result from
(
68
) and the fact that
\mathcal{S}
and its inverse are both bounded
bijections.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

Property
(i)
and
(iv)
in
Theorem

5.1
are by far the most important one
for applications. In conjunction with a connection of the spectrum of the
semigroup to the generator, for instance
Theorem

4.19
, they allow us to study long-time asymptotics for arbitrary side lengths
L>0
.

6
Symmetric Models with Neumann Boundary Conditions

This section only deals with the one dimensional case
d=1
and we consider the Banach spaces
L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
for
L>0.
Similar to the previous section, the dependence on
1\leq p<\infty
plays a secondary role and our notation does not include this parameter, but stresses the boundary conditions and the length
L>0
of the domain
(0,L).

Many times in applications, for example in
[
29
,
18
,
19
,
17
]
, there are
N
different particles

u_{1},\cdots,u_{N}
, whose density can be split into right and left moving
subgroups
\alpha_{j}
and
\beta_{j}
with
u_{j}=\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}
for
j=1,\cdots,N
. We write

v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}>0
for the speeds of the particles and use the notation

\Gamma\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&v_{N}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\alpha\coloneqq(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})^{T}\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta\coloneqq(\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{N})^{T}.

With this notation, many models studied in the above mentioned literature
read

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&B_{2}\\
B_{2}&B_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}\eqqcolon B\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}

(69)

for some matrices
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}.
The left-hand side of
(
69
) simply transcribes the model assumption
that left and right moving particles have the same speed and the symmetry of
B

is always a consequence of symmetry assumptions on the linear turning behavior
of the particles and on the nonlinearities appearing in the non-linearized
models. Given this setup, it is very natural to assume that particles hitting
the boundary of
[0,L]
are reflected and turn into particles moving in the
other direction. These considerations yield the Neumann boundary conditions

\alpha(t,0)=\beta(t,0)\quad\text{ and }\quad\alpha(t,L)=\beta(t,L)\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

(70)

We show a connection of (
69
)
with Neumann boundary conditions (
70
) on
(0,L)
to
(
69
) with periodic boundary conditions on

(0,2L).
This connection has already been pointed out in
[
28
, Lemma 2, p. 242]
and in the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 4.5, p. 18]
for a Goldstein-Kac model, which is probably the most
famous example of an equation of type (
69
); see

Section

7
for the model description. We show that the idea
from
[
20
, Theorem 4.5, p. 18]
carries over to the general
case by using (without proof) that

(-A+B)^{\text{Neu},\,L}\coloneqq-\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}+B

(71)

with domain

D((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},\,L})=\bigg\{(\alpha,\,\beta)^{T}\in W^{1,p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})\,\colon\,\alpha(0)=\beta(0)\text{ and }\alpha(L)=\beta(L)\bigg\}

(72)

generates a strongly continuous semigroup on
L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
, which we
call
(R^{\text{Neu},L}(t))_{t\geq 0}.
This statement seems very reasonable
and it should not be too difficult to prove it with, for example, the
Lumer-Phillips
Theorem

B.9
and
the same perturbation argument as in
Theorem

3.6
.

To make the connection to periodic boundary conditions precise, we introduce the
extension operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\colon L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{p}((0,2L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})

(73)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}

with

\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=\begin{cases}\alpha(x)&x\in(0,L)\\
\beta(2L-x)&x\in(L,2L)\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\beta}(x)=\begin{cases}\beta(x)&x\in(0,L)\\
\alpha(2L-x)&x\in(L,2L)\end{cases}.

Furthermore, we introduce the notation
(-A+B)^{\text{per},L}
for the
operator defined in (
71
) with domain

D((-A+B)^{\text{per},\,L})=\bigg\{(\alpha,\,\beta)^{T}\in W^{1,p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})\,\colon\,\alpha(0)=\alpha(L)\text{ and }\beta(0)=\beta(L)\bigg\}.

(74)

It should be noted that
(-A+B)^{\text{per},L}
is the the generator of the transport-reaction semigroup on the circle
\mathbb{T}_{L}
from
Theorem

3.6
. We introduced this alternative
definition solely for notation purposes.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let
L>0
. Moreover, let

(R^{\text{Neu},L}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup on
L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
generated by
(-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L}
and let
(R^{\text{per},2L}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup on
L^{p}((0,2L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
generated by

(-A+B)^{\text{per},2L}
. Then

R^{\text{Neu},L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}={\left.\kern-1.2ptR^{\text{per},2L}(t)\mathcal{E}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{(0,L)}}

holds for all
(\alpha,\,\beta)\in L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
and all

t\geq 0
, where
\mathcal{E}
is the extension operator defined in
(
73
).

Proof.

Let
(\alpha,\beta)\in D((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L})
and define

(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})\coloneqq\mathcal{E}((\alpha,\beta))\in D((-A+B)^{\text{per},2L}).
Notice that the periodic boundary conditions are
fulfilled due to the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,p}((0,L))\subseteq C([0,L])
. Consider the solution

\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(t,\cdot)=R^{\text{per},2L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(\cdot)

(75)

of the model (
69
) on
(0,2L)
with periodic boundary conditions. The next step is
to show that the symmetry induced by the extension
\mathcal{E}
is preserved
for all times. To this end, we define the function

\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\alpha}\\
\widehat{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(t,\cdot)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\beta}\\
\widetilde{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}(t,2L-\cdot),

which fulfills the initial condition

(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\beta})(0,x)=(\widetilde{\beta},\widetilde{\alpha})(0,2L-x)=(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})(0,x)

by definition (
73
) of the extension. In addition,
(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\beta})
fulfills periodic boundary conditions and we
have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\alpha}\\
\widehat{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(t,x)

\displaystyle+\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\alpha}\\
\widehat{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(t,x)=\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\beta}\\
\widetilde{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}(t,2L-x)+\begin{pmatrix}-\Gamma&0\\
0&\Gamma\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\beta}\\
\widetilde{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}(t,2L-x)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}B_{2}&B_{1}\\
B_{1}&B_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(t,2L-x)=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&B_{2}\\
B_{2}&B_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\alpha}\\
\widehat{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(t,x)

almost everywhere. Uniqueness of the solution to (
69
) implies
(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\beta})=(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta}),
i.e.

\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(t,\cdot)=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\beta}\\
\widetilde{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}(t,2L-\cdot)

(76)

and, together with the periodic boundary conditions for

(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})
, equation (
76
) particularly
shows

\widetilde{\alpha}(t,0)=\widetilde{\beta}(t,2L)=\widetilde{\beta}(t,0)\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\alpha}(t,L)=\widetilde{\beta}(t,L).

That is, the restriction of
(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})
to

(0,L)
solves (
69
) with the Neumann boundary
conditions (
70
). Recalling (
75
), this means

R^{\text{Neu},L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}={\left.\kern-1.2ptR^{\text{per},2L}(t)\mathcal{E}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[0,L]}}

for all
(\alpha,\beta)\in D((-A_{p}+B_{p})^{\text{Neu},L})
and all
t\geq 0.

Density of the domain of the generator, see
Theorem

B.6
, yields the theorem.
∎

Remarks 5.4
.

(i)

The result simply states that every solution of the Neumann problem on

(0,L)
can be obtained by solving the periodic problem on
(0,2L)
with the
appropriately chosen initial function and subsequently restricting it to the
original interval.

(ii)

This correspondence heavily relies on the fact that right and left moving
particles have the same speed. If the speeds differ, concentrations in the
Neumann model will “overlap”, which is not the case in periodic models (this
illustration refers to
B=0
).

The result clearly justifies our strong focus on periodic boundary conditions.
Nevertheless, it would be desirable to characterize the spectrum of the
generator
(-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L}
and its corresponding semigroup

(R^{\text{Neu},L}(t))_{t\geq 0}.
Without any additional effort, we can only
state the following result.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and
L>0
. Assume that the transport directions

v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
are non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0
. Moreover, let
(R^{\text{Neu},L}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup on
L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
generated by
(-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L}
and let
(R^{\text{per},2L}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be
the semigroup on
L^{p}((0,2L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
generated by

(-A+B)^{\text{per},2L}
. Then the following properties hold true:

(i)

\sigma((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L})=\sigma_{p}((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L}),

(ii)

\sigma((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L})\subseteq\sigma((-A+B)^{\text{per},2L}),

(iii)

\sigma_{p}(R^{\text{Neu},L}(t))\subseteq\sigma_{p}(R^{\text{per},2L}(t))\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

Proof.

(i)

This follows similarly to the proof of

Theorem

4.12
and every
property mentioned there also holds for the generator with Neumann boundary
conditions. Everything works out nicely due to the compact embedding

D((-A+B)^{\text{Neu,L}})\subset\subset L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N}),

which is a consequence of the assumption of non-vanishing transport
directions. To see that spectrum is non-empty, let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
be an eigenvalue of
B_{1}+B_{2}
with eigenvector
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\backslash\{0\}
and define the constant function
(\alpha,\beta)\equiv(z,z).
Then,
(\alpha,\beta)
fulfills the boundary conditions
\alpha(0)=\beta(0),\,\alpha(L)=\beta(L)
and satisfies

(-A+B)^{\text{Neu},\,L}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&B_{2}\\
B_{2}&B_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z\\
z\end{pmatrix}=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}.

(ii)

Let
\lambda\in\sigma((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L}).
We know from
(i)
that

\lambda
is an eigenvalue, i.e. there exists
(\alpha,\beta)\in D((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L})\backslash\{0\}
with

-\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&B_{2}\\
B_{2}&B_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}.

(77)

The extension
(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})\coloneqq\mathcal{E}((\alpha,\beta))
fulfills periodic boundary conditions and for

x\in(L,2L)
and we can use (
77
) to obtain

\displaystyle-\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix}
