\displaystyle\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(x)+\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&B_{2}\\
B_{2}&B_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}(2L-x)+\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&B_{2}\\
B_{2}&B_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}(2L-x)

\displaystyle=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}(2L-x)=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(x)

almost everywhere. Hence,
(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})\in D((-A+B)^{\text{per},2L})
is an eigenfunction of

(-A+B)^{\text{per},2L}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
.

(iii)

Let
t\geq 0
and
\lambda\in\sigma_{p}(R^{\text{Neu},L}(t))
with
eigenfunction
(\alpha,\beta)\in L^{p}((0,L),\mathbb{C}^{2N}).
Consider the extension

(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})=\mathcal{E}((\alpha,\beta))\in L^{p}((0,2L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N}).

Theorem

5.3
yields

{\left.\kern-1.2ptR^{\text{per},2L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{(0,L)}}=R^{\text{Neu},L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\lambda{\left.\kern-1.2pt\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{(0,L)}}.

(78)

Moreover, density of
D((-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L})
in
L^{p}((0,L),\,\mathbb{C}^{2N})
and (
76
)
implies

R^{\text{per},2L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}\\
I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}&0\end{pmatrix}\bigg(R^{\text{per},2L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(2L-x)\bigg)

almost everywhere on
(0,2L).
Thus, almost every
x\in(L,2L)
fulfills

\displaystyle R^{\text{per},2L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}\\
I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}&0\end{pmatrix}R^{\text{Neu},L}(t)\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}(2L-x)

\displaystyle=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}(2L-x)=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}\\
\widetilde{\beta}\end{pmatrix}(x)

by (
78
) and we obtain
\lambda\in\sigma_{p}(R^{\text{per},2L}(t))
with eigenfunction
(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta}).

∎

Chapter 6
Applications: Long-Time Behavior of Hyperbolic Models

The theory from
Chapter

4
can deal with many existing myxobacteria models on a linearized level. It allows us to study the long-time
behavior of solutions and the results obtained can, for example, be used to verify already existing qualitative stability theorems from the perspective of semigroup theory. In addition, the weak spectral mapping
Theorem

4.19
enables us to prove sharp quantitative estimates.

The methods used do not rely on any symmetry assumption or on the typically used Kac trick
[
22
]
, which allows to reduce symmetric systems of hyperbolic equations to reaction telegraph equations for sufficiently smooth solutions. It has for example been used in
[
18
,
29
,
7
]
and we refer
to these references and
[
20
]
for any details on the trick.

We show an optimal convergence result for a classical Goldstein-Kac model, apply
our theory to models “with killing” and end with an application to a more
delicate model from
[
18
]
.

7
A Goldstein-Kac Model

In
[
29
]
, the authors proposed a myxobacteria model of
Goldstein-Kac type. They focused on a system with
N=2
bacteria groups and
their equation reads

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}v&0\\
0&-v\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-(\mu+\eta_{1})&\mu+\eta_{2}\\
\mu+\eta_{1}&-(\mu+\eta_{2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(79)

where
v>0
is the speed,
\mu>0
is an autonomous turning rate and

\eta_{j}=\eta_{j}(w_{1},\,w_{2})\geq 0
for
j=1,2
are turning rates, which
depend on the concentrations. Additionally, they assume symmetry with respect to
a change of left and right, i.e.

\eta_{1}(w_{1},w_{2})=\eta_{2}(w_{2},w_{1})\eqqcolon\eta(w_{1},w_{2})

for all
w_{1},\,w_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.
System (
79
) has the
stationary states
w_{1}=w_{2}\equiv c
for arbitrary
c\in\mathbb{R}.
We define
u_{1}\coloneqq w_{1}-c,\,u_{2}\coloneqq w_{2}-c
and linearize with Taylor to obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u

\displaystyle+\begin{pmatrix}v&0\\
0&-v\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}u=\begin{pmatrix}-\mu&\mu\\
\mu&-\mu\end{pmatrix}w+\begin{pmatrix}-\eta(w_{1},w_{2})&\eta(w_{2},w_{1})\\
\eta(w_{1},w_{2})&-\eta(w_{2},w_{1})\end{pmatrix}w

(80)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\mu&\mu\\
\mu&-\mu\end{pmatrix}u+\begin{pmatrix}(\eta(c,c)+\partial_{1}\eta(c,c)c-\partial_{2}\eta(c,c)c)(w_{2}-w_{1})\\
(\eta(c,c)+\partial_{1}\eta(c,c)c-\partial_{2}\eta(c,c)c)(w_{1}-w_{2})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\Lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&-\Lambda\end{pmatrix}u

for
\Lambda\coloneqq\mu+\eta(c,c)+\partial_{1}\eta(c,c)c-\partial_{2}\eta(c,c)c
, cf.
[
29
, (5), p. 624]
.

This linearized problem is well-posed and the solution of the abstract Cauchy
problem with initial function
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
is given by
t\mapstochar\rightarrow R(t)u
, where
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
generated by

-A+B\coloneqq-\begin{pmatrix}v&0\\
0&-v\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}+\begin{pmatrix}-\Lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&-\Lambda\end{pmatrix},

(81)

with domain
H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
see
Theorem

3.6
and
Corollary

2.11
.
Due to
Theorem

4.19
, a stability analysis reduces to a spectral
analysis of the generator. In particular, the growth bound of the semigroup
coincides with the spectral bound of the generator by
Lemma

D.9
. Secondly,
Theorem

4.16

implies

\sigma(-A+B)=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))},

where

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v&0\\
0&-v\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}-\Lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&-\Lambda\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi ikv-\Lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&2\pi ikv-\Lambda\end{pmatrix}.

(82)

Hence, we need to compute the roots of

\displaystyle\det\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi ikv-\Lambda-\lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&2\pi ikv-\Lambda-\lambda\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=(-2\pi ikv-\Lambda-\lambda)(2\pi ikv-\Lambda-\lambda)-\Lambda^{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}+2\Lambda\lambda+4\pi^{2}k^{2}v^{2}.

They are clearly given by

\lambda_{1,2}(k)=-\Lambda\pm\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}k^{2}v^{2}}

and particularly

\sigma(-A+B)=\{-\Lambda\pm\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}k^{2}v^{2}}\,\colon\,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}

(83)

because the union of all eigenvalues is a closed set. It follows immediately that the stationary states are unstable for
\Lambda<0
(consider
k=0
) and it always holds
0\in\sigma(-A+B)
.

The goal is now to show a precise convergence result if we have
\Lambda>0.
Then, every eigenvalue except
0
has negative real part and we essentially need a spectral decomposition of the generator. To this end, let

\displaystyle\{1\}

\displaystyle=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})\,\colon\,u\equiv const\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\},

(84)

\displaystyle\{1\}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})\,\colon\,\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,dx=0\},

such that
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})=\{1\}\oplus\{1\}^{\perp}.
Notice that

E\coloneqq(\{1\}^{\perp},\,\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})})
defines a Banach space.
We consider the part of
(-A+B,\,H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2}))
in
E,
i.e.

{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}u\coloneqq(-A+B)u

with

D({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})\coloneqq\left\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})\,\colon\,\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,dx=0\right\}.

(85)

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
v>0
and let
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}.
Then
({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}},\,D({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}))

generates a strongly continuous semigroup on
E.
This semigroup is given by

({\left.\kern-1.2ptR(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})_{t\geq 0},
i.e. the restriction of
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
to
E.

Moreover, it holds

\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})=\sigma(-A+B)\backslash\{-2\Lambda,0\}=\{-\Lambda\pm\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}k^{2}v^{2}}\,\colon\,k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}\}

Proof.

Firstly, note that
E
is
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
invariant due to
Lemma

3.21
. Secondly,
E
is continuously embedded into
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}).
We find, using
[
8
, II 2.3, p. 60]
, that the restricted
semigroup
({\left.\kern-1.2ptR(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})_{t\geq 0}
has the generator

{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}

with domain

\displaystyle D({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})

\displaystyle=\big\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\cap E\,\colon\,(-A+B)u\in E\big\}=\left\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})\,\colon\,\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,dx=0\right\}.

Here, the second equality follows for example from the
E
-invariance of
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
,
Definition

B.3
of generators and closedness of
E
as a subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

It is left to show the statements on the spectrum. The second equality is a
direct consequence of (
83
) and we start with
the direction
``\supseteq"
for the first equality. If
k\neq 0
and

\lambda\in\sigma(M(k)),
we can define

u(x)\coloneqq ze^{2\pi ikx}\in E,

where
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
is an eigenvector of
M(k)
with eigenvalue
\lambda.

Proposition

4.7
implies
{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}u=\lambda u
and
\lambda\in\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})
follows.

Next, we show the direction
``\subseteq".
Similarly to the proof of
Theorem

4.12
, one can easily see that
(D({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}),\,\|\cdot\|_{-A+B})
is compactly embedded into
E.
This yields

\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})=\sigma_{p}({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}),

since we already know that the spectrum is non-empty from the previously proven
direction. Notice that every eigenvalue of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}
is also an
eigenvalue of
-A+B,
simply by definition. We conclude

\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})\subseteq\sigma(-A+B)

and the final step is to show that neither
-2\Lambda
nor
0
can be eigenvalues
of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}.
We present the proof for
\lambda=-2\Lambda
and argue
by contradiction. The proof for
\lambda=0
is similar. To this end, let
u\in D({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})\backslash\{0\}
and assume

-\begin{pmatrix}v&0\\
0&-v\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u^{\prime}_{1}\\
u^{\prime}_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}-\Lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&-\Lambda\end{pmatrix}u=-2\Lambda\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix},

which is equivalent to

-\begin{pmatrix}v&0\\
0&-v\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u^{\prime}_{1}\\
u^{\prime}_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&\Lambda\end{pmatrix}u=0.

(86)

We obtain
(u_{1}+u_{2})^{\prime}=0,
i.e.
u_{1}\equiv-u_{2}+c
for some constant
c\in\mathbb{C}.

The integral constraint (
85
) implies

c=0
and
u_{1}\equiv-u_{2}.
Plugging this information back into (
86
)
yields
u_{1}=-u_{2}\equiv\widetilde{c}
for a second constant

\widetilde{c}\in\mathbb{C}.
Using again the integral constraint (
86
) gives

u=0,
which contradicts our assumption of
u
being an eigenfunction.
∎

In what follows, the idea is to use stability of the semigroup

({\left.\kern-1.2ptR(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})_{t\geq 0}
on
E
. We would expect stability due to shown
spectral properties of the generator but actually, we still need a weak spectral
mapping theorem for the restricted semigroup to make this connection rigorous,
cf. the introduction of
Chapter

4
and Appendix
D
for a detailed
explanation.

The theory on matrix multiplication operators, see

Definition

4.4
and the subsequent page, can also deal with
this technical problem. Taking
(X,\Sigma,\,\mu)=(\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}),\,\#)
with counting measure
\#
yields

L^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{C}^{2})=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
\mathcal{F}(E)=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
, where
\mathcal{F}
is the Fourier transform
defined in (
39
). Following the proof of

Theorem

4.19
and using exactly the same arguments, we obtain

\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2ptR(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})=\overline{e^{t\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})}}.

(87)

In fact, these abstract arguments with matrix multiplication operators offer an alternative proof for the characterization of
\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})
in

Proposition

6.1
.

Proposition

6.1
decomposes the spectrum of the generator
(-A+B,\,H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2}))
into its stable and central spectrum. Together with (
87
), we are finally able to prove an optimal convergence result for the model (
80
) in the case

\Lambda>0
. For the sake of readability, we define the negative constant

\omega\coloneqq-\Lambda+\real(\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}v^{2}})=\begin{cases}-\Lambda&\Lambda^{2}\leq 4\pi^{2}v^{2},\\
-\Lambda+\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}v^{2}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(88)

before stating the theorem. Notice that
\omega\in[-\Lambda,0)
is the largest
real part of the eigenvalues in the stable spectrum of
-A+B.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
v>0,\,\Lambda>0
and let
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
generated by the operator defined in (
81
). Moreover, let
\omega
be defined as in (
88
). Then, for every
\varepsilon>0,
there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\|R(t)u-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(\omega+\varepsilon)t}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}

(89)

holds for all
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2}).
The estimate (
89
) does not
hold for
\omega+\varepsilon
replaced by any constant
\widetilde{\omega}<\omega.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0.

Step 1: Exponential stability of the restricted semigroup

({\left.\kern-1.2ptR(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})_{t\geq 0}.

Recall that
Proposition

6.1
states

\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})=\{-\Lambda\pm\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}k^{2}v^{2}}\,\colon\,k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}\}.

The eigenvalue with the largest real part is
\lambda\coloneqq-\Lambda+\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}v^{2}}
, i.e.

s({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})=\real(\lambda)=\omega,

where
s({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})
is the spectral bound of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}},
see

Definition

D.4
.
The weak spectral mapping theorem (
87
) yields

\omega_{0}({\left.\kern-1.2pt-A+B\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}})=\omega

for the exponential growth bound of the restricted semigroup, see

Definition

D.4
and

Lemma

D.9
. Hence, there exists a constant

C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\|{\left.\kern-1.2ptR(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(\omega+\varepsilon)t}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}

(90)

holds for all
u\in E.

Step 2: A computation of
e^{tB}.

The matrix

B=\begin{pmatrix}-\Lambda&\Lambda\\
\Lambda&-\Lambda\end{pmatrix}=\Lambda\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}

can be diagonalized with

B=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&-2\Lambda\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&-2\Lambda\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}

and we obtain

e^{tB}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&e^{-2\Lambda t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+e^{-2\Lambda t}&1-e^{-2\Lambda t}\\
1-e^{-2\Lambda t}&1+e^{-2\Lambda t}\end{pmatrix}.

In particular,
\Lambda>0
implies

e^{tB}y-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}+y_{2}\\
y_{1}+y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\Lambda t}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}-y_{2}\\
y_{2}-y_{1}\end{pmatrix}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0

(91)

as
t\rightarrow\infty
and for all
y\in\mathbb{C}^{2}.

Step 3: The convergence result for the full model.

Notice that, if
u\equiv const,
an application of

Proposition

4.17
shows

R(t)u=e^{tM(0)}u=e^{tB}u.

Finally, using (
90
) and (
91
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|R(t)u

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\|R(t)(u-\int_{\mathbb{T}}u)+R(t)\int_{\mathbb{T}}u-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\|{\left.\kern-1.2ptR(t)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E}}(u-\int_{\mathbb{T}}u)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}+\|e^{tB}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(\omega+\varepsilon)t}\|u-\int_{\mathbb{T}}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\Lambda t}\bigg|\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}\end{pmatrix}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(\omega+\varepsilon)t}\bigg(\|u-\int_{\mathbb{T}}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}+\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{1}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{2}\bigg|\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(\omega+\varepsilon)t}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}.

In the last two steps, we used new constants
C_{\varepsilon}
without
relabeling.

Step 4: The estimate does not hold for
\omega+\varepsilon
replaced by

\widetilde{\omega}<\omega
.

To see this claim, notice that
\lambda=-\Lambda+\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-4\pi^{2}v^{2}}
is an eigenvalue of
M(1)
, where
M(1)
was defined in (
82
). We
take an associated eigenvector
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
with
|z|=1
and define

u\coloneqq ze^{2\pi ix}\in E.

Proposition

4.17
implies

(R(t)u)(x)=e^{tM(1)}ze^{2\pi ix}=e^{t\lambda}ze^{2\pi ix}.

Therefore, we have

\|R(t)u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}=|e^{\lambda t}|=e^{\omega t}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}

and the exponential rate of convergence cannot be smaller than
\omega.

∎

Remarks 6.3
.

(i)

Notice that
\sigma(B)=\{-2\Lambda,0\}
. The qualitative behavior, namely
convergence to the mean of the initial value or blow-up, does not change due to the additional spacial variable, cf. (
91
).
This is contrary to the pattern formation phenomena discussed in

Chapter

7

(ii)

One might think that
Theorem

6.2
can be easily obtained
from the exact solution formula given in

Proposition

4.17
, the eigenvalues of
M(k)
and Parseval’s identity. However, this is a
fallacy and incorrect. The matrices
M(k)
are not normal for

k\neq 0
and therefore, it is non-trivial to find estimates of the
operator norms
\|M(k)\|
and
\|e^{tM(k)}\|
in terms of the
eigenvalues of
M(k).

(iii)

Theorem

5.1
allows us to state the corresponding result on

\mathbb{T}_{L}=L\mathbb{T}
for arbitrary length
L>0
of the circle. Let
\varepsilon>0.

Using the properties
(i)
and
(iv)
from
Theorem

5.1
yields the
optimal (up to
\varepsilon
) estimate

\|R^{L,v}(t)u-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}_{L}}u_{1}+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}_{L}}u_{2}\\
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}_{L}}u_{1}+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}_{L}}u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\mathbb{C}^{2})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(\omega_{L}+\varepsilon)t}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}

for all
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{2}),
where
\omega_{L}
is the constant

\omega_{L}\coloneqq-\Lambda+\real(\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-\tfrac{4\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}v^{2}})=\begin{cases}-\Lambda&\Lambda^{2}\leq\tfrac{4\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}v^{2},\\
-\Lambda+\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}-\tfrac{4\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}v^{2}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(iv)

The Goldstein-Kac model (
80
) is an example of a
symmetric model discussed in
Section

6
. Therefore,

Theorem

5.3
can be applied to study the model on

(0,L)
with the Neumann boundary conditions
u_{1}(t,0)=u_{2}(t,0)
and

u_{1}(t,L)=u_{2}(t,L)
for all
t\geq 0.
For all
\varepsilon>0
, we obtain

\|R^{\text{Neu},L}(t)u-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{[0,L]}u_{1}+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{[0,L]}u_{2}\\
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{[0,L]}u_{1}+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{[0,L]}u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\|_{L^{2}([0,L],\mathbb{C}^{2})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(\omega_{2L}+\varepsilon)t}\|u\|_{L^{2}([0,L],\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}.

With Neumann boundary conditions, however, it is unclear whether the
convergence rate is optimal, cf.
Corollary

5.5
.

The visualization of
Theorem

6.2
is intuitive, but also surprising. For small transport speeds
v>0,
the movement of the particles is the factor slowing down the convergence and an increasing speed also increases the rate of convergence towards the equilibrium. Then, for transport faster than the critical velocity
v^{*}=\tfrac{\Lambda}{2\pi}
, a higher speed / faster “stirring” motion does not correspond to a faster rate of convergence and the reactions described by
B
become the factor slowing down the convergence. Somehow, however, the fastest rate is capped by half of the rate of
the single cell model, cf. (
91
).

8
Models with Killing

A second way to apply the theory from
Chapter

4
is to
carry over convergence results to different norms. We illustrate the main idea
with the example of positive and mass conserving models
exposed to killing

in the case
d=1
. As a first step, we briefly discuss what we mean by

killing
and present the most important properties of this artificial
modification of the model. The latter can be done in arbitrary dimension
d
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N},\,N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\delta>0.
The (original) model

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

(92)

on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
and
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is exposed to
\delta
-killing if we modify the model to

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}\nabla u_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\nabla u_{N}\cdot\mathbf{v_{N}}\end{pmatrix}=Bu-\delta u.

(93)

Remarks 6.5
.

(i)

The additional term
-\delta u
simply changes the birth/death rate of the

N
subgroups in a uniform manner.

(ii)

The mathematically rigorous and consistent notation will often be
-\delta I_{E}
for an appropriate Banach space
E
. But we will neglect this subtlety
and always use the notation
-\delta
from above.

Let
1\leq p<\infty.
Recall that the operator

(-A+B)u=-\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}+Bu

with appropriately chosen domain
D(-A)
generates the transport-reaction semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, see
Theorem

3.6
. We refer to
Chapter

2
and
Chapter

3
for
the precise setup and the definition of
D(-A)
. Of course,
B-\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is still a matrix and the whole theory from

Chapter

3
and
Chapter

4
can
still be applied. The appropriate point of view, however, is to see

\delta
-killing as a modification of the original model. The next lemma
clarifies this perturbative effect.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A+B,\,D(-A)).
Moreover, let
\delta>0.
The semigroup
(R_{\delta}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A+B-\delta,\,D(-A))
is given by

R_{\delta}(t)=e^{-\delta t}R(t)

for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
(R_{\delta}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup generated by

(-A+B-\delta,\,D(-A)),
which exists due to

Theorem

3.6
, and let
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}\coloneqq(e^{-\delta t}R(t))_{t\geq 0}
. It is clear that
(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
defines a strongly continuous semigroup. In addition, it holds

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{\widetilde{R}(t)u-u}{t}=e^{-\delta\cdot 0}(-A+B)u-\delta e^{-\delta\cdot 0}u=(-A+B-\delta)u

for all
u\in D(-A)
by the product rule, i.e. the generator of

(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
extends
(-A+B-\delta,\,D(-A)).
However,
(R_{\delta}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the only
C_{0}
-semigroup with a generator that extends
(-A+B-\delta,\,D(-A))
by
Corollary

3.7
, so we obtain
(R_{\delta}(t))_{t\geq 0}=(\widetilde{R}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
∎

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let
\delta>0.
Then

\displaystyle\omega_{0}(-A+B-\delta)

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}(-A+B)-\delta,

(94)

\displaystyle\sigma(-A+B-\delta)

\displaystyle=\sigma(-A+B)-\delta,

(95)

\displaystyle\sigma(R_{\delta}(t))

\displaystyle=e^{-\delta t}\sigma(R(t)),

(96)

where
\omega_{0}(\cdot)
is the growth bound of the respective semigroup, see

Definition

D.4
. In
particular, an exposure to
\delta
-killing turns the model exponentially stable
if and only if
\delta>\omega_{0}(-A+B)
. Secondly, the weak spectral mapping
theorem for the model exposed to
\delta
-killing holds if and only if it holds for the original model.

Remark 6.8
.

The existence of a weak spectral mapping theorem implies
\omega_{0}(-A+B)=s(-A+B)
, see
Lemma

D.9
.

Proof.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}.
There exists
M_{\omega}\geq 1
with

\|R_{\delta}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\leq M_{\omega}e^{\omega t}

for all
t\geq 0
if and only if there exists
M_{\omega}\geq 1
such that

\|R(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\leq M_{\omega}e^{(\omega+\delta)t}

holds for all
t\geq 0
by
Lemma

6.6
. Statement (
94
) can now be shown with the definition of the growth bound, see

Definition

D.4
,
and the respective addendum on exponential stability results from

Proposition

D.8

(iv).

Equations (
95
) and (
96
) are immediate
consequences of the definition of the spectrum and
Lemma

6.6
. Both
equations and a routine computation for sets yield that the weak
spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(R_{\delta}(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A+B-\delta)}}\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 0

holds iff we have

e^{-\delta t}\sigma(R(t))=e^{-\delta t}\overline{e^{t(\sigma(-A+B))}}\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

The latter is true if and only if the weak spectral mapping theorem holds for
the original model.
∎

8.1
Positive and Mass Conserving Models with Killing

Assume that the transport-reaction model is positive and mass conserving in the
sense of
Definition

3.3
and

Definition

3.19
. Both properties have been characterized in

Theorem

3.17
and
Theorem

3.23
respectively.
Together, given
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1,\cdots,N},
they are equivalent to

b_{ij}\geq 0\qquad\text{ for }i,j=1,\cdots,N\quad\text{ with }\quad i\neq j

(97)

and

b_{jj}=-\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}b_{ij}\qquad\text{ for }j=1,\cdots,N.

(98)

Positivity of the semigroup and mass conservation allow us to bound the

L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{N})
norm of the solution with the
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
norm of the
initial function. The next lemma is typical for positive semigroups and is
similar to a computation from
[
34
, p. 106]
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and assume that
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
fulfills (
97
). Let
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the positive transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A+B,\,D(-A))
. Then,

|R(t)u|\leq R(t)|u|

holds for all
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
t\geq 0
, i.e.

|(R(t)u)_{j}(\mathbf{x})|\leq(R(t)|u|)_{j}(\mathbf{x})

holds for all
j=1,\cdots,N
and almost all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Here,
|u|\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is the componentwise absolute value of
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Proof.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.
For
j=1,\cdots,N
, choose

\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{C}
with

|\alpha_{j}|=1
and

\mathbb{R}\ni\alpha_{j}\big((R(t)u)_{j}(\mathbf{x})\big)=(R(t)(\alpha_{j}u))_{j}(\mathbf{x})=(R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u))_{j}(\mathbf{x}).

The second equality follows by decomposing
\alpha_{j}u
into its real
and imaginary part and using that
R(t)
maps real valued functions to real
valued ones. Positivity implies

\displaystyle\pm R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u)

\displaystyle=\pm R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u)_{+}\mp R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u)_{-}\leq R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u)_{+}+R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u)_{-}

\displaystyle=R(t)|\real(\alpha_{j}u)|\leq R(t)|\alpha_{j}u|=R(t)|u|

and therefore
|R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u)|\leq R(t)|u|,

i.e. it holds

|(R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u))_{i}(\mathbf{y})|\leq(R(t)|u|)_{i}(\mathbf{y})

(99)

for all
i=1,\cdots,N
and all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Using this and
|\alpha_{j}|=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle|(R(t)u)_{j}(\mathbf{x})|=|\alpha_{j}\big((R(t)u)_{j}(\mathbf{x})\big)|=|(R(t)\real(\alpha_{j}u))_{j}(\mathbf{x})|\leq(R(t)|u|)_{j}(\mathbf{x})

for all
j=1,\cdots,N
. Notice that this estimate holds for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.
Given an arbitrary function
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, the claim follows by taking an approximating sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
of
u
.
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
p=1
and assume that
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
fulfills (
97
) and (
98
). Let
(R_{1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the positive and mass conserving transport-reaction semigroup on
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{1}+B_{1},\,D(-A_{1}))
. Then,

\|R_{1}(t)u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq\sqrt{N}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

holds for all
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and all
t\geq 0
. Furthermore, we have
\omega_{0}(-A_{1}+B_{1})=0.

Proof.

Let
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
Applying
Lemma

6.9
, positivity and mass conservation yields

\displaystyle\|R(t)u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle=\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|R(t)u|(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)|u|)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1,\cdots,1)^{T}\cdot\int_{T^{d}}(R(t)|u|)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=(1,\cdots,1)^{T}\cdot\int_{T^{d}}|u|(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{N}\,\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|u|(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}\|_{2}=\sqrt{N}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

This estimate particularly shows
\omega_{0}(A_{1}+B_{1})\leq 0.
Equality follows
from
Proposition

D.8

(iv):
if we
had
\omega_{0}(-A_{1}+B_{1})<0,
the semigroup
(R_{1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
would be
exponentially stable and we would obtain

\|R(t)u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0

as
t\rightarrow\infty
for all
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
This would yield a contradiction to mass
conservation for all
u>0
due to

0<(1,\cdots,1)^{T}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=(1,\cdots,1)^{T}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}\leq\sqrt{N}\|R(t)u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\rightarrow 0.

∎

Remark 6.11
.

Neither positivity nor mass conservation alone are sufficient to get a bound as
in
Corollary

6.10
. This is easy to see if we solely assume
positivity. For a diagonal matrix
B=D
with positive diagonal entries, the transport-reaction semigroup is positive and every component grows exponentially. For mass conservation, consider the following example.

Example 6.12
.

Consider the mass conserving equation

\partial_{t}u(t,\mathbf{x})=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix}u(t,\mathbf{x})=Bu(t,\mathbf{x})

on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with some initial function
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
with
u_{1}\neq u_{2}.
The computation of the matrix exponential in step (ii) of

Theorem

6.2
shows that the solution is given by

(R(t)u)(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(\mathbf{x})+u_{2}(\mathbf{x})\\
u_{1}(\mathbf{x})+u_{2}(\mathbf{x})\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}e^{2t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(\mathbf{x})-u_{2}(\mathbf{x})\\
u_{2}(\mathbf{x})-u_{1}(\mathbf{x})\end{pmatrix}

and consequently

\|R(t)u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg(e^{2t}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|u_{1}(\mathbf{x})-u_{2}(\mathbf{x})|\,d\mathbf{x}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|u_{1}(\mathbf{x})+u_{2}(\mathbf{x})|\,d\mathbf{x}\bigg)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\infty

as
t\rightarrow\infty.
A similar blow-up behavior with motion of the particles
included happens in the mass conserving model (
80
) with

\Lambda<0.

The explicit computation
\omega_{0}(-A_{1}+B_{1})=0
in the case of positive and
mass conserving model done in
Corollary

6.10
and

Theorem

6.7
imply that
\delta
-killing
for any
\delta>0
results in exponential stability with respect to the
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
norm. More precisely,
\omega_{0}(-A_{1}+B_{1}-\delta)=-\delta
and

Lemma

6.6
and
Corollary

6.10
show the intuitive
estimate

\|R_{1,\delta}(t)u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,C^{N})}\leq\sqrt{N}e^{-\delta t}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

(100)

for all
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
The result is sharp in the sense that the
convergence rate is optimal.

So far, every result holds true for arbitrary dimension
d
but in the
following, we will assume
d=1
. The weak spectral mapping
Theorem

4.19
and the independence of the spectrum of the generator
(-A_{p}+B_{p},\,D(-A_{p}))
of
1\leq p<\infty
shown in
Theorem

4.16
allow us to expand the convergence result
(
100
) to the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})

norm. In the process, we “lose” an arbitrary small fraction of the convergence
rate.

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
d=1
and assume that
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
fulfills (
97
) and (
98
). Moreover, assume that the transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
are non-vanishing, i.e.
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\neq 0.
Let
(R_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the positive and
mass conversing semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by
(-A_{2}+B_{2},\,D(-A_{2})).
For every
\varepsilon>0,
there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0

such that

\|R_{2,\delta}(t)u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}e^{(-\delta+\varepsilon)t}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

(101)

holds for all
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N}).
The estimate (
101
) does not
hold for
-\delta+\varepsilon
replaced by any constant
\widetilde{\delta}<-\delta.

Proof.

We have already seen

\omega_{0}(-A_{1}+B_{1}-\delta)=-\delta

and it holds

s(-A_{1}+B_{1}-\delta)\leq\omega_{0}(-A_{1}+B_{1}-\delta)=-\delta

because the spectral bound of a semigroup is always dominated by the growth
bound, see
Proposition

D.6
. In fact,
we have equality:
Theorem

4.16
characterizes the spectrum
of the generator, which is independent of
1\leq p<\infty
, and implies

\sigma(-A+B-\delta)\supseteq\sigma(M(0))=\sigma(B-\delta).

Furthermore, the model was assumed to be mass conserving, so we obtain

0\in\sigma(B^{T})=\sigma(B)
by
Theorem

3.23
. Hence, there
exists
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\backslash\{0\}
with
Bz=0
and
(B-\delta)z=-\delta z
,
i.e.
-\delta\in\sigma(-A+B-\delta)
and

s(-A+B-\delta)=-\delta.

Now, the weak spectral mapping
Theorem

4.19
for
p=2
and

Lemma

D.9
result in

\omega_{0}(-A_{2}+B_{2}-\delta)=s(-A+B-\delta)=-\delta

and the theorem follows from the definition of the growth bound.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

If we had a weak spectral mapping theorem for
1\leq p<\infty
, the same
result would be true on
L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

9
A Reaction Random Walk System

In this section, we always assume
d=1
and consider the domain
\mathbb{T}
. The goal
is to apply our theory from
Chapter

4
to the main model
from
[
18
]
with periodic boundary conditions. This is
different to the original paper, where Neumann boundary conditions in the sense
of
Section

6
were studied. Essentially, we give an easier and shorter proof of
[
18
, Lemma 4.1, p. 61 and Theorem 4.3, p. 63]
.

The author of
[
18
]
assumed that the particle densities
u_{1}

and
u_{2}
of two different species can be split into particle densities of right
and left moving particles, i.e.
u_{1}=\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}
and
u_{2}=\alpha_{2}+\beta_{2}.
Each species
j=1,2
has a speed
v_{j}>0
and a
turning rate
\mu_{j}>0
. Moreover, the reactions of the particles are
assumed to be described by a continuously differentiable function
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2},
which depends on the concentrations of the
species
.
Introducing the variables

\displaystyle\Gamma\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&0\\
0&v_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad M\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}&0\\
0&\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix}

and

\alpha\coloneqq(\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})^{T},\qquad\beta\coloneqq(\beta_{1},\,\beta_{2})^{T},\qquad u\coloneqq\alpha+\beta,

the model, cf.
[
18
, (15), p. 54 and (38), p. 58]
, reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\alpha+\Gamma\partial_{x}\alpha

\displaystyle=M(\beta-\alpha)+\tfrac{1}{2}F(\alpha+\beta),

(102)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\beta-\Gamma\partial_{x}\beta

\displaystyle=M(\alpha-\beta)+\tfrac{1}{2}F(\alpha+\beta).

Assuming that the reactions
F
have a stationary state
u^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
i.e.

F(u^{*})=0,
the equilibrium of (
102
) is given by

\alpha^{*}=\beta^{*}\equiv\frac{u^{*}}{2}.

Let
N\coloneqq DF(u^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
be the Jacobian of
F
at the
stationary state. A simple computation shows that the linearization
around the equilibrium of (
102
) reads

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-M&M\\
M&-M\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}N&N\\
N&N\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}.

(103)

It should be noted that (
103
) describes the time evolution
of the functions
\alpha-u^{*}/2
and
\beta-u^{*}/2
but we relabeled them back to

\alpha
and
\beta
respectively. System (
103
) is
equivalent to
[
18
, (20), p. 55]
with the introduction of
the variables
\alpha+\beta
and
\alpha-\beta.
In
[
18
]
,
these new variables were used to reduce (
102
) to a reaction
telegraph system, see
[
18
, p. 54]
for details on this
connection.

The linearized model (
103
) is a transport-reaction model
studied in
Chapter

3
. In order to be consistent with
our previous notation, we introduce the block matrices

V\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma&0\\
0&-\Gamma\end{pmatrix},\qquad B\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}-M&M\\
M&-M\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}N&N\\
N&N\end{pmatrix}

and the operator

-A+B=-V\partial_{x}+B

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{4})
with domain
D(-A+B)=D(-A)=H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{4}).
Then,
-A+B

generates the strongly continuous transport-reaction semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{4})
by
Theorem

3.6
and
Corollary

2.11
. The solution of
(
103
) with initial function
(\alpha,\beta)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,C^{4})
is given by
t\mapstochar\rightarrow R(t)(\alpha,\beta)
, see Appendix
B
for details on the solution concepts used in a semigroup context.

The stability analysis can be performed with
Theorem

4.16

and
Theorem

4.19
. If all eigenvalues of the generator
(-A+B,\,H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{4}))
have a negative real part, the zero solution of
(
103
) is exponentially stable because of
Lemma

D.9
. Conversely, existence of an eigenvalue
\lambda\in\sigma(-A+B)
with
\real\lambda>0
means that the zero solution is unstable. Therefore, by
Theorem

4.16
, the question of stability comes down to the computation of the eigenvalues of

M(k)=-2\pi ikV+B

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}.
It is left to determine the roots of

\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow P(\lambda)\coloneqq\det(-2\pi ikV+B-\lambda I_{\mathbb{C}^{4\times 4}})=\det\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi ik\Gamma-M+\tfrac{1}{2}N-\lambda&M+\tfrac{1}{2}N\\
M+\tfrac{1}{2}N&2\pi ik\Gamma-M+\tfrac{1}{2}N-\lambda\end{pmatrix}

with the abbreviation
\lambda
for
\lambda I_{\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}}.
At first glance,
this looks quite laborious but some clever manipulations reduce the length of
the computation tremendously. Notice that we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I&-I\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi ik\Gamma-M+\tfrac{1}{2}N-\lambda&M+\tfrac{1}{2}N\\
M+\tfrac{1}{2}N&2\pi ik\Gamma-M+\tfrac{1}{2}N-\lambda\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I&I\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I&-I\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi ik\Gamma-M+\tfrac{1}{2}N-\lambda&-2\pi ik\Gamma+N-\lambda\\
M+\tfrac{1}{2}N&2\pi ik\Gamma+N-\lambda\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi ik\Gamma-2M-\lambda&-4\pi ik\Gamma\\
M+\tfrac{1}{2}N&2\pi ik\Gamma+N-\lambda\end{pmatrix}.

This transformation does not change the determinant and now, the upper left and
upper right matrices are diagonal matrices because
\Gamma
and
M
are diagonal
by definition. They particularly commute and we can use
[
36
, Theorem 3, p.
4]
to deduce

\displaystyle P(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi ik\Gamma-2M-\lambda&-4\pi ik\Gamma\\
M+\tfrac{1}{2}N&2\pi ik\Gamma+N-\lambda\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}(2\pi ik\Gamma+N-\lambda)(-2\pi ik\Gamma-2M-\lambda)-(M+\tfrac{1}{2}N)(-4\pi ik\Gamma)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\det(\lambda^{2}+\lambda(2M-N)+(4\pi^{2}k^{2}\Gamma^{2}-2NM)).

With the notation

N=\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{1}&\nu_{2}\\
\nu_{3}&\nu_{4}\end{pmatrix},

the obtained
2\times 2
-matrix reads

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{2}+(2\mu_{1}-\nu_{1})\lambda+(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{1}^{2}-2\nu_{1}\mu_{1})&-\nu_{2}\lambda-2\nu_{2}\mu_{2}\\
-\nu_{3}\lambda-2\nu_{3}\mu_{1}&\lambda^{2}+(2\mu_{2}-\nu_{4})\lambda+(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{2}^{2}-2\nu_{4}\mu_{2})\end{pmatrix}

and its determinant is given by

\displaystyle P(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}+\big[(2\mu_{2}-\nu_{4})+(2\mu_{1}-\nu_{1})\big]\lambda^{3}

\displaystyle+\big[(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{2}^{2}-2\nu_{4}\mu_{2})+(2\mu_{1}-\nu_{1})(2\mu_{2}-\nu_{4})+(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{1}^{2}-2\nu_{1}\mu_{1})-\nu_{2}\nu_{3}\big]\lambda^{2}

\displaystyle+\big[(2\mu_{1}-\nu_{1})(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{2}^{2}-2\nu_{4}\mu_{2})+(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{1}^{2}-2\nu_{1}\mu_{1})(2\mu_{2}-\nu_{4})-2\nu_{2}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}-2\nu_{2}\nu_{3}\mu_{2}\big]\lambda

\displaystyle+\big[(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{1}^{2}-2\nu_{1}\mu_{1})(4\pi^{2}k^{2}v_{2}^{2}-2\nu_{4}\mu_{2})-4\nu_{2}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}+\big[2(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})-(\nu_{1}+\nu_{4})\big]\lambda^{3}

\displaystyle+\big[4\pi^{2}k^{2}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2})-2(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})(\nu_{1}+\nu_{4})+4\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\det N\big]\lambda^{2}

\displaystyle+\big[4\pi^{2}k^{2}\big(v_{1}^{2}(2\mu_{2}-\nu_{4})+v_{2}^{2}(2\mu_{1}-\nu_{1})\big)-4(\nu_{1}+\nu_{4})\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+2(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})\det N\big]\lambda

\displaystyle+\big[16\pi^{4}k^{4}v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{2}-8\pi^{2}k^{2}\big(v_{1}^{2}\nu_{4}\mu_{2}+v_{2}^{2}\nu_{1}\mu_{1}\big)+4\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\det N\big].

(104)

Including the side length
L>0
to the model by applying
Theorem

5.1
and using the notations
(R^{L,v}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(-A+B)^{L,v}
for the semigroup and generator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{4})
yields the following theorem.

Theorem 6.15
.

A complex number
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of the operator

(-A+B)^{L,v}
if and only if there is a mode
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
P_{L}(\lambda)=0,
where
P_{L}
is the
polynomial

P_{L}(\lambda)=\lambda^{4}+a_{3}\lambda^{3}+a_{2}\lambda^{2}+a_{1}\lambda+a_{0}

with coefficients

\displaystyle a_{3}

\displaystyle=2(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})-(\nu_{1}+\nu_{4}),

\displaystyle a_{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{4\pi^{2}k^{2}}{L^{2}}(v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2})-2(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})(\nu_{1}+\nu_{4})+4\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\det N,

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{4\pi^{2}k^{2}}{L^{2}}\big(v_{1}^{2}(2\mu_{2}-\nu_{4})+v_{2}^{2}(2\mu_{1}-\nu_{1})\big)-4(\nu_{1}+\nu_{4})\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+2(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})\det N,

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{16\pi^{4}k^{4}}{L^{4}}v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{2}-2\tfrac{4\pi^{2}k^{2}}{L^{2}}\big(v_{1}^{2}\nu_{4}\mu_{2}+v_{2}^{2}\nu_{1}\mu_{1}\big)+4\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\det N.

In
[
18
]
, the author computed the eigenvalues of the generator

(-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L}
on
(0,L)
with the Neumann boundary conditions
introduced in
Section

6
. The astonishing thing is the
correspondence between both results.

Theorem 6.16

(
[
18
, Lemma 4.1, p. 61; Theorem 4.3, p. 63]
)
.

A complex number
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
\real\lambda>\max\{-2\mu_{1},-2\mu_{2}\}

is an eigenvalue of the operator
(-A+B)^{\text{Neu},L}
if and only if there
is a mode
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
P_{2L}(\lambda)=0,
where
P_{2L}
is exactly
the same polynomial as in
Theorem

6.15
.

Remark 6.17
.

The restriction
\real\lambda>\max\{-2\mu_{1},-2\mu_{2}\}
is a technical
assumption needed for the computations in
[
18
]
and, due to

\mu_{1},\mu_{2}>0,
it is irrelevant if one is only interested in stability or
blow-up results.

The theorems strongly support the presumption that there is a deeper connection
of the spectral properties between the generator of symmetric models on
(0,L)
with Neumann boundary conditions to the same models on
(0,2L)
with periodic boundary conditions than the one pointed out in
Section

6
. We conjecture
that all inclusions in
Corollary

5.5
are actually
equalities.

The next logical step is to study the polynomial from
Theorem

6.15

and to characterize parameter constellations which turn the model
(
103
) stable or unstable. T. Hillen studied this question
in the second half of his paper
[
18
]
under the two
assumptions that
u^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a stable equilibrium of the ODE model

y^{\prime}=F(y)
and that
N=DF(u^{*})
corresponds to an activator-inhibitor system, see

[
18
, (H1) and (H2), p. 56]
for the exact parameter choices. We
briefly elucidate his main idea, which we believe is currently the standard approach to
deal with stability questions of sophisticated transport-reaction models. The
author used, cf.
[
18
, p. 63]
, the Routh-Hurwitz criterion
from
[
12
, p. 194ff.]
:

All roots of a polynomial

P(\lambda)=\lambda^{4}+a_{3}\lambda^{3}+a_{2}\lambda^{2}+a_{1}\lambda+a_{0}

have negative real part if and only if

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<a_{3},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<a_{2}a_{3}-a_{1},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<(a_{2}a_{3}-a_{1})a_{1}-a_{3}^{2}a_{0},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<a_{0}\big((a_{2}a_{3}-a_{1})a_{1}-a_{3}^{2}a_{0}\big).

These conditions are equivalent to

a_{3},\,a_{2},\,a_{1},\,a_{0}>0\quad\text{ and }\quad(a_{2}a_{3}-a_{1})a_{1}-a_{3}^{2}a_{0}>0.

We refer to
[
18
]
for the actual computations and findings for
the reaction random walk system (
103
).

Chapter 7
Pattern Formation

The goal of this chapter is to study transport-driven instabilities. This means that we are interested in the question whether an additional spacial variable and transport can cause stable reactions to become unstable. If so, what are the patterns or phenomena occurring and how does the solution of the transport-reaction equation (
18
) behave qualitatively?

Originally, this questions was studied by Turing in the context of reaction-diffusion equations
[
38
]
. Although chemical reactions and diffusion are both homogenizing and stabilizing mechanisms, together they can drive very regular instabilities, so called Turing patterns. His discovery led to a broad study of these patterns in reaction-diffusion equations, especially in the context of biology.

The first section gives an introduction to the mathematical modeling of chemical reactions and the concept of Turing patterns, where the second part of it follows the recent review
[
39
]
. Beyond motivating reaction-diffusion equations and the concept “Turing patterns”, we also emphasize that the typical parameter choices causing these instabilities are of activator-inhibitor type.

For the transport-reaction system

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

(105)

with
N\in\mathbb{N}
components, transport directions
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and linear reactions described by a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N},
pattern formation mechanisms and instabilities caused by movement are less studied than for reaction-diffusion equations. Even though linear transport is not homogenizing, the detailed study of the simple Goldstein-Kac model in
Section

7
and the
6.3
could suggest that it corresponds to a “stirring motion”, which has no influence on the stability of an equilibrium. On top of that,
Lemma

3.21
shows that the averages of the concentrations will converge to zero, if
B
is stable, i.e. if
B
has only eigenvalues with a negative real part.

However, it is known that (
105
) can also generate Turing patterns. This has for example been shown in
[
18
]
for a concrete system of
N=4
equations. In
[
37
, Theorem 11, p. 20]
, specific conditions for a system with an arbitrary number
N
of components were found, which lead to Turing patterns. These conditions from
[
37
]
are unfortunately a little elusive, less concrete as for reaction-diffusion equations and require a priori assumptions on
B
. One reason for this is that Turing patterns in transport-reactions models require a minimal degree of complexity in the sense that the number of components
N
needs to be greater than two.

To the best of our knowledge, there is currently no general analysis of transport-driven instabilities and we aim to close this gap. Restricting ourselves to the case
d=1
, the transport reaction system (
105
) reads

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u_{N}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

(106)

with
N\in\mathbb{N}
components, transport directions
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
and linear reactions described by a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
. We consider the equation on the interval
(0,1)
with periodic boundary conditions, i.e. on the circle
\mathbb{T}.
At this point, it should be mentioned that this restriction to the one-dimensional case is, in principle, unnecessary.
Theorem

4.10
,
Theorem

4.19
and the methods we present in this chapter also allow a rigorous pattern formation analysis on the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}.
Nevertheless, we do not address the higher dimensional case here and propose it as a topic for future research.

In contrast to reaction-diffusion systems, Turing patterns are not the only instabilities caused by the additional spacial variable. We introduce the notion of hyperbolic instabilities which allows us to categorize all possible instabilities for (
106
): an instability is either a Turing pattern or a hyperbolic instability. Intuitively, hyperbolic instabilities correspond to an ever growing oscillation due to the dominance of increasingly high frequencies. As a side product of our analysis, we find a fairly general condition which ensures the existence of Turing patterns.

10
Reaction-Diffusion Equations and Turing Patterns

Before introducing reaction-diffusion equations and diffusion-driven instabilities, we give a brief introduction to the mathematical modeling of chemical reaction networks.

10.1
Reaction Networks

This subsection will only deal with the dynamics in a single cell. As a mathematical consequence, there is no spacial
variable and the reaction network can be fully described by a system of ODEs. The short
overview given here is inspired by the lecture ”Computational Systems Biology”
held by Professor Jan Hasenauer in the winter term 2020 at the University of
Bonn and
[
24
]
.

To begin with, let us put chemical reactions in concrete and formal terms.
Chemical species
Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{N}
are chemically identical molecular entities
and their state
Y=(Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{N})
is given by a
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{N}
valued vector
describing the current number of molecules. These chemical species interconvert
by reacting. In general, a single chemical reaction

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{j=1}^{N}s_{j}^{-}Y_{j}}_{\text{reactants}}\quad\xrightarrow{a(Y)}\quad\underbrace{\sum_{j=1}^{N}s_{j}^{+}Y_{j}}_{\text{products}}

(107)

is characterized by the
stoichiometric coefficients

s_{j}^{-},\,s_{j}^{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
j=1,\cdots N
and by the
state-dependent
reaction propensity

a(Y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The latter is
defined such that
a(Y)h+o(h)
gives the probability of the reaction taking
place in
(t,\,t+h)
for
h>0.
In principle, this propensity can have the
time
t
as an additional argument but under the assumption of thermodynamic
equilibrium it is (almost) proportional to the product of molecule counts

\prod_{j=1}^{N}Y_{j}^{s_{j}^{-}}

[
14
]
. Prototypes of these reactions are zeroth order
reactions
\emptyset\xrightarrow{k}products
with propensity
k
for some
constant
k\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
or first order reactions
Y_{j}\xrightarrow{k}products
and second order reactions
Y_{i}+Y_{j}\xrightarrow{k}products
with propensities
kY_{j}
and
kY_{i}Y_{j}
respectively (in the case
i\neq j
).

Instead of tracking the number of molecules, we are interested in the
concentrations
y_{j}=[Y_{j}]
of the chemical species and accordingly, the vector
y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{N})
of all concentrations is
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{N}
valued. This
macroscopic perspective is mainly motivated by the fact that molecule numbers
are often high and continuous models are much easier to study qualitatively and
quantitatively with analytical tools. The propensities
a(y)
in this
macroscopic setting are proportional to
\prod_{j=1}^{N}y_{j}^{s_{j}^{-}}
. In particular, the propensities of the zeroth,
first and second order reactions are
k,\,ky_{j}
and
ky_{i}y_{j}
respectively. One can use the above mentioned considerations to
obtain a system of ODEs given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{N}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(s_{1}^{+}-s_{1}^{-})a(y)\\
\vdots\\
(s_{N}^{+}-s_{N}^{-})a(y)\end{pmatrix}.

Let us now consider an arbitrary number of reactions
R_{1},\cdots,R_{M}
with
reaction
m=1,\cdots,M
being

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}s_{mj}^{-}Y_{j}\quad\xrightarrow{a_{m}(y)}\quad\sum_{j=1}^{N}s_{mj}^{+}Y_{j}

and define the stoichiometric matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times M}
by
S_{jm}\coloneqq s_{mj}^{+}-s_{mj}^{-}.
Summing up all reaction fluxes
yields the ODE system

\frac{d}{dt}y=Sa(y)\eqqcolon F(y)

(108)

for the concentrations of the chemical species and
a(y)\coloneqq(a_{1}(y),\cdots,a_{M}(y)).

Example 7.1

(Reversible reaction)
.

Consider three chemical species and the reversible reaction

Y_{1}+Y_{2}\xrightleftharpoons[k_{-}]{k_{+}}Y_{3},

which can be split up into the two reactions

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}R_{1}\colon&Y_{1}+Y_{2}&\overset{k_{+}}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}&Y_{3},\\
R_{2}\colon&Y_{3}&\overset{k_{-}}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}&Y_{1}+Y_{2}.\\
\end{array}

The corresponding system of ODEs reads

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
y_{3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\
-1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}k_{+}\,y_{1}y_{2}\\
k_{-}\,y_{3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-k_{+}\,y_{1}y_{2}+k_{-}\,y_{3}\\
-k_{+}\,y_{1}y_{2}+k_{-}\,y_{3}\\
k_{+}\,y_{1}y_{2}-k_{-}\,y_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

10.2
The Full Model and Diffusion-Driven Instabilities

This subsection follows
[
39
]
. For reasons of consistency, we consider all equations on the one-dimensional domain
(0,L)
for
L>0
and with periodic boundary conditions, i.e. on the circle
\mathbb{T}_{L}.

Reaction-diffusion equations in the context of chemicals appear, if one adds a spacial variable to the models from
Section

10.1
. In contrast to bacteria and without any external force, the movement of chemicals is determined by Fick’s Law of Diffusion
[
10
]
. Therefore, the full model reads

\partial_{t}u(t,x)-D\Delta u(t,x)=F(u(t,x))

(109)

where
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{N})
is the vector of all concentrations and
D
is a diagonal matrix with entries
D_{j}>0
for
j=1,\cdots,N.
These diagonal entries are often called diffusivity constants and they control how quickly chemicals spread along the circle
[
39
, p. 221]
. The function
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
describes the chemical reactions and is typically given by (
108
).

For clarity, we now consider the reaction-diffusion equation describing two concentrations
u_{1}
and
u_{2}.
In this simple case, (
109
) reads

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}&0\\
0&D_{2}\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F_{1}(u_{1},u_{2})\\
F_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

(110)

Talking about a diffusion-driven instability only makes sense if there exists a homogeneous steady state
c\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that
F(c)=0
and
y\equiv c
is a stable equilibrium of the ODE model
y^{\prime}=F(y),
i.e. all eigenvalues of the Jacobian of
F
evaluated at the steady state

B\coloneqq DF(c)

have a negative real part. Let us write

B=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}.

Then, both eigenvalues of
B
having a negative real part is equivalent to

\displaystyle\trace(B)

\displaystyle=a+d<0,

(111)

\displaystyle\det(B)

\displaystyle=ad-bc>0.

(112)

Notice that this works out so nicely because
B
is only a
2\times 2
matrix. Also, these conditions cover both the cases of complex conjugate eigenvalues and two real eigenvalues.

In order to characterize conditions which turn the stable equilibrium
y\equiv c
to an unstable steady state
u\equiv c
of (
110
), we study the linearization

\partial_{t}u-\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}&0\\
0&D_{2}\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{2}u=Bu

(113)

of (
110
) with the same Fourier approach as in
Chapter

4
. From the perspective of semigroup theory, the operator

D+B\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}&0\\
0&D_{2}\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{2}+B

(114)

with domain
H^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
generates an analytic semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{2}).
This semigroup fulfills the spectral mapping theorem, see
Corollary

D.12
, and the question of stability comes down to studying the spectrum of
D+B.
If there exists
\lambda\in\sigma(D+B)
with
\real\lambda>0,
the equilibrium
y\equiv c
of the ODE model will turn unstable in the reaction-diffusion model. Now, any function
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
can be written as its unique Fourier series

u(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi i\tfrac{k}{L}x}

(115)

with Fourier coefficients

\hat{u}(k)=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{L}}u(x)e^{-2\pi i\tfrac{k}{L}x}\,dx\in\mathbb{C}^{2}.

An informal application of the generator (
114
) to the Fourier series (
115
) of
u
yields

\big((D+B)u\big)(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}M(k)\hat{u}(k)e^{2\pi i\tfrac{k}{L}x},

where
M(k)\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
are the matrices

M(k)=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{4\pi^{2}k^{2}}{L^{2}}D_{1}+a&b\\
c&-\frac{4\pi^{2}k^{2}}{L^{2}}D_{2}+d\end{pmatrix}\eqqcolon\begin{pmatrix}-k_{L}^{2}D_{1}+a&b\\
c&-k_{L}^{2}D_{2}+d\end{pmatrix}.

(116)

These considerations can be made rigorous on the domain
H^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L},\,\mathbb{C}^{2})
of
D+B
and

\sigma(D+B)=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))

(117)

follows similarly to
Proposition

4.7
and
Theorem

4.10
. Because we assumed
D_{1,2}>0
, the eigenvalues of
M(k)
never converge to a complex number as
|k|\rightarrow\infty
and we do not need to take the closure on the right-hand side of (
117
). It is left to characterize all parameter constellations which imply existence of an eigenvalue
\lambda
of some
M(k)
with a positive real part.

Hence, we need to compute the roots of

\displaystyle\det((M(k)-\lambda I_{\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}})

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}-k_{L}^{2}D_{1}+a-\lambda&b\\
c&-k_{L}^{2}D_{2}+d-\lambda\end{pmatrix}=(k_{L}^{2}D_{1}+\lambda-a)(k_{L}^{2}D_{2}+\lambda-d)-bc

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}+\lambda\big(k_{L}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})-\trace(B)\big)+k_{L}^{4}D_{1}D_{2}-k_{L}^{2}(D_{1}d+D_{2}a)+\det(B)

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\lambda^{2}+\lambda\big(k_{L}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})-\trace(B)\big)+h(k_{L}^{2}).

They are given by

\lambda_{\pm}(k)=\frac{\trace(B)-k_{L}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})}{2}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{\trace(B)-k_{L}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})}{2}\right)^{2}-h(k_{L}^{2})}

and (
111
) implies
\real(\lambda_{-}(k))<0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}.
Also,
\real(\lambda_{+}(k))
is positive if and only if
h(k_{L}^{2})<0,
which is equivalent to

k_{L}^{4}-\frac{D_{1}d+D_{2}a}{D_{1}D_{2}}k_{L}^{2}+\frac{\det(B)}{D_{1}D_{2}}<0

by the definition of
h(k_{L}^{2})
. This implies the necessary condition

k_{-}^{2}<k_{L}^{2}<k_{+}^{2},

(118)

where

k_{\pm}^{2}=\frac{D_{1}d+D_{2}a}{2D_{1}D_{2}}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{D_{1}d+D_{2}a}{2D_{1}D_{2}}\right)^{2}-\frac{\det(B)}{D_{1}D_{2}}}.

(119)

Condition (
118
) can be realized iff
k_{+}^{2}>0,
i.e. if and only if

k_{+}^{2}=\frac{D_{1}d+D_{2}a}{2D_{1}D_{2}}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{D_{1}d+D_{2}a}{2D_{1}D_{2}}\right)^{2}-\frac{\det(B)}{D_{1}D_{2}}}>0.

(120)

Given
\det(B)>0
, see (
112
), (
120
) is equivalent to the two conditions

\frac{D_{1}d+D_{2}a}{2D_{1}D_{2}}>0\quad\text{ and }\quad\left(\frac{D_{1}d+D_{2}a}{2D_{1}D_{2}}\right)^{2}-\frac{\det(B)}{D_{1}D_{2}}>0.

These two inequalities yield the single condition

D_{1}d+D_{2}a>2\sqrt{D_{1}D_{2}}\sqrt{\det(B)}>0.

(121)

Finally, putting (
111
), (
112
), (
120
) and (
121
) together yields a characterization of the existence of Turing patterns for the reaction-diffusion equation (
110
).

Theorem 7.2

(
[
39
, p. 225]
)
.

Let
L>0
be the length of the circle
\mathbb{T}_{L}
. Moreover, let
c\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
F(c)=0
and let
B=DF(c).
The reaction-diffusion equation (
110
) generates Turing patterns if and only if the diffusion and reaction parameters fulfill

\displaystyle\trace(B)

\displaystyle=a+d<0,

\displaystyle\det(B)

\displaystyle=ad-bc>0,

\displaystyle D_{1}d+D_{2}a>2

\displaystyle\sqrt{D_{1}D_{2}}\sqrt{\det(B)}>0

and there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with

k_{-}^{2}<\frac{4\pi^{2}k^{2}}{L^{2}}<k_{+}^{2},

where
k_{\pm}^{2}
are defined in (
119
).

Remarks 7.3
.

(i)

The reaction-diffusion equation (
110
) is symmetrical in
u_{1}
and
u_{2}.
Moreover, either
a
or
d
has to be negative for Turing patterns to exist. Let
d<0
without loss of generality. Then, for Turing patterns to exist,
a
has to be positive and
b
and
c
need to have different signs.

The situation in which
b,\,d<0
is often interpreted as an activator-inhibitor system. The activator
u_{1}
increases by itself and its growth can be offset by the inhibitor
u_{2},
which increases only in the presence of the activator
[
9
, p. 175]
.

(ii)

The wave number
|k|
with the largest possible real part for an eigenvalue of
M(k)
is dominant for large times and indicates the number of peaks in the emerging pattern
[
39
, cf. p. 223]
.

(iii)

The last condition in the above theorem shows that a decreasing domain size shrinks the window of viable wave numbers
k

[
39
, p. 226]
.

We end this introduction to Turing pattern formation with the general definition of Turing patterns, which can be applied in a broader context, e.g. in the context of linear systems of
N\in\mathbb{N}
equations. To this end, we introduce the notation

\Sigma(k)=\max_{\lambda\in\sigma(M(k))}\real\lambda.

Definition 7.4

(Turing pattern formation)
.

Model (
113
) generates Turing patterns if
\sigma(B)\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{-}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,\real\lambda<0\}
and if there exist finitely many
k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the following holds:

(i)

\Sigma(k_{i})=\Sigma(k_{j})>0\qquad\text{for all }i,j=1,\cdots,n,

(ii)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
k\neq k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}\end{subarray}}\Sigma(k)<\Sigma(k_{i})\qquad\text{for all }i=1,\cdots,n.

11
Transport-Driven Instabilities

As mentioned in the introduction of this chapter, the goal is now to study the phenomenon of instabilities driven by an additional spacial variable in the context of the transport-reaction equation

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u_{N}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

(122)

on the circle
\mathbb{T}
and with transport directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{R}.
Here,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is a real matrix. The length of the circle can be easily incorporated with the knowledge from
Section

5
but we decided to neglect this additional index for the sake of readability.

The main difference to reaction-diffusion equations is that transport does not damp high frequencies. Mathematically, this corresponds to the transport-reaction semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
generated by

-A+B=-\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&v_{N}\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}+B=-V\partial_{x}+B,

see
Theorem

3.6
, not being analytic. Nevertheless, the question of stability still reduces to the study of the spectrum of the generator
\sigma(-A+B)
because of the weak spectral mapping
Theorem

4.19
. By
Theorem

4.10
, this spectrum is given by

\sigma(-A+B)=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))},

where
M(k)\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
are the matrices

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&v_{N}\end{pmatrix}+B=-2\pi ikV+B

(123)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}.
Compared to (
116
), the matrices (
123
) are complex valued. Furthermore, there exist constants
c_{1},\,c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\sigma(-A+B)
is contained in a strip
\mathbb{C}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\coloneqq\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,c_{1}<\real\lambda<c_{2}\}
by
Lemma

3.14
. In particular, we will see that infinitely many eigenvalues of
-A+B
can have a positive real part, even if
B
is stable and has only eigenvalues with a negative real part. However, there is still an underlying structure in the spectrum
\sigma(-A+B),
which becomes apparent after a detailed study of the eigenvalues of
M(k).
We emphasize that the following considerations are valid for arbitrary matrices
B
.

The main idea is to write
M(k)
as

M(k)=-2\pi ik\left(V+\frac{i}{2\pi k}B\right),

so it suffices to compute the eigenvalues of

V+\frac{i}{2\pi k}B.

(124)

Now for large
|k|
, the matrices (
124
) are a small perturbation of
V
and perturbation theory from
[
23
, Chapter 2]
can be applied. The theory in
[
23
]
is presented in the most general way possible, which makes the application not too straight forward, even though our situation is essentially the easiest case regarding
[
23
, Chapter 2, (2.1), p. 74]
. Therefore, we follow
[
23
]
carefully, add some intermediate steps and try to attach importance to bibliographical references.

In the following, we always assume:

Assumption 1
.

The transport-directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
are pairwise different, i.e.
v_{i}\neq v_{j}
holds for all
i,j=1,\cdots,N.

1
is necessary in order to get exact formulas for the eigenvalues of
M(k)
for large
|k|
from an application of
[
23
, Chapter 2]
. It has also been assumed in
[
37
]
and is very unlikely to be violated in applications.

Now, we follow
[
23
]
to study the eigenvalues of

V(z)\coloneqq V+zB

(125)

for
z\in\mathbb{C}
with small
|z|.
This eigenvalue problem falls into the category of
[
23
, (1.2), p. 63]
with
B^{(1)}=B
and
B^{(n)}=0
for all
n\geq 2.
Notice that we adapted the notation from
[
23
]
to our setup by using the letters
V
and
B^{(1)}
instead of
T
and
T^{(1)}
respectively.

Under
1
, the unperturbed matrix
V(0)=V
has the distinct eigenvalues
v_{j}
with eigenprojections
P_{j}=e_{j}\otimes e_{j}
, eigennilpotents
D_{j}=0
and algebraic and geometric multiplicities
m_{j}=1
for
j=1,\cdots,N.

Let
j=1,\cdots,N
and let us introduce the matrices

\displaystyle S_{j}(\zeta)=-\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\left[(\zeta-v_{i})^{-1}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i})+\sum_{n=1}^{m_{i}-1}(\zeta-v_{i})^{-n-1}D_{i}^{n}\right]=-\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(\zeta-v_{i})^{-1}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i})

from
[
23
, (5.32), p. 40]
and

S_{j}\coloneqq S_{j}(v_{j})=-\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(v_{j}-v_{i})^{-1}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i})=\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i})

(126)

from
[
23
, (5.28), p. 40]
. Given this notation, the Laurent series of the resolvent
R(\zeta)=(V-\zeta)^{-1}
(caution: the definition from
[
23
]
differs to the definition of the resolvent we used in the previous chapters) at
\zeta=v_{j}
is given by

\displaystyle R(\zeta)

\displaystyle=-(\zeta-v_{j})^{-1}(e_{j}\otimes e_{j})+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\zeta-v_{j})^{n}S_{j}^{n+1}\eqqcolon\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}(\zeta-v_{j})^{n}S_{j}^{(n+1)},

(127)

see
[
23
, (5.18), p. 39]
and
[
23
, (2.9), p. 76]
. Here, we used the notation

S_{j}^{(0)}=-P_{j}=-e_{j}\otimes e_{j}\quad\text{ and }\quad S_{j}^{(n)}=S_{j}^{n}\text{ for }n\geq 1.

(128)

Formula (
127
) does not come as a surprise and can also be computed with the geometric series because
R(\zeta)
is of course just given by

R(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}(v_{1}-\zeta)^{-1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&(v_{N}-\zeta)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

(129)

on
\mathbb{C}\backslash\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\}.

For the perturbed matrix
V(z)
from (
125
), the resolvent
R(\zeta,z)=(V(z)-\zeta)^{-1}
can be written as the power series

R(\zeta,z)=R(\zeta)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}R^{(n)}(\zeta),

(130)

where

\displaystyle R^{(n)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{p}=n\\
\nu_{l}\geq 1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{p}R(\zeta)B^{(\nu_{1})}R(\zeta)B^{(\nu_{2})}\cdots B^{(\nu_{p})}R(\zeta)=(-1)^{n}R(\zeta)\big(BR(\zeta)\big)^{n},

see
[
23
, (1.13) and (1.14), p. 67]
. Notice that we used
B^{(n)}=0
for all
n\geq 2
because
V(z)
is just a linear perturbation of
V
.

Let
j=1,\cdots,N
and let
\Gamma_{j}
be a closed positively-oriented circle in the resolvent set
\rho(V)=\mathbb{C}\backslash\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\}
enclosing
v_{j}
but no other
v_{i}
for
i\neq j.
Let
|z|
be sufficiently small. Then, the operator

P_{j}(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}R(\zeta,z)\,d\zeta

(131)

is equal to the sum of the eigenprojections for all the eigenvalues of
V(z)
lying inside
\Gamma_{j}

[
23
, p. 67]
. The eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}(z)
are continuous functions in
z
by
[
23
, Summary, p. 73]
and therefore, assuming small
|z|,

1
ensures that there is exactly one eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(z)
lying inside
\Gamma_{j}
and
P_{j}(z)
is itself the eigenprojection of this eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(z)

[
23
, p. 68]
. At
z=0,
no eigenvalues depart from the unperturbed eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(0)=v_{j}
, i.e. there is no splitting, cf.
[
23
, Chapter 2, §1.2, p. 65f., p. 68]
.

The trick from
[
23
]
is now the following: the trace of a matrix is the sum of all its eigenvalues (including multiplicities). Consequently, we obtain the formula

\lambda_{j}(z)=\lambda_{j}(z)+(N-1)\cdot 0=\trace(V(z)P_{j}(z))=v_{j}+\trace\big((V(z)-v_{j})P_{j}(z)\big),

(132)

see
[
23
, (2.5), p. 75]
.

It is left to find more concrete formulas for the trace appearing in (
132
). Combining (
130
) and (
131
) yields

\displaystyle P_{j}(z)=e_{j}\otimes e_{j}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}P_{j}^{(n)}

(133)

with

P_{j}^{(n)}=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}R^{(n)}(\zeta)\,d\zeta=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}R(\zeta)\big(BR(\zeta)\big)^{n}\,d\zeta

see
[
23
, (1.17) and (1.18), p. 68]
. Now, we plug the Laurent series (
127
) of the resolvent
R(\zeta)
at
\zeta=v_{j}
into the integrand. This gives a Laurent series in
\zeta-v_{j},
of which only the term with power
(\zeta-v_{j})^{-1}
contributes to the integral
[
23
, p. 76]
. More precisely, the substitution yields

\displaystyle P_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}

\displaystyle R(\zeta)\big(BR(\zeta)\big)^{n}\,d\zeta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\bigg(\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}(\zeta-v_{j})^{k_{1}}S_{j}^{(k_{1}+1)}\bigg)\bigg(B\sum_{k_{2}=-1}^{\infty}(\zeta-v_{j})^{k_{2}}S_{j}^{(k_{2}+1)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\cdots\bigg(B\sum_{k_{n+1}=-1}^{\infty}(\zeta-v_{j})^{k_{n+1}}S_{j}^{(k_{n+1}+1)}\bigg)\Bigg]\,d\zeta

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}\cdots

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{n+1}=-1}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}(\zeta-v_{j})^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n+1}}\,d\zeta\bigg)S_{j}^{(k_{1}+1)}BS_{j}^{(k_{2}+1)}\cdots S_{j}^{(k_{n}+1)}BS_{j}^{(k_{n+1}+1)},

which results in the finite sum

\displaystyle P_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n+1}=-1\\
k_{l}\geq-1\end{subarray}}S_{j}^{(k_{1}+1)}BS_{j}^{(k_{2}+1)}\cdots S_{j}^{(k_{n}+1)}BS_{j}^{(k_{n+1}+1)}

(134)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n+1}=n\\
k_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}S_{j}^{(k_{1})}BS_{j}^{(k_{2})}\cdots S_{j}^{(k_{n})}BS_{j}^{(k_{n+1})},

cf.
[
23
, (2.12), p. 76]
.

Concerning the computation of (
132
), the same idea can be used. Notice that (
131
) and

(V(z)-v_{j})R(\zeta,z)=I_{\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}}+(\zeta-v_{j})R(\zeta,z)

for all
\zeta\in\range(\Gamma_{j})
gives

(V(z)-v_{j})P_{j}(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}(\zeta-v_{j})R(\zeta,z)\,d\zeta,

(135)

see
[
23
, (2.15), p. 77]
. Similar to above, using the power series (
130
) for
R(\zeta,z)
and holomorphy of

\zeta\longmapsto(\zeta-v_{j})R(\zeta)

in the interior of the circle
\Gamma_{j}
, see (
129
), yields

\displaystyle(V(z)-v_{j})P_{j}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}(\zeta-v_{j})R(\zeta)\,d\zeta+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}\widetilde{B}_{j}^{(n)}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}\widetilde{B}_{j}^{(n)}.

(136)

Here,
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{(n)}
are given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}(\zeta-v_{j})R^{(n)}(\zeta)\,d\zeta=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}(\zeta-v_{j})R(\zeta)\big(BR(\zeta)\big)^{n}\,d\zeta

(137)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n+1}=n-1\\
k_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}S_{j}^{(k_{1})}BS_{j}^{(k_{2})}\cdots S_{j}^{(k_{n})}BS_{j}^{(k_{n+1})},

cf.
[
23
, (2.18), p. 77]
. Notice that the last step follows analogously to the computation of
P_{j}^{(n)}
with the only difference being the additional factor
(\zeta-v_{j})
.

Finally, (
132
) and (
136
) gives the exact formula

\lambda_{j}(z)=v_{j}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}\coloneqq v_{j}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}\trace\widetilde{B}_{j}^{(n)}

(138)

for the eigenvalues of
V(z)=V+zB,
assuming that
|z|
is sufficiently small, cf.
[
23
, (2.22), p. 78]
. Although being horribly complicated, the formula for the coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}
only depend on the model parameters
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
and
B
, see (
126
) and (
137
). Moreover, these coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}
are always real.

There is a second more convenient expression for
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)},
which is

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{j}}\sum_{p=1}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{p}}{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{p}=n\\
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{p}=p-1\\
\nu_{l}\geq 1,\,k_{p}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\trace\big(B^{(\nu_{1})}S_{j}^{(k_{1})}\cdots B^{(\nu_{p})}S_{j}^{(k_{p})}\big)

(139)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=n-1\\
k_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\trace\big(BS_{j}^{(k_{1})}\cdots BS_{j}^{(k_{n})}\big),

see
[
23
, (2.31), p. 79]
. We refer to
[
23
, p. 78f.]
for any details on the proof. Notice that (
139
) and (
134
) also prove
[
23
, Problem 2.1, p. 80]
, namely the expression

\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}=\frac{1}{n}\trace\big(BP_{j}^{(n-1)}\big).

For convenience, we summarize everything in a theorem.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
V\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be a diagonal matrix with pairwise different diagonal entries and let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}.
Then for
z\in\mathbb{C}
with sufficiently small
|z|
, the matrix

V(z)=V+zB

has
N
distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(z),\cdots,\lambda_{N}(z)
with

\lambda_{j}(z)=v_{j}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}

(140)

for all
j=1,\cdots,N.
The coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n+1}=n-1\\
k_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\trace\big(S_{j}^{(k_{1})}BS_{j}^{(k_{2})}\cdots S_{j}^{(k_{n})}BS_{j}^{(k_{n+1})}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=n-1\\
k_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\trace\big(BS_{j}^{(k_{1})}\cdots BS_{j}^{(k_{n})}\big),

(141)

where
S_{j}^{(n)}
are the matrices

S_{j}^{(0)}=-P_{j}=-e_{j}\otimes e_{j}\quad\text{ and }\quad S_{j}^{(n)}=S_{j}^{n}\text{ for }n\geq 1

with

S_{j}=\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i}).

Before applying this result in the context of transport-driven instabilities, we actually compute the first three coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}
for
j=1,\cdots,N
and estimate
|\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}|.
The latter verifies convergence of the series in
Theorem

7.5
and is also useful for estimates of the remainder of the series in (
140
).

Lemma 7.6
.

Consider the setup from
Theorem

7.5
. For
j=1,\cdots,N
and
n=1,2,3
, the coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=b_{jj},

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{ji}b_{ij},

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}
