\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,l=1\\
i,l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{l}-v_{j})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{jl}b_{li}b_{ij}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{ij}b_{ji}b_{jj}.

Proof.

Let
j=1,\cdots,N.
We always use formula (
141
). For
n=1
, we have

\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(1)}=-\trace\big(BS_{j}^{(0)}\big)=\trace\big(B(e_{j}\otimes e_{j})\big)=b_{jj}.

The cyclic property of the trace yields

\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=1\\
k_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\trace\big(BS_{j}^{(k_{1})}BS_{j}^{(k_{2})}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\trace\big(BS_{j}B(-P_{j})\big)+\trace\big(B(-P_{j})BS_{j}\big)\right)=-\trace\big(BS_{j}BP_{j}\big)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\Big(

\displaystyle\trace\big(BS_{j}BS_{j}B(-P_{j})\big)+\trace\big(BS_{j}B(-P_{j})BS_{j}\big)+\trace\big(B(-P_{j})BS_{j}BS_{j}\big)

\displaystyle+\trace\big(BS^{2}_{j}B(-P_{j})B(-P_{j})\big)+\trace\big(B(-P_{j})BS_{j}^{2}B(-P_{j})\big)+\trace\big(B(-P_{j})B(-P_{j})BS_{j}\big)\,\Big)

\displaystyle=\trace\big(B

\displaystyle S_{j}BS_{j}BP_{j}\big)-\trace\big(BS_{j}^{2}BP_{j}BP_{j}\big).

We refer to Appendix
A
for the computation of the traces.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Consider the setup from
Theorem

7.5
. Then

|\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}|\leq\max_{i\neq l}|v_{i}-v_{l}|\left(\frac{2\|B\|_{\infty}}{\min_{i\neq l}|v_{i}-v_{l}|}\right)^{n}

holds for all
j=1,\cdots,N
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

The proof follows
[
23
, p. 88]
. Notice that the power series of
R(\zeta,z)
given in (
130
) converges for

|z|\,\|BR(\zeta)\|<1.

Moreover, the resolvent

R(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}(v_{1}-\zeta)^{-1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&(v_{N}-\zeta)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

(142)

has the matrix norm

\|R(\zeta)\|_{\infty}=\frac{1}{\min_{i=1,\cdots,N}|v_{i}-\zeta|}

for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\}.
Let
j=1,\cdots,N
and let
\Gamma_{j}
be the closed positively-oriented circles around
v_{j}
with radii

\frac{1}{2}\dist\Big(v_{j},\,\bigcup_{i\neq j}\{v_{i}\}\Big).

The series (
130
) is uniformly convergent for
\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}
if

|z|<\min_{\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}}\frac{1}{\|BR(\zeta)\|},

cf.
[
23
, (3.3), p. 88]
. In particular, it is uniformly convergent for

|z|<\frac{\min_{i\neq l}|v_{i}-v_{l}|}{2\|B\|_{\infty}}

(143)

because of

\displaystyle\frac{\min_{i\neq l}|v_{i}-v_{l}|}{2\|B\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\dist\big(v_{j},\,\cup_{i\neq j}\{v_{i}\}\big)}{2\|B\|_{\infty}}=\frac{1}{\|B\|_{\infty}}\min_{\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}}|v_{j}-\zeta|=\frac{1}{\|B\|_{\infty}}\min_{\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}}\min_{i=1,\cdots,N}|v_{i}-\zeta|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|B\|_{\infty}}\min_{\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}}\frac{1}{\|R(\zeta)\|_{\infty}}\leq\min_{\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}}\frac{1}{\|BR(\zeta)\|}.

Under the assumption of
z
satisfying (
143
), als o the power series (
133
) for
P_{j}(z)
and (
140
) for
\lambda_{j}(z)-v_{j}
is convergent
[
23
, p. 88]
, which ensures that every function we are dealing with is indeed holomorphic for small
|z|.
As a consequence, Cauchy’s inequality can be used to estimate the coefficients
|\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}|.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying (
143
), the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(z)
lies inside
\Gamma_{j}
by
[
23
, p. 88]
and it follows

|\lambda_{j}(z)-v_{j}|\leq\max_{\zeta\in\Gamma_{j}}|\zeta-v_{j}|\leq\max_{i\neq l}|v_{i}-v_{l}|,

cf.
[
23
, (3.4), p. 88]
. Finally, Cauchy’s inequality for the Taylor coefficients of holomorphic functions gives

|\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}|\leq\max_{i\neq l}|v_{i}-v_{l}|\left(\frac{2\|B\|_{\infty}}{\min_{i\neq l}|v_{i}-v_{l}|}\right)^{n},

see
[
23
, (3.5), p. 88]
.
∎

Given all the knowledge of the behavior of the eigenvalues of
V(z)
for small
|z|,
we can return our attention to the problem of transport-driven instabilities.

Corollary 7.8
.

Assume that the transport directions satisfy
1
. Then for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with sufficiently large absolute value, the matrix

M(k)=-2\pi ikV+B

has
N
distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(k),\cdots,\lambda_{N}(k)
with

\lambda_{j}(k)=b_{jj}-2\pi ikv_{j}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{i}{2\pi k}\right)^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n+1)}

for all
j=1,\cdots,N.
The coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}
are defined in
Theorem

7.5
. In particular, the real parts of the eigenvalues are given by

\real\lambda_{j}(k)=b_{jj}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2\pi k)^{2n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}.

(144)

Proof.

Notice that
M(k)
can be written as

M(k)=-2\pi ik\left(V+\frac{i}{2\pi k}B\right).

Then for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with large
|k|,

Theorem

7.5
implies that
M(k)
has the
N
distinct eigenvalues given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}(k)

\displaystyle=-2\pi ik\left(v_{j}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{i}{2\pi k}\right)^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}\right)=b_{jj}-2\pi ikv_{j}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{i}{2\pi k}\right)^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n+1)},

where we used
Lemma

7.6
in the last step. The addendum on the real part of the eigenvalues follows from the fact that all coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}
are real.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

Formula (
144
) for the real parts of the eigenvalues of
\lambda_{j}(k)
and
Lemma

7.7
directly imply

\lim_{|k|\rightarrow\infty}\real\lambda_{j}(k)=b_{jj}.

(145)

The statement from (
145
) can be strengthened in the sense that the convergence in (
145
) is “almost always” strictly monotone. This turns out to be the core result for the study of transport-driven instabilities. Before proving a result on the monotonicity of the convergence in (
145
), we need to estimate the remainder of (
144
).

Lemma 7.10
.

Assume that the transport directions satisfy
1
. Let
j=1,\cdots,N
and let
n^{*}\in\mathbb{N}.
Then it holds

\sum_{n=n^{*}+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2\pi k)^{2n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{k^{2(n^{*}+1)}}\right)\quad\text{ as }|k|\rightarrow\infty.

Proof.

This is a simple consequence of
Lemma

7.7
. Firstly, notice that we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n^{*}+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2\pi k)^{2n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi k)^{2(n^{*}+1)}}\sum_{n=n^{*}+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2\pi k)^{2(n-n^{*}-1)}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi k)^{2(n^{*}+1)}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n+n^{*}+1}}{(2\pi k)^{2n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2(n+n^{*}+1)+1)}.

Secondly,
Lemma

7.7
implies existence of constants
C_{1},\,C_{2}>0,
independent of
j
, with

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n+n^{*}+1}}{(2\pi k)^{2n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2(n+n^{*}+1)+1)}\right|\lesssim\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}k^{-2n}C_{1}C_{2}^{2(n+n^{*}+1)+1}\lesssim\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{C}{k}\right)^{n}\lesssim 1

as
|k|\rightarrow\infty.

∎

Theorem 7.11
.

Assume that the transport directions satisfy
1
and let
\lambda_{1}(k),\cdots,\lambda_{N}(k)
be the eigenvalues of

M(k)=-2\pi ikV+B.

Then one of the following is true:

(i)

\real\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually constant,

(ii)

\real\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually strictly monotone.

Remarks 7.12
.

(i)

We say that
(a_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}
is eventually constant / (strictly) monotone, if there exists some
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that the sequences
(a_{k})_{k\geq K}
and
(a_{-k})_{k\geq K}
are both constant / (strictly) monotone.

(ii)

The symmetry

\real\lambda_{j}(k)=\real\lambda_{j}(-k)

(146)

holds for all
j=1,\cdots,N
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
by
Lemma

4.11
.

Proof.

Let
j=1,\cdots,N
and define

n^{*}=\inf\big\{n\in\mathbb{N}\,\colon\,\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}\neq 0\big\}.

If
n^{*}=\infty,
then the real part of
\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually constant and equal to
b_{jj}
by (
144
) and (
145
). Otherwise, they are given by

\real\lambda_{j}(k)=b_{jj}+\sum_{n=n^{*}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2\pi k)^{2n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}=b_{jj}+\frac{(-1)^{n^{*}}}{(2\pi k)^{2n^{*}}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n^{*}+1)}+\sum_{n=n^{*}+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2\pi k)^{2n}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}.

Now,
Lemma

7.10
implies

\displaystyle\real\lambda_{j}(k+1)-\real\lambda_{j}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n^{*}}}{(2\pi)^{2n^{*}}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n^{*}+1)}\left(\frac{1}{(k+1)^{2n^{*}}}-\frac{1}{k^{2n^{*}}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{k^{2(n^{*}+1)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n^{*}}}{(2\pi)^{2n^{*}}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n^{*}+1)}\frac{(-2n^{*})}{|k|^{2n^{*}+1}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{k^{2(n^{*}+1)}}\right).

Consequently, in the case
(-1)^{n^{*}+1}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n^{*}+1)}>0
there exists a constant
C=C(n^{*})>0
with

\real\lambda_{j}(k+1)-\real\lambda_{j}(k)\geq\frac{2n^{*}(-1)^{n^{*}+1}}{(2\pi)^{2n^{*}}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n^{*}+1)}\frac{1}{|k|^{2n^{*}+1}}-\frac{C}{k^{2(n^{*}+1)}}>0

for large
|k|,
i.e. the real part of
\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually strictly monotonically increasing.

Similarly,
(-1)^{n^{*}+1}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n^{*}+1)}<0
implies that the real part of
\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually strictly monotonically decreasing.
∎

Remarks 7.13
.

(i)

The set of parameter constellations
(V,B)
, which imply existence of an eventually constant real part of an eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(k)
of
M(k)
is closed in the set of all parameters
(V,B)
fulfilling
1
. This is a consequence of the continuity of the map

(V,B)\longmapsto\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}

with domain

\{(V,B)\,\colon\,V\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}\text{ diagonal with }v_{i}\neq v_{j}\text{ for all }i,j=1,\cdots,N\text{ and }B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}\},

see (
141
).

(ii)

In some sense, it is “very unlikely” that one eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(k)
has eventually constant real part: the coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2n+1)}
have to vanish for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(iii)

Typically,
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}\neq 0
. In this case,
\real\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually strictly monotonically increasing if and only if

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,l=1\\
i,l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{l}-v_{j})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{jl}b_{li}b_{ij}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{ij}b_{ji}b_{jj}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}>0

and strictly monotonically decreasing if and only if

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,l=1\\
i,l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{l}-v_{j})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{jl}b_{li}b_{ij}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{ij}b_{ji}b_{jj}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}<0.

Although being very unlikely, the real part of an eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(k)
can be eventually constant. For
N=2,
we can give a characterization of all parameter choices leading to this instance.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
N=2.
Assume that the transport directions satisfy
1
, i.e.
v_{1}\neq v_{2}.
Let
\lambda_{1}(k)
and
\lambda_{2}(k)
be the eigenvalues of

M(k)=-2\pi ikV+B.

Then,
\real\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually constant if and only if
B
fulfills (at least) one of the following three conditions:

(i)

b_{12}=0
,

(ii)

b_{21}=0
,

(iii)

b_{11}=b_{22}
.

Remark 7.15
.

For
N=2
, the real parts of both eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}(k)
of
M(k)
are eventually constant if and only if the real part of one sequence
\lambda_{1}(k)
or
\lambda_{2}(k)
is eventually constant.

Proof.

The proof is given in Appendix
A

∎

As of now, we assume that
(V,B)
is not such an exceptional parameter constellation yielding existence of some eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(k)
of
M(k)
with eventually constant real part.

Theorem

7.11
and (
145
) have massive consequences for the long-term behavior of solutions of the transport-reaction system (
122
).

In the context of transport-driven instabilities, we assume

Assumption 2
.

The spectrum
\sigma(B)
is contained in the left half plane of
\mathbb{C},
that is

\sigma(B)\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{-}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,\real\lambda<0\}.

Let us recall the definition of Turing patterns. To this end, we again use the notation

\Sigma(k)=\max_{\lambda\in\sigma(M(k))}\real\lambda.

(147)

Definition 7.16

(Turing pattern formation)
.

The transport-reaction model (
122
) generates Turing patterns if
B
fulfills
2
and if there exist finitely many
k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the following holds:

(i)

\Sigma(k_{i})=\Sigma(k_{j})>0\qquad\text{for all }i,j=1,\cdots,n,

(ii)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
k\neq k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}\end{subarray}}\Sigma(k)<\Sigma(k_{i})\qquad\text{for all }i=1,\cdots,n.

In addition to Turing patterns, there is a second possible qualitative behavior for the solutions of (
122
).

Definition 7.17

(Hyperbolic instabilities)
.

The transport-reaction model (
122
) generates hyperbolic instabilities if
B
fulfills
2
and if there exists
b>0
and
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds:

(i)

The sequence
(\Sigma(k))_{k\geq K}
is strictly monotonically increasing with limit
b
,

(ii)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
|k|\leq K\end{subarray}}\Sigma(k)<b.

Remark 7.18
.

The definition relies on the symmetry
\sigma(M(-k))=\overline{\sigma(M(k))}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N},
see
Lemma

4.11
.

Intuitively, hyperbolic instabilities describe chaotic and increasingly oscillating behavior: for large
t
, high wave numbers
|k|
are dominant and for even larger
t
, even higher waves numbers are dominant due to the eventual strict monotonicity of
(\Sigma(k))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
. The remarkable thing is that
every single
high frequency is important. Given that the spectrum of the generator has to be contained in a strip
\mathbb{C}_{c_{1},c_{2}}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,c_{1}<\real\lambda<c_{2}\}
for some constants
c_{1},\,c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
by
Lemma

3.14
, hyperbolic instabilities in some sense correspond to the most chaotic qualitative behavior theoretically possible.

In general, if the initial function has an infinite Fourier series, increasingly high frequencies are dominant. If the initial function has a finite Fourier series, either the highest wave number or finitely many wave numbers independent of the initial function (
\text{argmax}_{|k|\leq K}\Sigma(k)
) are dominant. The number of peaks of the solution typically increases over time.

Regarding transport-driven instabilities, there is the following dichotomy.

Theorem 7.19
.

Let the transport-directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
be pairwise different. Moreover, assume that
B
is a stable matrix, i.e.
1
and
2
are fulfilled. If the transport-reaction model (
122
) generates an instability, the instability is either a Turing pattern or a hyperbolic instability.

Proof.

Notice that
Theorem

7.11
implies

\displaystyle\Sigma(k)

\displaystyle=\max_{\lambda\in\sigma(M(k))}\real\lambda=\max_{j=1,\cdots,N}\real\lambda_{j}(k)=\max\big\{\Sigma_{\text{incr}}(k),\,\Sigma_{\text{decr}}(k)\big\},

with

\Sigma_{\text{incr}}(k)\coloneqq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1,\cdots,N\\
\real\lambda_{j}(k)\text{ ev. strictly }\\
\text{ increasing }\end{subarray}}\real\lambda_{j}(k)

and

\Sigma_{\text{decr}}(k)\coloneqq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1,\cdots,N\\
\real\lambda_{j}(k)\text{ ev. strictly }\\
\text{ decreasing }\end{subarray}}\real\lambda_{j}(k).

Hence,
\Sigma(k)
is eventually the maximum of a strictly monotonically increasing and a strictly monotonically decreasing function. Moreover, (
145
) implies

\displaystyle\lim_{|k|\rightarrow\infty}\Sigma_{\text{incr}}(k)

\displaystyle=\max\{b_{jj}\,\colon\,\real\lambda_{j}(k)\text{ eventually strictly monotonically increasing}\}\eqqcolon b_{1},

\displaystyle\lim_{|k|\rightarrow\infty}\Sigma_{\text{decr}}(k)

\displaystyle=\max\{b_{jj}\,\colon\,\real\lambda_{j}(k)\text{ eventually strictly monotonically decreasing}\}\eqqcolon b_{2},

\displaystyle\lim_{|k|\rightarrow\infty}\Sigma(k)

\displaystyle=\max\{b_{1},\,b_{2}\}=\max_{j=1,\cdots,N}b_{jj}\eqqcolon b.

(148)

A case distinction yields the dichotomy: if we have
b_{1}\leq b_{2}
, the sequence
\Sigma(k)
converges eventually strictly monotonically decreasing to
b
and Turing patterns emerge. If we have
b_{1}>b_{2},
there exists some
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that the sequence
(\Sigma(k))_{k\geq K}
converges strictly monotonically increasing to
b
. Now, if there exists some
k
with absolute value smaller than
K
and

\Sigma(k)>b\qquad,

the transport-reaction model generates Turing patterns. If not,

\Sigma(k)<b

holds for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and the instability is hyperbolic.
∎

We also obtain a useful criteria, which guarantees the existence of transport-driven instabilities.

Corollary 7.20
.

Let the transport-directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
be pairwise different. Moreover, assume that
B
is a stable matrix, i.e.
1
and
2
are fulfilled. Assume that
B
has a positive diagonal entry
b_{jj}>0
. Then the transport-reaction model (
122
) is unstable and it either generates a Turing pattern or a hyperbolic instability.

Proof.

By assumption and (
11
), we have

\lim_{|k|\rightarrow\infty}\Sigma(k)=\max_{j=1,\cdots,N}b_{jj}>0

and the transport-reaction model (
122
) generates an instability. It is either a Turing pattern or a hyperbolic instability by the dichotomy
Theorem

7.19
.
∎

7.13
and (
145
) also reveal a useful criterion for the existence of Turing patterns.

Corollary 7.21
.

Let the transport-directions
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}
be pairwise different. Moreover, assume that
B
is a stable matrix, i.e.
1
and
2
are fulfilled. Let
j^{*}
be the index with
b_{j^{*}j^{*}}=\max_{j=1,\cdots,N}b_{jj}
(wlog unique). Assume that the model is unstable and assume

\lambda_{j^{*}}^{(3)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,l=1\\
i,l\neq j^{*}\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{l}-v_{j^{*}})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{j^{*}})^{-1}b_{{j^{*}}l}b_{li}b_{i{j^{*}}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq{j^{*}}\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{i}-v_{j^{*}})^{-2}b_{i{j^{*}}}b_{{j^{*}}i}b_{{j^{*}}{j^{*}}}<0.

Then the model generates Turing patterns.

Proof.

We eventually have

\Sigma(k)=\real\lambda_{j^{*}}(k),

which is eventually strictly monotonically decreasing due to
\lambda_{j^{*}}^{(3)}<0,
see
7.13
.
∎

11.1
Transport-Driven Instabilities for
N=2

Until now, we almost always considered the general case of arbitrary many components
N\in\mathbb{N}
. This subsection gives a very brief excursion to the case
N=2.
In this simple case, the transport-reaction model (
122
) reads

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
v_{2}u_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(149)

where we used the notation

B=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

for the matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}.
The key takeaway is that basically the same parameter choices for
B
which lead to Turing pattern formation in reaction-diffusion equations, see
Theorem

7.2
, lead to hyperbolic instabilities in transport-reaction equations. The difference is that the transport speeds
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
as well as the length
L
of the circle
\mathbb{T}_{L}
have no influence on the instability to occur.

In contrast to the general case with
N
components,
N=2
ensures that the sequences of the real parts of the eigenvalues of

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&0\\
0&v_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

are not only eventually monotone, but even monotone from
k=0
on. That is,
\big(\real\lambda_{1}(k)\big)_{k\geq 0}
and
\big(\real\lambda_{2}(k)\big)_{k\geq 0}
are monotone sequences. Also the assumption
v_{1}\neq v_{2}
from
Corollary

7.8
is no mathematical restriction at all. However, the case
v_{1}=v_{2}
is uninteresting, because thereby the eigenvalues of
M(k)
lie on straight lines.

In any case, for a transport-driven instability to occur,
B
has to be stable, i.e.

\displaystyle\trace(B)

\displaystyle=a+d<0,

\displaystyle\det(B)

\displaystyle=ad-bc>0.

Additionally, the case of eventually constant real parts of the eigenvalues of
M(k)
in
Theorem

7.11
can be ruled out by
Lemma

7.14
. If both eigenvalues are eventually constant, the eigenvalues lie either on straight lines or eventually have a real part equal to
a=d.
Under the assumption that
B
is stable and given the immediate monotonicity of the real parts of the eigenvalues, the whole spectrum of
-A+B
lies in
\mathbb{C}_{-}
and the transport-reaction model does not generate instabilities.

This consideration, (
145
) and the immediate monotonicity of the real parts of the eigenvalues can be summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 7.22
.

Let
N=2
. The transport-reaction model (
149
) generates instabilities if and only if
v_{1}\neq v_{2}
and the matrix
B
fulfills

\displaystyle\trace(B)

\displaystyle=a+d<0,

\displaystyle\det(B)

\displaystyle=ad-bc>0,

\displaystyle a>0

\displaystyle\text{ or }d>0.

In this case, the instability is a hyperbolic instability in the sense of
Definition

7.17
.

Remark 7.23
.

Wlog, let
d<0.
For hyperbolic instabilities to exist,
a
has to be positive and
b
and
c
need to have different signs.

The situation in which
b,\,d<0
and
a,\,c>0
fulfill all conditions is remarkably similar to the activator-inhibitor scenario for reaction-diffusion equations. The “hyperbolic interpretation” of these parameters is to see
u_{1}
as a prey and
u_{2}
as a predator. The prey
u_{1}
increases by itself and its growth can be offset by the predator
u_{2},
which increases only in the presence of the prey.

12
Supplementary Figures and Simulations

Let
v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
and let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}.
Consider the transport-reaction equation

\partial_{t}u+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
\vdots\\
v_{N}u_{N}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=Bu

(150)

Complementary to our abstract findings from
Corollary

7.8
, we add figures to demonstrate how the spectrum

\sigma(-A+B)=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))}

of generator of the transport-reaction semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\,\mathbb{C}^{N})
typically looks like. As always, the matrices
M(k)
are given by

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&v_{N}\end{pmatrix}+B=-2\pi ikV+B.

In addition, we simulated the solution of (
150
) in one exemplary case of Turing patterns and hyperbolic instabilities respectively. The simulations are based on formula (
55
). We have no expertise in numerical simulations of hyperbolic equations and used the exact formula in order to avoid difficulties like numerical diffusion.

12.1
Figures of The Spectrum for
N=2

In the case
N=2
, the transport-reaction model (
150
) reads

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
v_{2}u_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=B\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix},

for a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}.
What follows are plots of

\sigma(-A+B)=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))},

where the matrices
M(k)
are given by

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&0\\
0&v_{2}\end{pmatrix}+B.

Obviously, the plots only contain a part of the spectrum and there is a cutoff in
|k|.
Each dot in the pictures corresponds to one eigenvalue of
M(k),\,k\in\mathbb{Z}
. The black crosses are the eigenvalues of
B
and the parameter choices are written down on the right of each plot.

12.2
Figures of The Spectrum for
N=3

In the case
N=3
, the transport-reaction model (
150
) reads

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&0&0\\
0&v_{2}&0\\
0&0&v_{3}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}^{\prime}\\
u_{2}^{\prime}\\
u_{3}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=B\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{pmatrix}

with
B\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}.
What follows are plots of

\sigma(-A+B)=\overline{\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma(M(k))},

where the matrices
M(k)
are given by

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&0&0\\
0&v_{2}&0\\
0&0&v_{3}\end{pmatrix}+B.

(151)

Again, the plots only contain a part of the spectrum and there is a cutoff in
|k|.
Each dot in the pictures corresponds to one eigenvalue of
M(k),\,k\in\mathbb{Z}
. The black crosses are the eigenvalues of
B
and the parameter choices are written down on the right of each plot.

In contrast to
N=2,
there are a variety of possibilities for the location of the eigenvalues for small
|k|.
In particular, the real parts of the eigenvalues are generally no longer immediately monotone.

12.3
A Simulation for
N=3

As already mentioned, the solution formula (
55
) was used for the simulations. We cut off the frequencies at
K=100,
i.e. we considered an initial function
u_{0}
with

\hat{u}_{0}(k)=0

for all
|k|>100.
Then, the formula

u(t,x)=\sum_{k=-100}^{100}e^{tM(k)}\hat{u}_{0}(k)e^{2\pi ikx}

for the solution to (
150
) with initial function
u_{0}
is exact. Regarding the initial function, we perturbed each component
j=1,2,3
of the constant equilibrium by sampling the Fourier coefficients as random normal variables with

\hat{u}_{0,j}(k)\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,10^{-4}(k+1)^{-2}\right)

for
k=0,\cdots,100
and setting
\hat{u}_{0,j}(-k)=\overline{\hat{u}_{0,j}(k)}
. The computation of the matrix exponential in the solution formula was implemented with
scipy.linalg.expm
from the SciPy library.

Concerning the
3
D-plots below, an exponential scaling of the solution is necessary in order to get a reasonable visualization of the time evolution. In both our examples, we plotted the rescaled solution

\widetilde{u}(t,x)\coloneqq e^{-\frac{3}{2}t}u(t,x).

(152)

Given a specific model, the appropriate rate of the rescaling depends on the maximum time
t_{\text{max}}
of the simulation and on the growth rate of the solution - the latter can be read off from a plot of the spectrum of the generator.

The simulation of Turing patterns was performed in the exemplary case of

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0.1&0&0\\
0&-0.2&0\\
0&0&0.2\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-8&2&-9\\
-5&-3&-10\\
9&-9&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

(153)

This parameter choice coincides with the one from
Figure

10
. In particular, the spectrum of the generator of the transport-reaction semigroup, see
Figure

10
, indicates that Turing patterns emerge. It is probably not easily apparent from
Figure

10
but the wave numbers
k=\pm 4
maximize
\Sigma(k),
with
\Sigma(k)
being defined in (
147
). The matrices
M(k)
are accordingly given by

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}0.1&0&0\\
0&-0.2&0\\
0&0&0.2\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}-8&2&-9\\
-5&-3&-10\\
9&-9&-1\end{pmatrix},

see (
151
). Therefore, the number of peaks of the solution of (
153
) for large times should be equal to
4
. Our simulation verifies these theoretical considerations.

. The lower row shows plots of each component of the solution at the time

). Notice that the values of

are scaled, see (

).

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\

0&0&0.5\end{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\

(154)

This parameter choice coincides with the one from
Figure

. In particular, the spectrum of the generator of the transport-reaction semigroup, see
Figure

8
, indicates that hyperbolic instabilities emerge. Therefore, the number of peaks of the solution to (
154
) should increase and chaotic, increasingly oscillating behavior should emerge. Indeed, the simulation verifies our theoretical considerations.

The purpose of these detailed appendices is to strongly facilitate reading the
thesis and to write the paper as self-contained as possible.

We decided to present the most important results from semigroup and spectral
theory we applied, including standard references and comments for readers
unfamiliar with semigroup theory.

Our summaries of semigroup and spectral theory are based on the excellent books
from A. Pazy
[
30
]
and K.-J. Engel and R. Nagel

[
8
]
.

Appendix A
Postponed Proofs

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and let
k=1,\cdots,d.

(i)

Let
D_{k}^{h}u\rightharpoonup v
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Then the

k
-th weak derivative exists in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\partial_{k}u=v.

(ii)

Conversely, if
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it follows that
D_{k}^{h}u\rightarrow\partial_{k}u
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
p>1
and
D_{k}^{h}u\rightharpoonup\partial_{k}u
for
p=1
.

All convergences are with respect to
h\rightarrow 0.

Proof of

Lemma

2.2
.

(i)

Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be a test function. A translation yields

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}D_{k}^{h}u(\mathbf{x})\varphi(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})D_{k}^{-h}\varphi(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}

and by assumption, the left-hand side converges to

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v(\mathbf{x})\varphi(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}.

On the other hand
\varphi
is smooth, so the difference quotient converges
and dominated convergence implies

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v(\mathbf{x})\varphi(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(\mathbf{x})\partial_{k}\varphi(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x},

i.e. the
k
-th weak derivative of
u
exists and is given by
v
.

(ii)

The converse direction is a little bit more involved and we first prove a
uniform
L^{p}
bound of the difference quotients for
1\leq p<\infty
. To this
end, let us firstly consider
u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).
By the fundamental theorem of
calculus

\left|\frac{u(\mathbf{x}+h\mathbf{e_{k}})-u(\mathbf{x})}{h}\right|\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left|\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{x}+sh\mathbf{e_{k}})\right|\,ds

holds for arbitrary
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.
We now integrate over the torus,
apply Jensen’s inequality and use Fubini to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|D_{k}^{h}u(\mathbf{x})|^{p}\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}|\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{x}+sh\mathbf{e_{k}})|\,ds\right)^{p}d\mathbf{x}\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}|\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{x}+sh\mathbf{e_{k}})|^{p}\,dsd\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{x}+sh\mathbf{e_{k}})|^{p}\,d\mathbf{x}ds=\|\partial_{k}u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{p}.

For
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),
the bound follows by the density of
C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
respect to
\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.
Notice that the difference quotients of
an approximation sequence
(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converge for fixed
h
as

\|D_{k}^{h}u-D_{k}^{h}u^{(n)}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq 2|h|^{-1}\|u-u^{(n)}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\rightarrow 0\qquad\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

This shows

\|D_{k}^{h}u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq\|\partial_{k}u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}

(155)

for all
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

In the following, let
1<p<\infty.
Since
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is reflexive in this
case, we can choose a subsequence (without relabeling) such that
D_{k}^{h}u\rightharpoonup v
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).
It follows by (i) that
v=\partial_{k}u.
Notice that starting with a subsequence of

(D_{k}^{h}u)_{h}
implies that every subsequence has a subsequence which
converges weakly to the same limit in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),
namely
\partial_{k}u
. This implies weak convergence for
p>1
. Since (
155
) implies

\limsup_{h\rightarrow 0}\|D_{k}^{h}u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq\|\partial_{k}u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},

strong convergence follows from
[
3
, Proposition 3.32,
p. 78]
as
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is uniformly convex for
p>1.

Unfortunately,
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is not reflexive. However, the Dunford-Pettis
theorem, see
[
3
, Theorem 4.30, p. 115]
, characterizes
weakly compact sets and it suffices to show that
(D_{k}^{h}u)_{h}
is
equiintegrable. The same subsubsequence argument as before then implies weak
convergence of the whole sequence. Let
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and let
u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).
Note that we also have

\int_{A}|D_{k}^{h}u(\mathbf{x})|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{A}|\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{x}+sh\mathbf{e_{k}})|\,d\mathbf{x}ds=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{A_{s,h}}|\partial_{k}u(\mathbf{x})|\,d\mathbf{x}ds

for arbitrary measurable
A
and with
A_{s,h}
being the translated set.
Now, consider an arbitrary function
u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and take a sequence

(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
u^{(n)}\rightarrow u
in

W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. In particular,
(\partial_{k}u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
uniformly integrable and
D_{k}^{h}u^{(n)}\rightarrow D_{k}^{h}u
in

L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for every fixed
h
. Let
\varepsilon>0.
By the uniform
integrability of the approximation sequence, there exists
\delta>0
such that

\mathcal{L}^{d}(B)\leq\delta\quad\implies\quad\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{B}|\partial_{k}u^{(n)}|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq\varepsilon

for all measurable sets
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).
Now take
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
\mathcal{L}^{d}(A)\leq\delta
and fix
h>0.
It holds

\int_{A}|D_{k}^{h}u(\mathbf{x})|\,d\mathbf{x}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{A}|D_{k}^{h}u^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{A_{s,h}}|\partial_{k}u^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})|\,d\mathbf{x}ds\leq\varepsilon

due to
\mathcal{L}^{d}(A_{s,h})=\mathcal{L}^{d}(A).
This estimate is uniform
in
h
and hence, it holds

\sup_{h\neq 0}\int_{A}|D_{k}^{h}u|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq\varepsilon,

i.e.
(D_{k}^{h}u)_{h}
is equiintegrable.

∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\mathbf{v_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be non-vanishing, i.e.

\mathbf{v_{1},}\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\neq 0.
Moreover, let
j=1,\cdots,N.
The following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

Component
j
is transport periodic.

(ii)

\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\in\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\mathbb{Q}

for all
k=1,\cdots,d
and one (and hence all)
k^{*}
with

\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\neq 0.

(iii)

\displaystyle\bigcap^{d}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\mathbb{Z}\neq\{0\}.

Proof of

Lemma

2.15
.

(i)\implies(ii)
If component
j
is transport periodic, there
exists
t^{*}>0
with
t^{*}\mathbf{v_{j}}=(l_{1},\cdots,l_{d})^{T}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.
Then

\mathbf{v_{j,k}}=\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\frac{l_{k}}{l_{k^{*}}}\in\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\mathbb{Q}

for all
k=1,\cdots,d
and all
k^{*}
with

\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\neq 0.

(ii)\implies(iii)
By assumption, there exists
k^{*}
with

\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\neq 0
and

\mathbf{v_{j,k}}=\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\frac{p_{k}}{q_{k}}

for all
k=1,\cdots,d
and some
p_{k}\in\mathbb{Z},\,q_{k}\in\mathbb{N}.
Take an arbitrary
k
with
\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\neq 0.
It follows that

0\neq\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
p_{l}\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{d}p_{l}=\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\bigg(q_{k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\neq k\\
p_{l}\neq 0\end{subarray}}p_{l}\bigg)\in\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\mathbb{Z}.

(iii)\implies(ii)\implies(i)
Condition
(iii)
implies that there exists

\lambda\neq 0
and
p_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\lambda=\mathbf{v_{j,k}}p_{k}

for all
k
with
\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\neq 0.
In this
case

\mathbf{v_{j,k}}=\frac{\lambda}{p_{k}}=\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\frac{p_{k^{*}}}{p_{k}}\in\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\mathbb{Q}

for one (and hence all)
k^{*}
with
\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\neq 0.
For

t\coloneqq\frac{1}{\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
\mathbf{v_{j}^{l}\neq 0}\end{subarray}}^{d}p_{l}>0

we obtain

t\mathbf{v_{j,k}}=t\mathbf{v_{j,k^{*}}}\frac{p_{k^{*}}}{p_{k}}=p_{k^{*}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\neq k\\
\mathbf{v_{j}^{l}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}p_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}

for all
k
with
\mathbf{v_{j,k}}\neq 0.
For the
other
k,
we trivially have
t\mathbf{v_{j,k}}=0\in\mathbb{Z}.

∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Consider the setup from
Theorem

7.5
. For
j=1,\cdots,N
and
n=1,2,3
, the coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=b_{jj},

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{ji}b_{ij},

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,l=1\\
i,l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{l}-v_{j})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{jl}b_{li}b_{ij}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{ij}b_{ji}b_{jj}.

Proof of

Lemma

7.6
.

Recall

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\trace\big(BS_{j}BP_{j}\big),

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\trace\big(BS_{j}BS_{j}BP_{j}\big)-\trace\big(BS_{j}^{2}BP_{j}BP_{j}\big),

where
P_{j}
and
S_{j}
are the matrices

P_{j}=e_{j}\otimes e_{j}\quad\text{ and }\quad S_{j}=\sum^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i}).

For the following matrices, the column differing from all other columns is always the
j
-th one. We have

\displaystyle BP_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0&b_{1j}&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&b_{Nj}&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle BS_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}(v_{1}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{11}&\cdots&(v_{j-1}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{1,j-1}&0&(v_{j+1}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{1,j+1}&\cdots&(v_{N}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{1N}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
(v_{1}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{N1}&\cdots&(v_{j-1}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{N,j-1}&0&(v_{j+1}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{N,j+1}&\cdots&(v_{N}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{NN}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle BS_{j}BP_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0&\sum_{i\neq j}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{1i}b_{ij}&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&\sum_{i\neq j}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{Ni}b_{ij}&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}.

This implies

\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(2)}=-\trace\big(BS_{j}BP_{j}\big)=-\big(BS_{j}BP_{j}\big)_{jj}=-\sum_{i\neq j}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{ji}b_{ij}

and

\displaystyle\trace\big(BS_{j}(BS_{j}BP_{j})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(BS_{j}BS_{j}BP_{j}\big)_{jj}=\sum_{l\neq j}(v_{l}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{jl}\sum_{i\neq j}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{li}b_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,l\neq j}(v_{l}-v_{j})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{jl}b_{li}b_{ij}.

(156)

Concerning the computation of
\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)},
we also need to compute

\displaystyle(BP_{j})^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0&b_{1j}b_{jj}&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&b_{Nj}b_{jj}&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle BS_{j}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}(v_{1}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{11}&\cdots&(v_{j-1}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{1,j-1}&0&(v_{j+1}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{1,j+1}&\cdots&(v_{N}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{1N}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
(v_{1}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{N1}&\cdots&(v_{j-1}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{N,j-1}&0&(v_{j+1}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{N,j+1}&\cdots&(v_{N}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{NN}\end{pmatrix}.

We obtain

\displaystyle\trace\big(BS_{j}^{2}BP_{j}BP_{j}\big)=\big(BS_{j}^{2}BP_{j}BP_{j}\big)_{jj}=\sum_{i\neq j}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{ji}b_{ij}b_{jj}

(157)

and finally, combining (
A
) and (
157
) gives

\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{(3)}=\sum_{i,l\neq j}(v_{l}-v_{j})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-1}b_{jl}b_{li}b_{ij}-\sum_{i\neq j}(v_{i}-v_{j})^{-2}b_{ji}b_{ij}b_{jj}.

∎

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
N=2.
Assume that the transport directions satisfy
1
, i.e.
v_{1}\neq v_{2}.
Let
\lambda_{1}(k),\lambda_{2}(k)
be the eigenvalues of

M(k)=-2\pi ikV+B.

Then
\real\lambda_{j}(k)
is eventually constant if and only if
B
fulfills (at least) one of the following three conditions:

(i)

b_{12}=0
,

(ii)

b_{21}=0
,

(iii)

b_{11}=b_{22}
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.14
.

Wlog, consider
j=1.
Let the real parts of
\lambda_{1}(k)
be eventually constant, i.e. constant for large
|k|.
In particular,
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(3)}=0
has to hold due to the proof of
Theorem

7.11
. Given the formula for
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(3)}
from
Lemma

7.6
and
N=2
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(3)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,l=1\\
i,l\neq 1\end{subarray}}^{2}(v_{l}-v_{1})^{-1}(v_{i}-v_{1})^{-1}b_{1l}b_{li}b_{i1}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq 1\end{subarray}}^{2}(v_{i}-v_{1})^{-2}b_{i1}b_{1i}b_{11}

\displaystyle=(v_{2}-v_{1})^{-2}b_{12}b_{22}b_{21}-(v_{2}-v_{1})^{-2}b_{21}b_{12}b_{11}

\displaystyle=(v_{2}-v_{1})^{-2}b_{12}b_{21}(b_{22}-b_{11}).

Hence, one of the three conditions of
(i),\,(ii)
and
(iii)
has to be fulfilled.

Conversely, if
(i)
holds true, i.e.
b_{12}=0
, the matrices
M(k)
read

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&0\\
0&v_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}b_{11}&0\\
b_{21}&b_{22}\end{pmatrix}

and the eigenvalues of
M(k)
are given by

\lambda_{j}(k)=b_{jj}-2\pi ikv_{j}

for
j=1,2.
In particular, their real parts are constantly equal to
b_{jj}.
In the case
(ii),

M(k)
is an upper triangular matrix and the computation is the same as in
(i).

Given
b_{11}=b_{22}\eqqcolon b,
the matrices
M(k)
read

M(k)=-2\pi ik\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&0\\
0&v_{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}b&b_{12}\\
b_{21}&b\end{pmatrix}

with

\displaystyle\det(M(k)-\lambda I_{\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}})

\displaystyle=(b-2\pi ikv_{1}-\lambda)(b-2\pi ikv_{2}-\lambda)-b_{12}b_{21}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}-\big(2b-2\pi ik(v_{1}+v_{2})\big)\lambda+(b-2\pi ikv_{1})(b-2\pi ikv_{2})-b_{12}b_{21}

Consequently, the eigenvalues of
M(k)
are given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(k)

\displaystyle=b-\pi ik(v_{1}+v_{2})\pm\sqrt{\big(b-\pi ik(v_{1}+v_{2})\big)^{2}-(b-2\pi ikv_{1})(b-2\pi ikv_{2})+b_{12}b_{21}}

\displaystyle=b-\pi ik(v_{1}+v_{2})\pm\sqrt{\pi^{2}k^{2}\big(4v_{1}v_{2}-(v_{1}+v_{2})^{2}\big)+b_{12}b_{21}}

\displaystyle=b-\pi ik(v_{1}+v_{2})\pm\sqrt{-\pi^{2}k^{2}(v_{1}-v_{2})^{2}+b_{12}b_{21}}.

For

k^{2}>\frac{b_{12}b_{21}}{\pi^{2}(v_{1}-v_{2})^{2}},

the term under the root is negative and
\real\lambda_{j}(k)=b.

∎

Appendix B
C_{0}
-Semigroups, Generators and Abstract Cauchy
Problems

Throughout this section,
(E,\|\cdot\|_{E})
is a complex Banach space and

\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open domain.

In this chapter, we give a heuristic introduction of semigroups and explain
central definitions, generator theorems and solution concepts.

Consider the partial differential equation

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\partial_{t}u(t,\mathbf{x})+Au(t,\mathbf{x})&=&F(t,\mathbf{x},u(t,\mathbf{x}))&\qquad(t,\mathbf{x})\in(0,\infty)\times\Omega,\\
u(0,\mathbf{x})&=&u_{0}(\mathbf{x})&\qquad\mathbf{x}\in\Omega\end{array}\end{cases}

(158)

for a spacial differential operator
-A
, e.g. the Laplacian
\Delta,
reasonable boundary conditions on
\partial\Omega
and a function
F\colon(0,\infty)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

The basic idea in semigroup theory is to consider the mapping
t\mapstochar\rightarrow u(t,\cdot)\in E
for fixed initial value
u_{0}
and some Banach space
(E,\|\cdot\|_{E}).
Then,
u(t)\coloneqq u(t,\cdot)\in E
is the solution at time

t
and (
158
) is interpreted as an ordinary differential equation on

E,
i.e. as

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+Au(t)&=&F(t,u(t))&\qquad t>0,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}

(159)

for a densely defined operator
-A
on
E
with domain
D(-A)
and a function
F\colon(0,\infty)\times E\rightarrow E.
Equation (
159
) is called
abstract Cauchy problem and the family of operators
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
E
,
which maps an initial function
u_{0}
to the solution for all

t\geq 0
, is called the semigroup generated by
(-A,\,D(-A)).
This family
should clearly satisfy
T(0)=I_{E},
because the solution at time
t=0
is the
initial function itself. Secondly, taking
t,s>0
and restarting
(
159
) after time
s
with initial function
T(s)u_{0}
should give
the same solution as if we did not restart the equation. Thirdly, the solution
map
t\mapstochar\rightarrow T(t)u_{0}
should fulfill a continuity property for every fixed
initial function. These considerations motivate the definition of a strongly
continuous semigroup.

Definition B.1

(
[
30
, Chapter 1, Definition 1.1, p. 1 and Definition 2.1,
p. 4]
)
.

A one parameter family
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
of bounded linear operators from
E

into
E
is a strongly continuous semigroup if

(i)

T(0)=I_{E},

(ii)

T(t+s)=T(t)T(s)\quad\text{ for all }t,s>0,

(iii)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\searrow 0}T(t)u=u\quad\text{ for every }u\in E.

Remark B.2
.

Strongly continuous semigroups are often called
C_{0}
-semigroups and
(ii)
is
the so called semigroup property.

Definition B.3

(
[
30
, Chapter 1, Definition 1.1, p. 1]
)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a strongly continuous semigroup. The linear operator

(-A,\,D(-A))
defined by

D(-A)=\bigg\{u\in E\,\colon\,\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{T(t)u-u}{t}\text{ exists}\bigg\}

and

-Au=\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{T(t)u-u}{t}\quad\text{ for }u\in D(-A)

is the infinitesimal generator of the semigroup,
D(-A)
is the domain of

-A.

In applications, one almost always starts with an abstract Cauchy problem
(
159
) and well-posedness comes down to the question, whether
-A

generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup. The domain of the operator has to be chosen
carefully and should take the boundary values of the PDE into account. With this
in mind, criteria which characterize infinitesimal generators of

C_{0}
-semigroups are extremely useful and are given through the theorems of
Hille-Yosida and Lumer-Phillips.

Theorem B.4

(
[
30
, Chapter 1, Theorem 2.2, p. 4]
)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup. There exists constants

\omega\geq 0
and
M\geq 1
such that

\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq Me^{\omega t}\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

(160)

Definition B.5

(
[
30
, Chapter 1, p. 8]
)
.

A strongly continuous semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
which fulfills the estimate
(
160
) with
\omega=0
is called uniformly bounded and if moreover

M=1
, it is called a semigroup of contractions.

Theorem B.6

(Hille-Yosida,
[
30
, Chapter 1, Theorem 5.3, p. 20]
)
.

A linear operator
(-A,\,D(-A))
is the infinitesimal generator of a

C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
, satisfying
\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq Me^{\omega t},
if and only if

(i)

-A
is closed and
D(-A)
is dense in
E
,

(ii)

The resolvent set
\rho(-A)
of
-A
contains the ray
(\omega,\infty)
and

\|R(\lambda,\,-A)^{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq\frac{M}{(\lambda-\omega)^{n}}\quad\text{ for all }\lambda>\omega\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N},

(161)

where
R(\lambda,\,-A)=(\lambda+A)^{-1}
is the resolvent.

Remark B.7
.

This general version of the Hille-Yosida theorem is mainly of theoretical
importance. In practice, the estimate (
161
) is hard to check for
all
n\in\mathbb{N}.
For contraction semigroups, there is a version of the Hille-Yosida
theorem which avoids this problem, see
[
30
, Chapter 1, Theorem 3.1, p. 8]
.

Definition B.8

(
[
30
, Chapter 1, Definition 4.1, p. 13 and Theorem 4.2,
p. 14]
)
.

A linear operator
(-A,\,D(-A))
is dissipative if

\|(\lambda I_{E}+A)u\|_{E}\geq\lambda\|u\|_{E}\quad\text{ for all }u\in D(-A)\text{ and all }\lambda>0.

Theorem B.9

(Lumer-Phillips
[
30
, Chapter 1, Theorem 4.3, p. 14]
)
.

Let
(-A,\,D(-A))
be a linear operator with dense domain
D(-A)
in
E.

(i)

If
-A
is dissipative and there is a
\lambda_{0}>0
such that the range

\range(\lambda_{0}I_{E}+A)
of
\lambda_{0}I_{E}+A
is
E
, then
-A
is the
infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup of contractions.

(ii)

Conversely, if
-A
is the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup of
contractions on
E,
then
\range(\lambda I_{E}+A)=E
for all
\lambda>0
and
-A
is dissipative.

After ensuring that
(-A,\,D(-A))
generates a strongly continuous semigroup,
the abstract Cauchy Problem (
159
) can be “solved”. There are
specific solution concepts for semigroup theory, since (
158
) was
reformulated into a Banach space setting and we state the basic concepts and
results.

Let
T>0,
possibly
T=\infty,
and let
I=[0,T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

be an interval. For simplicity, we start with the cases
F\equiv 0
and

F(t,u)=F(t)
in order to obtain a feeling for abstract Cauchy problems. In the
homogeneous case, the abstract Cauchy problem (
159
) on
I=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
reads

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+Au(t)&=&0&\qquad t>0,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}

(162)

and the next theorem validates the concept of semigroups.

Theorem B.10

(
[
30
, Chapter 4, Theorem 1.3, p. 102]
)
.

Let
(-A,\,D(-A))
be a densely defined linear operator with non-empty
resolvent set
\rho(-A).
The initial value problem (
162
) has
a unique solution
u\in C^{1}([0,\infty),\,E)
for every
u_{0}\in D(-A)
if and
only if
-A
is the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}.
In this case, the solution is given by
u(t)=T(t)u_{0}.

Hereafter, we will assume that
(-A,\,D(-A))
generates the
C_{0}
-semigroup

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
so that the homogeneous equation (
162
) has
a unique solution for
u_{0}\in D(-A)
.

The inhomogeneous abstract Cauchy problem on
I=[0,T)
reads

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+Au(t)&=&F(t)&\qquad 0<t<T,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}.

(163)

Definition B.11

(
[
30
, Chapter 4, Definition 2.1, p. 105]
)
.

A function
u\colon I\rightarrow E
is a classical solution on
I
to the abstract Cauchy problem (
159
) if

(i)

u\in C(I,\,E)\cap C^{1}((0,T),\,E),

(ii)

u(t)\in D(-A)
for all
t\in(0,T)
and (
159
) is satisfied
on
I
.

Definition B.12

(
[
30
, Chapter 4, Definition 2.3, p. 106]
)
.

Let
u\in E
and
F\in L^{1}((0,T),\,E).
The function
u\in C([0,T],\,E)

given by

u(t)=T(t)u+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)F(s)\,ds,\qquad t\in[0,T],

(164)

is the mild solution to the initial value problem (
163
)
on
[0,T].

Remark B.13

(
[
30
, Chapter 4, Corollary 2.2, p. 106]
)
.

Under the assumption
F\in L^{1}((0,T),\,E)
, a classical solution coincides
with the mild solution and (
163
) has at most one solution.
If (
163
) has a solution, it is given by
(
164
).

Theorem B.14

(
[
30
, Chapter 4, Corollary 2.5, p. 107 and Corollary 2.6,
p. 108]
)
.

The initial value problem (
163
) has a unique classical
solution on
I
for every
u_{0}\in D(-A),
if
F
satisfies one of the following
two properties:

(i)

F\in C^{1}([0,T],\,E),

(ii)

F\in C((0,T),\,E)\cap L^{1}((0,T),\,E)\quad\text{ and }\quad F(s)\in D(-A)\text{ for all }s\in(0,T)\text{ with }AF(\cdot)\in L^{1}((0,T),\,E).

We now summarize the main results for the semilinear initial value problem

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{rrll}\dot{u}(t)+Au(t)&=&F(t,u(t))&\qquad 0<t<T,\\
u(0)&=&u_{0}\end{array}\end{cases}.

(165)

The definition of classical solutions for (
165
) is
the same as before, see
Definition

B.11
. Similar to the linear
inhomogeneous case, classical solutions are mild solutions in the sense of the
next definition.

Definition B.15

(
[
30
, Chapter 6, Definition 1.1, p. 184]
)
.

A function
u\in C(I,\,E)
which solves the integral equation

u(t)=T(t)u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)F(s,u(s))\,ds,\qquad t\in I,

is called a mild solution to the initial value problem
(
165
) on
I
.

A typical Lipschitz assumption on
F
ensures local existence of mild
solutions.

Definition B.16

(
[
30
, Chapter 6, p. 185]
)
.

A function
F\colon[0,\infty)\times E\rightarrow E
is called locally Lipschitz
continuous in
u
, uniformly in
t
on bounded intervals, if for every
t^{\prime}\geq 0

and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
C(t^{\prime},n)
such that

\|F(t,u)-F(t,\widetilde{u})\|_{E}\leq C(t^{\prime},n)\|u-\widetilde{u}\|_{E}

holds for all
u,\widetilde{u}\in E
with
\|u\|_{E},\,\|\widetilde{u}\|_{E}\leq n
and
t\in[0,t^{\prime}].

Theorem B.17

(
[
30
, Chapter 6, Theorem 1.4, p. 185]
)
.

Let
F\colon[0,\infty)\times E\rightarrow E
be continuous in
t
and locally
Lipschitz continuous in
u
, uniformly in
t
on bounded intervals. Then for
every
u_{0}\in E,
there is a time
T_{\text{max}}=T_{\text{max}}(\|u_{0}\|_{E})>0,
possibly
T_{\text{max}}=T,
such that the initial value problem
(
165
) has a unique mild solution
u
on

[0,T_{\text{max}}).
Moreover, if
T_{\text{max}}<T,
then

\lim_{t\nearrow T_{\text{max}}}\|u(t)\|_{E}=\infty.

Remarks B.18
.

(i)

The mild solution on
[0,T_{\text{max}})
is often called maximal mild
solution.

(ii)

If for each
t^{\prime}>0,
there exists a constant
C(t^{\prime})>0
such that

\|F(t,u)\|_{E}\leq C(1+\|u\|_{E})

holds for all
t\in[0,t^{\prime}]
and all
u\in E
, then
(
165
) has a mild solution on
[0,\infty),
i.e. one can
choose
T=\infty
and it holds
T_{\text{max}}=T.

There are multiple criteria which ensure that a mild solution is a classical
solution (see
[
30
, Chapter 6, p. 187ff.]
) and they
typically involve stronger regularity of the right-hand side. We endow the
domain
D(-A)
with the graph norm
\|u\|_{-A}=\|u\|_{E}+\|Au\|_{E},
which
turns
(D(-A),\|\cdot\|_{-A})
to a Banach space due to the closedness of

-A,
see
Theorem

B.6
. Then, for example,
Theorem

B.14

and
Theorem

B.17
imply the next statement.

Theorem B.19
.

Let
F\in C([0,\infty)\times E,\,D(-A))
be uniformly (in t) locally
Lipschitz continuous in
u
. Then for every
u_{0}\in D(-A),
the initial value
problem (
165
) possesses a unique maximal strong solution

u\in C([0,T_{\text{max}}),\,D(-A))\cap C^{1}((0,T_{\text{max}}),\,E).

We finish our overview with a result concerning the continuous dependence of
the solution on the initial data.

Corollary B.20

(
[
30
, Chapter 6, Theorem 1.2, p. 184]
)
.

Let
F\in C([0,\infty)\times E,\,E)
be globally Lipschitz continuous in

u.
Then the mild solution depends Lipschitz continuously on
u_{0}\in E,
i.e.
for all
T>0,
the mapping
u_{0}\mapstochar\rightarrow u
is Lipschitz continuous from
E
into

C([0,T],\,E)
.

Appendix C
Perturbation and Approximation of

C_{0}
-Semigroups

We assume throughout this chapter, similar to the previous one, that
(E,\,\|\cdot\|_{E})
is a complex Banach space.

Many times in applications, the verification of the generator theorems
from Appendix
B
is a difficult task and cannot be
performed in a direct way. Therefore, one tries to build up the given operator
and its semigroup from simpler ones. Perturbation and approximation are the
standard methods for this approach
[
8
, Chapter 3, Introduction, p. 157]
.

We present the main results for the perturbation of an infinitesimal generator

(-A,\,D(-A))
of a semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
with a bounded linear operator

B\in\mathcal{L}(E).

The following theorem summarizes multiple results from

[
8
,
30
]
, namely
[
8
, III. Theorem 1.3,
p. 158]
and
[
8
, III. Corollary 1.7, p. 161]
. The uniqueness claim concerning properties
(ii)
and

(iii)
is proven in
[
30
, Chapter 3, Proposition 1.2, p. 77]
and a proof of the (in our case obvious) equivalence of
the graph norms can be found in
[
8
, III. Lemma 2.4, p. 171]
for a more general framework.

Theorem C.1
.

Let
(-A,\,D(-A))
generate the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfying
\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq Me^{\omega t}
for all
t\geq 0
and constants
M\geq 1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R}.
Let
B\in\mathcal{L}(E).
Then the sum
-A+B
with

D(-A+B)=D(-A)
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
which fulfills

(i)

\|R(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq Me^{(\omega+M\|B\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)})t},

(ii)

R(t)u=T(t)u+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)BR(s)u\,ds,

(iii)

R(t)u=T(t)u+\int_{0}^{t}R(t-s)BT(s)u\,ds

for all
t\geq 0
and
u\in E.
The operator family
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the
only strongly continuous family of operators solving
(ii)
and/or
(iii).
The
graph norms of
-A
and
-A+B
on
D(-A)
are equivalent.

Aside from the implicit representation in

Theorem

C.1
, there are other, more
useful formulas for the semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}.

Theorem C.2

(Dyson-Phillips series,
[
8
, III. Theorem 1.10, p. 163]
)
.

Let
(-A,\,D(-A))
generate the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
and let

B\in\mathcal{L}(E).
The semigroup
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
generated by
-A+B
with

D(-A+B)=D(-A)
satisfies

R(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}R_{k}(t)

(166)

with

R_{0}(t)=T(t),\quad R_{k+1}(t)u=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)BR_{k}(t)u\,ds

for all
t\geq 0
and
u\in E.
The Dyson-Phillips series
(
166
) converges in

\mathcal{L}(E)
and uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Theorem C.3

(Lie-Trotter product forumla,
[
8
, III. Corollary
5.8, p. 227]
)
.

Assume that
(-A,\,D(-A))
and
(B,\,D(B))
generate the
C_{0}
-semigroups

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
respectively, subject to the stability
bound

\|(T(\tfrac{t}{n})S(\tfrac{t}{n}))^{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq Me^{\omega t}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N},\,t\geq 0
and some
M\geq 1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R}.
Let

D\coloneqq D(-A)\cap D(B)
and
(\lambda I_{E}-(-A+B))D
be dense in
E
for
some
\lambda>\omega.
Then the sum
-A+B
with domain
D(-A+B)\coloneqq D

has a closure
\overline{-A+B}
which generates the
C_{0}
-semigroup

(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
given by

R(t)u=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(T(\tfrac{t}{n})S(\tfrac{t}{n})\right)^{n}u

for all
t\geq 0
and
u\in E.
For every fixed
u\in E,
the convergence is
uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Appendix D
Spectral Theory and Long-Term Behavior of
C_{0}
-Semigroups

Again,
(E,\,\|\cdot\|_{E})
is assumed to be a complex Banach space.

The purpose of this chapter is to give an overview of the main theory we used
when studying the spectral properties and the long-term behavior of the
transport-reaction semigroups from
Chapter

3
.

It is necessary to recall definitions from spectral theory before presenting
statements on the asymptotics of strongly continuous semigroups as many results
characterize asymptotical properties in terms of spectral properties of the
semigroup.

As a guideline, the goal is often to deduce stability or more complex long-term
behavior of the semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
from spectral properties of its
generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
. To this end, it is necessary to study the connection
of the spectrum of the generator and the spectrum of the semigroup. This
connection should intuitively be given by

``\quad\sigma(T(t))=e^{t\sigma(-A)}\quad",

(167)

but generally, this formula is incorrect. Especially for hyperbolic equations,
proving a connection in the fashion of (
167
) is non-trivial.

In infinite dimensional spaces, a linear operator is no longer injective if and
only if it is surjective. Therefore, one needs a more refined definition for the
spectrum than in finite dimensions, where the spectrum of a matrix is simply
given by the set of its eigenvalues. The next definition and its remark unite

[
8
, IV. Definition 1.1, p. 239]
,
[
8
, IV.
Definition 1.6, p. 241]
,
[
8
, IV. Definition 1.8 and
Lemma 1.9, p. 242]
and
[
8
, IV. Definition 1.11, p. 243]
.

Definition D.1
.

Let
(-A,\,D(-A))
be a closed operator. We define

(i)

the spectrum
\sigma(-A)=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,\lambda I_{E}+A\text{ is not bijective}\right\},

(ii)

the resolvent set
\rho(-A)=\sigma(-A)^{c},

(iii)

the point spectrum
\sigma_{p}(-A)=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,\lambda I_{E}+A\text{ is not injective}\right\},

(iv)

the approximate point spectrum

\displaystyle\sigma_{ap}(-A)

\displaystyle=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,\lambda I_{E}+A\text{ is
not injective}\,\text{ or }\,R(\lambda+A)\text{ is not closed in }E\}

\displaystyle=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,\exists(u^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset D(-A)\colon\|u^{(n)}\|_{E}=1,\,\lambda u^{(n)}+Au^{(n)}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty\},

(v)

the residual spectrum
\sigma_{r}(-A)=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,\colon\,(\lambda I_{E}+A)D(-A)\text{ is not dense in E}\}.

Remarks D.2
.

(i)

For
\lambda\in\rho(-A),
the inverse

R(\lambda,-A)\coloneqq(\lambda+A)^{-1}

is, by the closed graph theorem, a bounded linear operator on
E
and is
called the resolvent (of
-A
at the point
\lambda
).

(ii)

Each
\lambda\in\sigma_{p}(-A)
is called an eigenvalue and each
0\neq u\in D(-A)
satisfying
(\lambda+A)u=0
is an eigenvector of
-A
(corresponding to

\lambda
).

Definition D.3

(
[
8
, IV. Corollary 1.4, p. 241]
)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(E).
The quantity

r(T)=\max\left\{|\lambda|\,\colon\,\lambda\in\sigma(T)\right\}

is called the spectral radius of
T.

Definition D.4

(
[
8
, I. Definition 5.6, p. 40 and
IV. Definition 2.1, p. 250]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\coloneqq(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
E
with
generator
(-A,\,D(-A)).
The quantity

\omega_{0}=\omega_{0}(\mathcal{T})=\inf\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}\,\colon\,\exists M_{\omega}\geq 1\,\forall t\geq 0\colon\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq M_{\omega}e^{\omega t}\right\}

is called its growth bound and

s(-A)=\sup\left\{\real\lambda\,\colon\,\lambda\in\sigma(-A)\right\}

is called the spectral bound of its generator.

Remark D.5
.

Another common notation for the growth bound of a semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
with generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
is
\omega_{0}(-A).

The spectral radius of the semigroup operators, the growth bound of the
semigroup and the spectral bound of the generator are closely connected via the
next proposition.

Proposition D.6

(
[
8
, IV. Proposition 2.2, p. 251]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a semigroup with generator
(-A,\,D(-A)).
One has

-\infty\leq s(-A)\leq\omega_{0}(\mathcal{T})=\inf_{t>0}\frac{1}{t}\log\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}=\frac{1}{t_{0}}\log r(T(t_{0}))<\infty

for all
t_{0}>0.
In particular, the spectral radius of the operator
T(t)
is
given by

r(T(t))=e^{\omega_{0}t}\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

Definition D.7

(equivalent to
[
8
, V. Definition 1.1 (a), p. 296]
)
.

A
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is called uniformly exponentially stable
if there exist constants
M,\,\varepsilon>0
such that

\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq Me^{-\varepsilon t}

holds for all
t\geq 0.

An application of
Proposition

D.6
yields the following
characterizations of exponential stability.

Proposition D.8

(
[
8
, V. Proposition 1.7, p. 299]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup with generator
(-A,\,D(-A)).
Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is uniformly exponentially stable.

(ii)

\exists t_{0}>0
such that
\|T(t_{0})\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}<1.

(iii)

\exists t_{1}>0
such that
r(T(t_{1}))<1.

(iv)

\omega_{0}(\mathcal{T})<0.

If this is the case,
(ii)
is valid for all sufficiently large
t_{0}>0,

assertion
(iii)
is true for all
t_{1}>0
and we have
s(-A)<0.

The above given criteria for uniform exponential stability have a huge
disadvantage: they rely on properties of the semigroup itself. Typically in
applications, only the generator is known. It is therefore desirable to
characterize uniform exponential stability directly in terms of the generator

[
8
, V. Introduction, p. 301]
.

One would hope that the inequality
s(-A)\leq\omega_{0}
from

Proposition

D.6
is actually an equality. This would imply that

s(-A)<0

is equivalent to uniform exponential stability. Unfortunately, this is generally incorrect
and the situation is non-trivial. There are (even positive) semigroups

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
with
s(-A)<0
and
\omega_{0}\geq 0;
see for example

[
8
, IV. Counterexample 2.7, p. 254]
. The equality
can also fail on less artificial spaces and there are counterexamples on
reflexive Banach spaces or even Hilbert spaces, cf.
[
8
, IV.
Counterexamples 3.3 and 3.4, p. 271-275]
.

The so called weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(T(t))\cup\{0\}=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A)}}\cup\{0\}\quad\text{
for all }t\geq 0

(WSMT)

is one possibility to get around the problem.

Lemma D.9

(
[
8
, V. Lemma 1.9, p. 301]
)
.

If for a strongly continuous semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
and its generator

(-A,\,D(-A))
the weak spectral mapping theorem (
WSMT
) holds, then
the growth bound
\omega_{0}
and the spectral bound
s(-A)
coincide, i.e.

s(-A)=\omega_{0}.

In this case, the semigroup is uniformly exponentially stable if and only if

s(-A)<0.

The question remaining is which assumptions on the semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
ensure the existence of a weak spectral mapping theorem. The next result shows that neither the point, nor the residual spectrum,
cause any problems.

Theorem D.10

(
[
8
, IV. Theorem 3.7, p. 277]
)
.

For a generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
of a strongly continuous semigroup

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
, we have the identities

\displaystyle\sigma_{p}(T(t))\backslash\{0\}

\displaystyle=e^{t\sigma_{p}(-A)},

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}(T(t))\backslash\{0\}

\displaystyle=e^{t\sigma_{r}(-A)}

for all
t\geq 0.

Moreover, there is the spectral inclusion theorem.

Theorem D.11

(Spectral Inclusion Theorem,
[
8
, IV. Theorem 3.6, p. 276]
)
.

For the generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
of a strongly continuous semigroup

(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
, we have the inclusions

\displaystyle\sigma(T(t))

\displaystyle\supseteq e^{t\sigma(-A)}

\displaystyle\sigma_{ap}(T(t))

\displaystyle\supseteq e^{t\sigma_{ap}(-A)}

for all
t\geq 0.

Special classes of semigroups fulfill a stronger version of (
WSMT
), the
spectral mapping theorem. A prominent example is the class of analytic
semigroups, which appears in the study of parabolic equations; see

[
8
, II. Definition 4.5, p. 101]
for their
definition.

Corollary D.12

(
[
8
, IV. Corollary 3.12, p. 281]
)
.

The spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(T(t))\backslash\{0\}=e^{t\sigma(-A)}\quad\text{
for all }t\geq 0

(SMT)

holds for the following class of strongly continuous semigroups:

(i)

eventually compact semigroups,

(ii)

eventually differentiable semigroups,

(iii)

analytic semigroups,

(iv)

uniformly continuous semigroups.

Remark D.13
.

For the definitions of eventually compact, eventually differentiable and
uniformly continuous semigroups, we refer to
[
8
, II. Definition
4.23, p. 117]
,
[
8
, II. Definition 4.13, p. 109]
and
[
8
, I. Definition 3.6, p. 16]
respectively.

Unfortunately, the transport-reaction semigroups appearing when studying the
linearized version of the hyperbolic equation

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
u_{N}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v_{N}}\cdot\nabla u_{N}\end{pmatrix}=F(u)

(168)

on the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
population subgroups, transport directions

\mathbf{v_{1}},\,\cdots,\mathbf{v_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a sufficiently smooth
reaction function
F
are less regular than all classes of semigroups from

Corollary

D.12
. Having said this, they can be expanded to a strongly continuous
group
(T(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and for these groups, there is the following result.

Theorem D.14

(
[
8
, IV. Theorem 3.16 and Exercise 3.22, p. 283ff.]
)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be a polynomially bounded stongly continuous group with
generator
(-A,\,D(-A))
. Then the weak spectral mapping theorem

\sigma(T(t))=\overline{e^{t\sigma(-A)}}

for
t\in\mathbb{R}
holds.

Remark D.15
.

Polynomially bounded means that there is a polynomial
P\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with

\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(E)}\leq P(t)\quad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}.

Without any reactions, i.e. the homogeneous case of
(
168
), the generated transport group is bounded and
therefore,
Theorem

D.14
applies. This is, to some extent,
the motivation for a weak spectral mapping theorem for the linearized version of
(
168
). It should be noted that the transport-reaction
groups generated in this case are almost never polynomially bounded. Depending
on the reactions, some components of the solution grow exponentially, either for

t\rightarrow\infty
or for
t\rightarrow-\infty.
